# **Jacques Derrida, *Introduction* to Husserl's *L'Origine de la géométrie***

**Text:** Jacques Derrida, "Introduction," in Edmund Husserl, *L'Origine de la géométrie*, traduction et introduction par Jacques Derrida (Paris: PUF, coll. « Épiméthée »), pp. 3–171 of the present edition. The Introduction is dated "Juillet 1961." 

## **Introduction**

Derrida opens by situating the text he is introducing: by date and by theme, the *Origin of Geometry* belongs to the final group of Husserl's writings gathered around the *Krisis* (*Die Krisis der europäischen Wissenschaften und die transzendentale Phänomenologie*, Husserliana VI, ed. Biemel, 1954 — which Derrida will cite throughout as "K," as his footnote indicates). The opening claim is deliberately deflationary: because the text is so deeply rooted in the *Krisis* complex, its originality "risks not being immediately apparent." Derrida then specifies what is *not* new in it, and this negative inventory is itself a compressed map of late Husserl. (a) The question is still the *status of the ideal objects of science* — geometry being only an example (*un exemple*; the exemplary status of geometry will become a major theme of the whole Introduction). (b) These objects are still understood as *produced* by acts of identification of the "same" (*le « même »*) — i.e., idealities are constituted through iterable acts that intend an identical pole across multiplicities of experience. (c) Exactness is still constituted by *idealization* and *passage to the limit* (*passage à la limite*) operating on sensible, finite, prescientific materials of the *monde de la vie* — Derrida's French for the *Lebenswelt* ("lifeworld"), the term he will shortly use in German as well. (d) The concrete, mutually solidary conditions of possibility of ideal objects remain the same triad: language, intersubjectivity, and the world as the unity of a *sol* ("ground" or "soil") and a *horizon* — a pairing (ground/horizon) that will organize the entire Introduction, especially its closing sections, where the Earth as *Boden* and the *Idea in the Kantian sense* as horizon become the two poles of historicity. (e) Finally, the techniques of phenomenological description, notably the various reductions, are still fully in force; Husserl's confidence in their validity and fecundity is, Derrida says, less impaired than ever (*moins que jamais... entamées*). The philosophical move of the paragraph is thus to deny that the text's importance lies at the level of doctrinal content — a denial that prepares the positive thesis (¶3 below) that its novelty lies in the *organization* and *radicalization* of familiar motifs. Note also the quiet methodological signal: Derrida reads Husserl's corpus as a single continuous meditation in which "almost all the motifs" of any one text are already present elsewhere — a hermeneutic of continuity-with-displacement that governs his procedure throughout.

A second deflation: at first sight (*au premier abord*) the *Origin* is not distinguished either by the "double beam of critiques" (*double faisceau de critiques*) one finds directed in it against two adversaries. The first beam targets a certain technicist and objectivist *irresponsibility* in the practice of science and philosophy; the second targets a historicism blinded by the empiricist cult of *fact* (Derrida italicizes *fait*) and by causalist presumption. Derrida then assigns each critique its textual genealogy in the corpus: the anti-objectivist critique stands at the point of departure of *Formal and Transcendental Logic*, the *Cartesian Meditations*, and the *Krisis*; the anti-historicist critique appeared much earlier, in the *Logical Investigations*, in *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* (where it is the "essential preoccupation"), and in *Ideas I*. The paragraph's key analytic claim is the *solidarity* of two reductions: the reduction — if not the outright condemnation — of historicist geneticism (*génétisme historiciste*) has always gone hand in hand with the reduction of psychogeneticism. Husserl's early battle against psychologism and his battle against historicism are structurally one battle: both would dissolve ideal validity into factual genesis. Derrida closes with a remark of great consequence for everything that follows: even when a certain historicity later *becomes a theme* for phenomenology — at the cost of many difficulties — there will never be any question of revoking this trial (*procès*) of historicism and psychologism. In other words: Husserl's late turn to history is not a relapse into what the *Prolegomena* refuted; the new historicity must be thought entirely on this side of the anti-historicist reduction. The whole problem of the Introduction is how a radical historicity can be affirmed *without* historicism — this paragraph fixes the constraint.

Now the positive determination of the text's singularity. Never before, Derrida says, had the two denunciations — of historicism and of objectivism — been so *organically united* as in the *Origin of Geometry*, where they proceed from a single impulse (*un même élan*) and mutually drive one another along an itinerary whose gait is at times disconcerting. The footnote attached here bears substantially on the reading and should be incorporated: Derrida observes that these pages, first written by Husserl for himself, have the rhythm of a thought *seeking itself* rather than expounding itself (*qui se cherche plutôt qu'elle ne s'expose*); but he adds that the apparent discontinuity also belongs to a method that is *always regressive*, which chooses its interruptions and multiplies its returns toward its own beginning in order to grasp it each time in a recurrent light. This is no mere apology for a rough draft: Derrida is claiming that the zigzag form of the *Origin* is internally motivated by the structure of the *Rückfrage* (the backward question, "questioning back"), the regressive inquiry that can only approach its origin by repeated returns — form mirrors method. The body of the paragraph then states the thesis: the singularity of the text lies in the fact that the conjunction of two classical, well-tried refusals gives rise to an *unprecedented design* (*un dessein inédit*): on the one hand, to bring to light a new type or a new *depth* of historicity; on the other, correlatively, to determine the new instruments and the original direction of *historical reflection* — the noematic and noetic faces, as it were, of a new science of history (this is the *Geschichtlichkeit/Historie* pair formalized in ¶4). The historicity in question is the historicity of *ideal objectities* (*objectités idéales* — Derrida's rendering of *Gegenständlichkeiten*, which he will justify later via Suzanne Bachelard's usage), i.e., their origin and their *tradition*. Derrida flags the constitutive ambiguity of the word *tradition*, which envelops at once the *movement of transmission* and the *perdurance of the heritage* — process and sediment, *traditio* as act and as content. The historicity of ideal objects obeys "unusual rules" (*règles insolites*): neither those of the factual concatenations of empirical history nor those of an ideal and ahistorical enrichment. This double negation — neither empirical-factual history nor timeless ideality — defines the narrow ridge the whole Introduction walks. Hence the conclusion: the birth and becoming of science must be accessible to a historical intuition of an unheard-of style (*d'un style inouï*), in which the *intentional reactivation of sense* must — *en droit*, by right, in principle — precede and condition the empirical determination of fact. The de jure/de facto distinction, and the priority of sense-reactivation over fact-establishment, is the methodological core of Husserl's text as Derrida reads it.

This paragraph draws the transcendental consequence. The historicity of science and the reflection it calls for — and here Derrida introduces the German pair *Geschichtlichkeit* and *Historie* (historicity as the mode of being of what is historical, versus historiographical inquiry or the discipline of history) — have, in their irreducible originality, *common a priori conditions*. A footnote attached to the pair bears on translation practice and should be noted: in his translation of Husserl's text Derrida will only mark the distinction between *Historie* and *Geschichte* in parentheses when it answers to an explicit intention of Husserl's — which, he dryly notes, is far from always the case. (The remark is a caution against over-reading the terminological opposition, made canonical by Heidegger, back into Husserl's usage.) In Husserl's eyes the unveiling of these common a priori conditions is *principiellement* — in principle, *principiellement* rendering *prinzipiell* — possible, and should lead us to reconsider in their broadest extension the problems of *universal* historicity. The argumentative move is an exemplary instance of phenomenological generalization: if the history of a science has a priori conditions, then "something like a history of science" being possible *imposes* a rereading and a reawakening of the *sense* (*sens* — Derrida sets *« sens »* in quotation marks, marking it as Husserl's *Sinn*) of history in general. And the paragraph ends with the thesis that will govern the final sections of the Introduction: the phenomenological sense of history will, in the last instance, *coincide with its teleological sense*. Sense (*Sinn*) and *telos* converge: history is ultimately intelligible only as the teleology of reason. Derrida states this here without argument, as the horizon toward which Husserl's text moves; sections X–XI will measure its difficulties.

The final paragraph of the untitled opening defines the status of Husserl's text and of Derrida's own enterprise. Husserl, Derrida says, attempts here the *singular test* (*l'épreuve singulière*) of these possibilities of principle — in connection with geometry — and attempts to decipher in it the prescription of a *general task*. Like most Husserlian texts, the *Origin of Geometry* has a value at once *programmatic* and *exemplary*: it both announces an infinite task and serves as an *eidetic example* of how that task is to be carried out. The reading must therefore be marked by the "consciousness of example" proper to all eidetic attention — i.e., the reader must treat geometry the way the eidetic phenomenologist treats any example: as a contingent point of departure for grasping an essential structure — and must regulate itself on the pole of that infinite task from which alone a phenomenology can open its way (the Idea in the Kantian sense already discreetly at work in the description of reading itself). Derrida then states his own ambition with studied modesty: in the introduction "we are now going to attempt," the sole ambition will be to *recognize and situate* in this text *one stage* (*une étape*) of Husserl's thought, with its own presuppositions and its own incompleteness (*inachèvement*). There follows a sentence of real consequence: this moment of Husserlian radicalism is *ultimate in fact* (*ultime en fait*) — it is, chronologically, the last state of the doctrine — but it is perhaps not ultimate *in principle* (*en droit*); and Husserl himself seems to concede as much on several occasions. The de facto/de jure distinction of ¶3 is now applied reflexively to Husserl's own corpus: the *Origin* is the factual terminus, not necessarily the rightful completion, of the phenomenological radicalization — an opening through which Derrida's own questions (about the limits of phenomenology) will pass. Hence the closing methodological vow, characteristic of the whole Introduction's double gesture of fidelity and pressure: it is from Husserl's *intention itself* that Derrida will always try to take inspiration, even when — *lors même que* — he attaches himself to certain *difficulties*. The word "difficulties," twice used in these opening pages (¶2, ¶5), is Derrida's discreet name for the points where, on his reading, the Husserlian project encounters limits it cannot think with its own resources.

## **I**

Section I opens with a thesis about the privileged position of the mathematical object in Husserl's itinerary: it seems to be the privileged example and the most permanent guiding thread (*fil conducteur* — rendering Husserl's *Leitfaden*, the "transcendental guide" of constitutional analysis) of Husserlian reflection. The reason given is rigorous: the mathematical object is *ideal*. Its being is exhausted in, and transparent through and through to, its phenomenality (*son être s'épuise et transparaît de part en part dans sa phénoménalité*). The formulation deserves weight: for an ideal object there is no hidden in-itself, no transcendence of adumbrations as with the perceptual thing; what it is and how it appears coincide. Hence the paradoxical double character Derrida underlines: the mathematical object is *absolutely objective* — totally delivered from empirical subjectivity — and yet it is *only what it appears to be*. It is therefore "always already *reduced*" (*toujours déjà réduit* — the phrase "always already," which will have its own destiny in Derrida's later work, appears here in a precise technical sense) to its phenomenal sense: its being is from the outset *being-an-object for a pure consciousness*. The mathematical object, in other words, performs the phenomenological reduction on itself; it is the entity for which the reduction is, so to speak, analytically accomplished, and this is why it is phenomenology's exemplary object. The attached footnote bears on the scope of this exemplarity and should be incorporated: Derrida refers to the discussion among Walter Biemel, Eugen Fink, and Roman Ingarden — following Biemel's Royaumont lecture on "the decisive phases in the development of Husserl's philosophy" (Cahiers de Royaumont, 1959\) — on the question whether the mathematical object is, for Husserl, the *model of the constitution of every object*, and on the consequences of such a hypothesis. The note quietly registers a live controversy: if all constitution is modeled on mathematical ideality, phenomenology risks an intellectualist prejudice (Ingarden's and Fink's worry); Derrida leaves the question suspended but lets it shadow the exemplarity claim he has just made.

A single long paragraph (running across three pages of the original) establishes the continuity-thesis announced in ¶1 by tracing the project of the *Origin of Geometry* back to Husserl's first major work. The *Philosophie der Arithmetik* (1891), Derrida says, *could have been entitled* "The Origin of Arithmetic." Already there — despite a psychologistic inflection whose *originality* (not merely its error) commentators such as Biemel have rightly stressed — the task was to *reactivate the originary sense* of the ideal unities of arithmetic by a return to the structures of perception and to the acts of a concrete subjectivity. Husserl already proposed to account *at once* (*à la fois*, italicized) for the *normative ideality* of number — which is never an empirical fact accessible to a history of the same style — *and* for its *foundation in and through the lived act of a production*. The two desiderata that the mature theory of historicity must reconcile — ideal normativity and productive genesis — are thus present from 1891\. Derrida's third footnote here substantially bears on the argument and must be incorporated: he quotes the remarkable passage from *Über den Begriff der Zahl* (1887, taken up as the first chapter of the *Philosophie der Arithmetik*) in which Husserl writes that numbers are "creations of the mind" insofar as they are results of activities exercised upon concrete contents, but that what these activities create are not new absolute contents locatable in space or in the "external world" — they are *concepts of relation*, which can only ever be *produced*, and in no way *found* somewhere ready-made. Derrida glosses: this passage already designates the *production* — hence the *originary historicity* — of idealities that will never belong to the time and space of empirical history. The claim is strong: the germ of the *Origin of Geometry* — ideality as produced yet non-worldly — is legible in Husserl's very first book, before the refutation of psychologism. The paragraph then measures the immense path between 1891 and 1936 while insisting on the fidelity of the project. The genesis of arithmetic in 1887–1891 was *not* thought as a *history* of arithmetic — not as a form of culture and an adventure of humanity; it was described in terms of *psychological genesis*. Fifty years of meditation later, the *Origin of Geometry* "repeats the same project" in the guise of a *phenomenological history*. Derrida then enumerates the stages of the path, and the enumeration amounts to a precise periodization of the problem of genesis in Husserl (the subject of Derrida's own 1953–54 thesis, *Le problème de la genèse dans la philosophie de Husserl*, whose results are compressed here). First: the reduction of *all* historical or psychological genesis (the *Logical Investigations* and *Ideas I* period). Then: even when the *genetic* dimension of phenomenology is discovered, genesis is not yet history. In the passage — announced in *Ideas I*, accomplished between 1915 and 1920 — from *static* to *genetic* constitution, Husserl had still not engaged phenomenological description with the problems of *historicity*: the thematization of *transcendental genesis* maintained the reduction of history, and everything classifiable under "objective spirit" and the world of culture remained repressed (*refoulé* — a psychoanalytically inflected verb worth noticing) within the sphere of *intra-mondanéité* ("intra-worldliness," i.e., treated as merely constituted worldly content, not as a transcendental theme). The return to *antepredicative* experience in *Erfahrung und Urteil* and in *Formal and Transcendental Logic* descended to a *pre-cultural and pre-historical* stratum of lived experience (*vécu*, Derrida's standard rendering of *Erlebnis*). And when Husserl speaks of the "unity of a history" in the *Cartesian Meditations* (§§37–38, in Levinas's translation, which Derrida cites as "M.C."), what is at issue is the unity of *traces*, of "referrals" (*renvois*), of "residues" of syntheses *within the pure egological sphere* — i.e., the "history" of the monad, not intersubjective history. Husserl himself underlines (CM §37) that *ideal objects*, the "higher" products of reason — which alone make possible historicity, that is, always, the consciousness of *intersubjective* history — do *not* belong to the *eidos* of the concrete ego. And at the end of the Third Meditation, the investigations concerning the "theory" of "man, human community, culture, etc." are defined as ulterior, *regional and dependent* tasks (§29). All these reductions hold *a fortiori*, Derrida concludes, for the descriptions of primordial temporality and immanent duration: the time-consciousness analyses are even further from history than the egological genesis is. The paragraph as a whole performs a double demonstration: (i) the project of an origin-question about ideality is Husserl's oldest project; (ii) at every stage prior to the *Krisis* period, the apparatus that would later make a phenomenology of history possible was deliberately developed *under reduction of history* — so that the late irruption of history (¶8–9) is prepared but in no way anticipated in its specific difficulty.

A short pivot paragraph. Thus (*Ainsi*) the neutralization of psychological genesis and that of history still go hand in hand even in the texts that place *transcendental becoming* at their focus — the genetic phenomenology of the 1920s repeats, at its own level, the founding double reduction identified in *But* when, at the time of the *Krisis*, it is *history itself* that erupts (*fait irruption*) into phenomenology, a *new space of interrogation* is opened — one that it will be difficult to keep within the regional limits long prescribed for it. The verb *faire irruption* is doing real work: history is not introduced by methodical extension; it breaks in, and the regional cordon (history as a dependent, founded discipline; the cultural world as intra-worldly) drawn around it since *Ideas I* cannot contain the question it brings. The paragraph thereby poses the structural problem of the *Krisis* period: an event within the development of phenomenology (the irruption of history) threatens the architectonic that was supposed to assign every event its place.

The new access to history, constantly *practiced* in the *Krisis* itself, is never *problematized* there — at least never directly and as such. Derrida supports this with a two-sided analysis. On the one hand, the consciousness of crisis and the affirmation of a teleology of reason are *only the path or the means* of a new legitimation of transcendental idealism: the historical material of the *Krisis* is instrumental, in the service of a foundational project that is not itself historical. On the other hand, everything the *Krisis* actually does with history — the placing-in-perspective of the whole becoming of Western philosophy, the definition of the European *eidos* and of man as the being with infinite tasks, the narrative of the adventures and misadventures of the *transcendental motif*, each time dissimulated by the very gesture that discovers it (Descartes being the canonical case: the discovery of the cogito immediately covered over by its objectivist interpretation) — all this extended to *synoptic retrospection* a *credit* that no critique of historical reason had explicitly and previously justified. The Kantian-Diltheyan phrase "critique of historical reason" is pointed: Husserl writes a philosophy of history without having first secured its conditions of possibility. Neither the *structures of historicity in general* — of which, Derrida notes in a parenthesis that will matter later, one does not yet know whether the historicity of science and philosophy is an *example* or an *exception*, the highest and most revealing possibility or a simple surpassing (*dépassement*) of historicity — nor the *methods* of a phenomenology of history had been made the object of original questions. The paragraph ends by explaining why this lacuna did not, in Husserl's own eyes, amount to dogmatism: the confidence was carried by the system of apodictic certainties of phenomenology itself, which can be considered a *critique of reason in general*; and if Husserl's teleological reading of history escapes the dogmatic imprudence with which so many philosophers — "from Aristotle to Hegel, and then to Brunschvicg" — perceived in the past only the laborious presentiment of their own thought, it is because that reading refers to the *Idea* of transcendental phenomenology, *which is not a philosophical system*. The distinction is crucial and characteristically Husserlian: a teleology oriented by an infinite Idea (phenomenology as infinite task) is not retrospectively self-congratulatory the way a teleology oriented by an achieved system (Hegel) must be — because the *telos* is not in anyone's possession. The footnote here refers the problem of history in Husserl to Ricœur's 1949 article "Husserl et le sens de l'histoire," and specifically to Ricœur's analysis of what *obstructs* a direct thematization of history in a transcendental phenomenology that simultaneously *calls for* it — Derrida thereby signaling both his debt to and his point of departure from Ricœur: the obstruction itself is what this Introduction will try to think.

But that reference of the teleological reading to the Idea of phenomenology was only *mediate* (*médiatement*). It still had to be shown — specifically, concretely, and directly — three things, which Derrida is about to enumerate. This short paragraph is the colon before the list: its function is to convert the diagnosis of ¶9 (history practiced, never problematized) into a program of legitimation in three theses. The implication is that the *Origin of Geometry* is precisely the place where the mediate justification becomes — or attempts to become — immediate and specific.

First enumerated requirement: it had to be shown that *history, as an empirical science*, was — like all empirical sciences — *dependent* on phenomenology, which alone could reveal to it its fund of *eidetic presuppositions*. And Derrida adds: this dependence, frequently *asserted*, had always been treated *par prétérition* — by preterition, i.e., mentioned in passing precisely so as not to be examined — signaled rather than explored. The footnote bears on the contrast and should be incorporated: this was *not* the case with psychology, whose relations to phenomenology were abundantly defined — notably in *Ideas II*, the *Cartesian Meditations*, and the third part of the *Krisis* — and Derrida points to Biemel's then-recent edition of the 1925 lectures and annexes on *Phänomenologische Psychologie* (Husserliana IX) as rich testimony. The contrast is telling: phenomenology developed an elaborate doctrine of its relation to the *other* genetic-empirical science of subjectivity (psychology) while leaving its relation to history — the genetic-empirical science of *spirit* — programmatic. An eidetics of history (a "material ontology" of the historical world) is the first desideratum.

Second enumerated requirement, and the most delicate: it had to be shown that history — whose *proper content*, unlike that of the other material and dependent sciences, is by virtue of its *sense of being* (*sens d'être*, rendering *Seinssinn*) always marked by *unicity* and *irreversibility*, that is, by *non-exemplarity* — nevertheless still lends itself to *imaginary variations* and *eidetic intuitions*. The difficulty is stated with maximal compression: eidetic method lives on exemplarity — any individual case is an interchangeable example of the essence, and the free variation in imagination presupposes that the example can be varied without loss. But the historical fact *qua* historical is unique, irreversible, non-substitutable: precisely *not* an example. An eidetics of history must therefore practice variation on a material whose sense of being resists variation. Derrida does not resolve the difficulty here; he registers it as the second condition the *Origin* must meet. (It will return at the center of the Introduction, in the discussion of the exemplarity of the geometrical origin and the "historical a priori.") The single-sentence enumerative unit thus carries one of the deepest tensions of the whole project: between *Eidos* and *Einmaligkeit*, essence and the once-only.

Third enumerated requirement (the unit runs over the page break and includes its concluding development): beyond the empirical and non-exemplary *content* of history, it had to be shown that the content of certain *eidetics themselves* — that of geometry, for example, as the eidetics of spatial nature — had itself been *produced or revealed in a history* that *irreducibly inhabits its sense of being*. This is the reflexive turn that distinguishes the third thesis from the first two: not only is empirical history subject to an eidetics (thesis 1), and not only can its unique content be varied (thesis 2), but the eidetic sciences — the very disciplines that seemed to stand outside all history as systems of timeless truths — have a constitutive historicity: history *inhabits* (*habite*) their *Seinssinn* irreducibly. Whence the consequence Derrida draws in the sentences completing the unit: if the history of the geometrical eidetics is *exemplary*, as Husserl asserts, then history in general risks no longer being merely *a dependent and distinct sector* of a more radical phenomenology. While remaining within a determinate relativity, history engages phenomenology *through and through* (*de part en part*), with all its possibilities and responsibilities, its original techniques and attitudes. The architectonic subordination of history (a regional, founded discipline) is destabilized by the discovery that the founding disciplines themselves have histories essential to their sense: the region "history" overflows its regional assignment. The conditional "risks" (*risque de*) is Derrida's precision: the *Origin* puts the architectonic at risk rather than calmly revising it — an instability he will exploit in ¶15 ("complicated, if not overturned").

These three ambitions, which are also three *difficulties*, no doubt animate the *Krisis* itself and, more virtually, the earlier works. But it is in the *Origin of Geometry* and in short fragments of the same period that they are, it seems, *most immediately assumed* (*assumées*). A brief stocktaking paragraph: it converts the three requirements into the specific warrant for treating this short, posthumously edited text as the privileged site of the late Husserl's most radical program — more radical, on these three points, than the *Krisis* whose "annex" it nominally is. The hedges (*sans doute*, *semble-t-il*, *plus virtuellement*) keep the claim philological as well as philosophical: it is a judgment about where in the corpus the problematic surfaces *as such*.

A warning (*Il faut y prendre garde*): these ambitions are, in the *Origin*, *only served by already familiar themes*, which they orient in a new sense — a restatement, at the level of this text, of the opening claim (¶1) that nothing is new but the orientation. The temptation would even be strong, Derrida concedes, to see in the *Origin of Geometry* — given the brevity of the sketch — nothing more than a continuation of the *Krisis*, indeed merely *the preface to a re-edition of Formal and Transcendental Logic*, its design simply adapted to a *material ontology* (geometry) instead of the formal one (logic). The rest of the long paragraph substantiates the parallel with FTL's Introduction, and in doing so gives a compact account of Husserl's concept of crisis. In FTL's introduction Husserl sees in "the present situation of the European sciences" the motive for "radical *prises de conscience*" (*Besinnungen* — the French *prise de conscience*, "becoming-aware," will be Derrida's standing rendition of *Besinnung*, and the term should be tracked: it means a reflective self-recollection that takes responsibility for sense, not mere awareness). For Husserl, the critical significance of this situation lies less in any epistemological conflict internal to the sciences' development than in a *divorce*: between, on one side, the theoretical and practical activity of a science in the full brilliance of its progress and successes, and, on the other, its *sense for life* and the possibility of being related to the totality of *our world*. Derrida then formulates the constitutive ambivalence that the whole *Krisis*\-complex turns on: the *liberation* of science with respect to its foundations (*assises*) in the *Lebenswelt* and with respect to the founding subjective acts is *a necessary condition of its conquests* — and at the same time carries the threat of an *objectivist alienation*, which conceals the founding origins from us, renders them foreign and inaccessible. Forgetting is not an accident that befalls science; it is the price of its progress. The closing movement (completing the unit across the page break) draws the consequences in Husserl's own vocabulary, which Derrida half-quotes from FTL: this occultation is also a *technicization*; it presupposes the "naïveté of a higher level" of the scientist become irresponsible; it has ruined the "great faith" of the sciences and of philosophy in themselves, and rendered our world "incomprehensible." To become conscious (*besinnen*) of the origins is at the same time to make oneself *responsible* (*verantworten*) for the *sense* (*Sinn*) of science and philosophy — Derrida inserts the German triad, displaying the etymological circuit *Sinn/besinnen* and the internal link of sense to responsibility that he will make one of the master-themes of the Introduction; it is to bring sense to the clarity of its "fulfillment" (*remplissement*, the French for *Erfüllung*, with its precise technical charge from the Sixth Investigation: intuitive fulfillment of an empty intention); it is to put oneself in a position to *answer for* sense on the basis of the sense of the totality of our existence. The footnote chain here is largely citational (FTL pages), but the fifth note quotes FTL's demand that we dominate the totality of life and of cultural traditions through radical *prises de conscience*, seeking the ultimate possibilities and necessities from which one can take position toward reality in judging, valuing, acting — confirming that the crisis-motif is from the start practical and existential, not merely epistemological.

The same disquiet and the same will, Derrida continues, are expressed with an identical accent and in rigorously identical terms from the first pages of the *Origin of Geometry*. And the question posed there seems indeed to be, at first sight, only a *specification* of the general question inaugurated and defined in FTL. Is it not a matter of applying to a singular and dependent science a general project whose program had already been organized? Had Husserl not written — and here the paragraph completes itself in the block quotation from FTL: these *prises de conscience* concerning the possible sense and possible method of authentic science in general bear first on what is essentially common to all possible sciences; in second place, corresponding *Besinnungen* would have to be pursued for the particular groups of sciences and for the sciences taken one by one. The quotation seems to settle the matter against the *Origin*'s originality: geometry would simply be one of the "sciences taken one by one," and the *Origin* an application, a regional execution of FTL's program. The paragraph is thus the strongest statement of the deflationary reading — set up precisely so that ¶¶17–19 can dismantle it.

Derrida now gives the deflationary reading its systematic ground, in order to measure exactly what the *Origin* will overturn. The anteriority of FTL with respect to the origin-problems of the other sciences has a *systematic and juridical* signification — not merely chronological. Its necessity derives first from the nature of traditional logic, which has always presented itself as the general doctrine of science, the *science of science*; this articulation refers in turn to the *hierarchy of ontologies* already elaborated in *Ideas I*: to *formal ontology*, treating the pure rules of objectivity in general, are subordinated the determinate *material ontologies*. Geometry, on this architectonic, is a material ontology whose object is determined as the *spatiality of the natural thing*. The footnotes bear substantially on the doctrine and should be incorporated. The first specifies that "formal ontology" here designates formal logic "in the narrow sense" plus all the disciplines of the formal *mathesis universalis* (arithmetic, pure analysis, theory of multiplicities), citing *Ideas I* in Ricœur's translation. The third quotes *Ideas I* §9: the essence of the material thing implies that it is a *res extensa*, and geometry is the ontological discipline relating to an *eidetic moment* of thinghood (*Dinglichkeit*), namely *spatial form* — with Derrida noting that Husserl himself italicizes; and it adds the reference to §25 where geometry and *kinematics* (which Husserl always associates with geometry in the *Krisis* and the *Origin*) are defined as "purely mathematical... material disciplines." The architectonic picture is thus complete: logic/formal ontology on top, geometry as a regional-material eidetics below, and therefore FTL by right *prior* to any "origin of geometry."

Consequently (*On s'expliquera ainsi que*) a whole dimension of the *Origin of Geometry* openly displays this dependence and this *relative superficiality* of its description — "superficiality" in the architectonic sense: it operates at a founded, regional level. Husserl notes several times in the *Origin* that he *presupposes* the constitution of the ideal objectities of logic and of language in general, the correlative constitution of intersubjectivity, etc., and all the investigations relating to them. In a certain sense, Derrida adds, one must take care *not to invert* this order of dependence: the phenomenon of "crisis," as forgetting of origins, has precisely the sense of an "*inversion*" (*Umkehrung*) of this type. The point is elegant: the architectonic order (transcendental logic before regional sciences; founding acts before founded idealities) is not pedantry — its inversion *is* the crisis. The footnote attached to *objectités* bears on translation and should be retained: Derrida justifies rendering *Gegenständlichkeit* by *objectité* (following Suzanne Bachelard's note in her FTL translation), explicitly disclaiming any complicity with Schopenhauer's *Objektität*; and he states his general policy of conforming to the established usage of the major French translations (Ricœur's *Idées I*, Bachelard's FTL, Élie's *Recherches logiques*), undertaking to justify his own innovations as they arise. The footnote attached to *Umkehrung* is philosophically substantial and must be incorporated at length: Derrida first quotes FTL — the originary relation between logic and science *inverted itself* remarkably in modernity; the sciences became independent, elaborating highly differentiated methods whose fecundity was practically certain but whose *performance* (*Leistung* — Derrida gives the German; "action" in Bachelard's French; "accomplishment/production," a cardinal Husserlian term) was no longer understood with evidence. He then observes that it is *above all in connection with geometry and mathematics* that Husserl defined this *Umkehrung* as falsification of sense, displacement of foundation, and forgetting of origins — and he distinguishes *three forms* this took across the corpus: (1) geometry, as the model of exact science, is responsible for the *naturalization of the psychical* denounced in the first part of *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* — to which Derrida adds the absurdity, denounced in *Ideas I* §§72–75, of a *geometrization of lived experience*, impossible by reason of both geometry's exactness and its deductivity (the *vécu* admits only morphological, inexact essences and no deductive eidetics); (2) it is the geometrical ideal (or that of mathematical physics) which, dogmatically received, pushed *Descartes* to cover over the transcendental motif he had brilliantly uncovered — the certainty of the cogito becomes an *axiomatic foundation* and philosophy a deductive system *ordine geometrico*, whereas (Husserl, CM §3) the foundation here is deeper than in geometry and is called on to constitute the ultimate foundation of geometrical science itself; (3) the whole *Krisis* tends to show how geometry, as foundation of the *mathematization of nature*, *hides the true nature*. And Derrida appends a remarkable philological observation: this is perhaps one of the reasons why, without ever explicitly revoking the definition of geometry as eidetic science or material ontology of the spatiality of the natural thing — a definition offered as standard up through *Ideas I* — Husserl *scarcely uses it thereafter*. The note thus quietly documents a drift in Husserl's own corpus: geometry migrates from being the paradigm of eidetics to being the paradigm of sense-forgetting — exactly the ambivalence (condition of progress/threat of alienation, ¶15) that makes it the right example for the question of origin.

But it is *also* in FTL — the very text that grounds logic's priority — that Husserl specifies that the logical path is only *one path among others* toward radical *Besinnung*. The unit completes itself in the block quotation: there are still other possible paths for *prises de conscience* directed toward radicalism, and the present work attempts to open one of them, suggested precisely by the fact that the idea of science has in the past been referred to logic as its norm. The citation is strategically chosen: FTL itself de-absolutizes its own point of departure. The juridical priority of logic (¶17) is the priority of *one* historical-factual route — motivated by the tradition's own self-understanding — not an architectonic necessity that would forbid beginning elsewhere, for instance with geometry. The dismantling of the deflationary reading is thereby authorized by Husserl's own text.

Now the positive characterization, in one of the Introduction's signature sentences: by a *spiraling movement* (*un mouvement en vrille* — a corkscrew or tendril movement; the figure matters, against both the line and the circle) which constitutes the whole value of our text, an *audacious breakthrough* (*une audacieuse percée*) is effected *within* the regional limits of the inquiry and *transgresses* them toward a new form of radicality. In connection with the *intentional history* of one particular eidetic science, a *prise de conscience* of its conditions of possibility will reveal to us *exemplarily* the conditions and the sense of the historicity of science in general, and then of *universal historicity* as the *ultimate horizon of all sense and all objectivity in general*. The three-step escalation should be marked: (i) regional starting point (geometry's intentional history); (ii) generalization to the historicity of science as such; (iii) radicalization to universal historicity as the final horizon of sense and objectivity *überhaupt* — at which point what began as a founded, regional inquiry touches the most fundamental level of all, the level transcendental phenomenology had reserved for itself. Hence: the architectonic relations just evoked (formal/material, founding/founded) find themselves *complicated, if not overturned* (*compliqués, sinon renversés*). Which would demonstrate, Derrida adds with a dry irony — "if it were still necessary" — how little *linear* the juridical order of implications is, and how difficult the recognition of the *point of departure*. The last clause names what is perhaps the deepest Husserlian aporia for Derrida (it organized his thesis on genesis and will organize this Introduction's analyses of the *Rückfrage*): in a philosophy of radical beginnings, the beginning is precisely what can never be simply *had*; the spiral, not the straight line of foundation, is the true figure of phenomenological method.

A single-sentence paragraph closing the section: it is *within these difficulties* (*à l'intérieur de ces difficultés*) that, with extreme prudence, Husserl tries to make his purpose heard in the *Origin of Geometry*. The sentence does triple duty: it credits Husserl with awareness of the architectonic complication (the "extreme prudence" answers the "disconcerting gait" of ¶3); it announces the function of section II, which will inventory the *methodological precautions* of the text's opening pages; and it reaffirms the hermeneutic stance of ¶5 — the difficulties are not objections brought from outside but the very element *inside which* the text moves and must be read.

## **II**

A two-line opening paragraph announcing the section's task: numerous methodological precautions of diverse types are taken, "in a rather enveloped way" (*de façon assez enveloppée*), in the first pages of Husserl's text. The adjective *enveloppée* matters: the precautions are implicit, folded into the exposition rather than formally announced — so that Derrida's section II will consist precisely in *unfolding* them, numbering what Husserl left unnumbered (1., 2., with sub-divisions A, B, C). The commentator's gesture here is explicitly reconstructive: the order of reasons must be extracted from a text whose "gait" was called disconcerting in First precaution: provided the notion of history is understood in a new sense, the question posed must be heard *in its most historical resonance*. It concerns a *repetition of origin* (*une répétition d'origine* — both genitive senses are active: a repetition that returns to the origin, and the origin's own repetition). This means that the reflection does not operate *on* or *in* geometry itself, as it "is delivered to us ready-made" (*toute prête*; Derrida cites his own translation of the *Origin*, hereafter "O."). The attitude chosen is therefore *not that of the geometer*: the geometer disposes of a system of truths that are already there, which he presupposes or puts to work in his geometrizing activity — or again of possibilities of *new axiomatizations* which, even in their problems and difficulties, already announce themselves as *geometrical* possibilities. (The remark is pointed: even revolutionary work in the foundations of geometry — non-Euclidean geometries, Hilbert-style axiomatics — remains *inside* the sense "geometry"; foundational crisis in mathematics is not the same as the question of origin.) Nor is the required attitude that of the *classical epistemologist*, who would study, in a sort of horizontal and ahistorical cross-section, the systematic structure of geometrical science or of the various geometries. Both attitudes, Derrida says, fall under what Husserl had defined in FTL and recalled in the *Krisis* as the "naïveté of aprioric self-evidence" that "keeps every normal geometrical work in motion" (K §9b). The paragraph closes with a generalization and a transition: not only the understanding and practice of geometry, but even a certain *second-order reflection* on constituted geometry, are always possible and sometimes profound and creative, *without geometry being solicited in its buried sense of origin* (*son sens d'origine inhumé* — the mortuary metaphor of interment will recur; sedimented sense is *buried* sense). "It is always the *Krisis* that is echoed here." Two distinctions thus structure the unit: (i) the vertical question of origin versus every horizontal occupation with geometry, however critical; (ii) creativity *within* a sense versus reactivation *of* that sense.

The echoed *Krisis* passage itself, set as a block quotation (K §9b): in the geometer's attitude the need (for the question of origin) is not felt — one has studied geometry, one "understands" the geometrical concepts and propositions, one familiarly extends *credit* to the operative methods as directions-for-use (*modes d'emploi*) of precisely defined formations. Derrida's footnote to the quotation generalizes its scope: "geometry" here designates *exemplarily* mathematics and even logic in general. The quotation supplies the two terms on which the next paragraph turns: *understanding in quotation marks* (operative comprehension without originary evidence) and *credit* — the fiduciary metaphor by which Husserl describes the scientist's relation to inherited method, a metaphor Derrida will exploit throughout the Introduction (sense circulates like currency whose backing is no longer verified; cf. the gold simile quoted at ¶45).

A two-sentence paragraph drawing the consequence: *no* geometrizing activity *as such*, however critical it may be, can get back on the hither side of this "credit" (*revenir en-deçà de ce « crédit »*). The phrase *en-deçà* ("this side of," "short of") names the direction of the whole enterprise: the question of origin must move back behind the fiduciary horizon within which all geometry, including critical and foundational geometry, operates. The limit is one of principle: it attaches to geometrizing activity *en tant que telle*, by its very sense, not to any contingent lack of rigor. Only a change of attitude — the transcendental conversion — and not an intensification of geometrical critique, can pose the question of origin.

Second precaution, introducing the vertical dimension and a tripartite warning: but if one leaves the actual or virtual data of received geometry and comes to the *vertical dimension of history* (*la dimension verticale de l'histoire* — vertical as opposed to the "horizontal cross-section" of ¶23: depth of sedimented genesis versus breadth of systematic structure), *three confusions still lie in wait for us* (*trois confusions nous guettent encore*). The paragraph is purely architectural — a colon before the subdivisions A, B, C — but its function is important: it concedes that abandoning the geometer's and epistemologist's attitudes does not yet secure the question of origin; the historical turn has its own characteristic temptations, each of which mistakes a factual inquiry for the intentional-historical one.

Confusion A. One is not interested here in the "mode of being that the sense (of geometry) had in the thought of Galileo," or "in that of all the later inheritors of an older geometrical knowledge" (Derrida quoting O., 173). Whatever value such an approach might have, it would fall, at best, under a *psychology* or a *history of knowledge*. And Derrida makes the concession maximally generous in order to make the exclusion maximally principled: *even if* this history and psychology escaped, by their descriptive style, those Husserl always suspected; *even if* they did not reduce the normativity of the ideal object and geometrical truth to the facticity of an empirical *vécu* — they would inform us only about the *factual rootedness* (*lien d'enracinement factice*) of truth in a de facto historical or psychological milieu. This rootedness can no doubt be made accessible to a descriptive phenomenology that would respect all its originality — Derrida is conceding the legitimacy of something like a phenomenological psychology or history of science — but about the *sense of origin* and the *truth* of geometry it would teach us *nothing*. The move is the classical Husserlian quaestio iuris/quaestio facti partition, now applied within the historical field itself: there can be a rigorous, even phenomenological, study of how geometrical sense was *lived* by Galileo and his heirs, and it would still not touch the question of how geometrical sense was *founded*.

The reason: Galileo — whose name here, rather than a proper name, is the *exemplary index of an attitude and a moment* — was *already an heir* of geometry. The first footnote substantiates the de-individualization of the name from K §9l, where Husserl admits having attached all these considerations to Galileo's name, "simplifying things by an idealization," although a more exact historical analysis would have to acknowledge what his thought owes to "precursors" — and declares he will continue, for good reasons, to proceed thus. (The note matters methodologically: Husserl's "Galileo" is itself an eidetic fiction, an idealization within the history of idealization — confirming that the inquiry was never empirically biographical.) The body of the paragraph then distinguishes the *Krisis*'s task from the *Origin*'s: if the *Krisis* reserves a very important place for Galileo and his revolution, which Husserl situates at the origin of the *modern spirit in peril*, here the radicalist exigency demands that the *sedimentations on which the enterprise of an infinite mathematization of nature was founded* be themselves *undone* (*défaites*). It is the *Galilean naïveté* itself — in its very genius (*dans sa génialité même*) — that must be *reduced* in order to free the question toward the origin of geometry. The second footnote bears substantially on the argument and should be incorporated: the Galilean inauguration, which opens a career for objectivism by making an "in itself" of mathematized nature, marks the *birth certificate of a crisis* of the sciences and of philosophy — hence its centrality for the *Krisis*; yet Husserl already insists heavily on the *secondarity* of the Galilean revolution and on the scientific heritage it presupposed, notably "pure geometry, the pure mathematics of spatio-temporal forms in general, pre-given to Galileo as an ancient tradition" (K §9a), a "relatively developed geometry... already extending to astronomical as well as terrestrial application" (K §9b); for Galileo the sense of origin of the geometrical tradition was *already lost*: "Galileo was himself, with respect to pure geometry, an heir. The inherited geometry and the inherited method of 'intuitive' invention, demonstration, construction, were no longer originary geometry; (it) was itself already emptied of its sense in this very 'intuitiveness'" (K §9h, Husserl's emphasis). The last clause of the quotation is the sharpest point: even *intuitive* geometrical evidence — the apparent opposite of formal technique — can be sense-emptied; loss of origin is not equivalent to formalization, and intuitiveness is no guarantee of originarity. The regress thus cannot stop at Galileo nor even at pre-formal, intuitive geometry.

Transitional paragraph: in the *Krisis*, by evoking this *blindness* of Galileo to the traditional space of his own adventure, by designating his "fatal negligence" (*négligence fatale*), Husserl announces in the most precise way the task he will undertake a little later in the *Origin*. The footnote attached to "négligence fatale" is among the most important in the entire Introduction and must be incorporated at length. Derrida first quotes K §9h: it was a fatal omission that Galileo did not pose the question-in-return (*question-en-retour*, Derrida's rendering of *Rückfrage*) toward the *originally sense-bestowing production* which operates, as *idealization*, on the *archi-ground* (*archi-sol*, *Urboden*) of all theoretical and practical life — the ground of the immediately intuitive world (here, specifically, of the empirically intuitive world of bodies) — and furnishes the ideal formations of geometry. Derrida then observes that, like forgetting in general, the "fatality" of this "negligence" or omission (*Versäumnis*) — which is *never interrogated for itself and in itself* by Husserl — is affected, depending on text and context, by one of *three significations*: (a) that of an *empirical necessity* (in the order of individual or social psychology and of factual history), hence an extrinsic necessity, contingent with regard to the sense and teleology of reason; it then has the inconsistent negativity of "non-essence" (*das Unwesen*) and of the merely "apparent" defeat of reason — illuminated by the teleology of Reason, it ceases to be "a dark destiny, an impenetrable fatality" (Derrida cites the Vienna Lecture in Ricœur's translation); (b) that of a *radical ethico-philosophical fault*: a failure of philosophical freedom and responsibility; (c) that of an *eidetic necessity*: the necessity of *sedimentation* prescribed to all constitution and all traditionalization of sense, hence to all history — a prescription in turn sometimes valorized (as condition of historicity and of the progressive advent of reason), sometimes devalorized (as the putting-to-sleep of origins and of gathered sense); it is in truth, Derrida says, *a threatening value* (*une valeur menaçante*). Then comes the speculative coda, one of the first places where Derrida's own thought visibly exceeds commentary: these three significations, apparently irreducible to one another, are thought by Husserl on the basis of *one and the same latent intuition*. In this intuition, *it is history itself that announces itself*. If one succeeded in thinking the unitary foundation from which these three propositions can be received simultaneously and without contradiction, *it is history itself that would think itself* (*c'est l'histoire elle-même qui se penserait*). But then — final twist — the possibility of a *crisis of reason* would disappear, since the crisis's negativity must remain *unthinkable in itself*. The argument in compressed form: Husserl needs forgetting to be (a) contingent (else reason is essentially defeasible), (b) imputable (else *Besinnung* loses its ethical urgency), and (c) essential (else sedimentation, hence tradition, hence history, is impossible); the three requirements cannot be unified without abolishing the very concept of crisis that motivates the inquiry. The unthought unity of essential, contingent, and culpable forgetting is, for Derrida, the place where "history itself" presses on phenomenology — an anticipation of the Introduction's final theses on delay and difference.

The announced *Krisis* passage, as a standalone block quotation (K §9b): this (pure geometry as tradition) was thus already there for Galileo, without his feeling the need — naturally enough, Husserl concedes — to penetrate the manner in which the *idealizing production* had originally arisen (that is, how it had arisen on the *substructure* (*soubassement*) of the pre-geometrical sensible world and its practical techniques), nor the need to immerse himself in the questions of the origin of apodictic mathematical evidence. The quotation deserves its separate weight for two reasons. First, it specifies the *terminus a quo* of the regress: not an earlier geometry but the *pre-geometrical* sensible world and its *practical arts* (surveying, measuring) — the *Lebenswelt* stratum from which idealization arose, the very stratum the *Origin* will reconstruct. Second, it pairs the genetic question (how idealizing production arose) with the validity question (the origin of *apodictic evidence*): the whole originality of intentional history is that these two questions, which the tradition assigns to psychology and epistemology respectively, are one question.

And if, in the *Origin* (O., 173), Husserl speaks of "engaging... in reflections that were certainly quite far (*ganz fern*) from Galileo," it is because, as was said in the *Krisis* — and the sentence completes itself in the block quotation (K §9b): a Galileo was quite far (*ganz fern*) from thinking that for geometry, as a branch of a universal knowledge of beings (as a branch of a philosophy), it could become in any way justified, indeed of fundamental importance, to *problematize geometrical evidence* — the "how" of its origin. How a conversion in the direction of the gaze was to become urgent, and how the "origin" of knowledge was to become the capital problem — this, Husserl says, will soon be of essential interest in the course of the historical considerations that start from Galileo. Derrida's philological care (tracking the repetition of *ganz fern* across the two texts) underwrites his larger claim that the *Origin* executes a program the *Krisis* states; and the quotation's footnote notes that this sentence announces equally the sequel of the *Krisis* — devoted to the transcendental motif in post-Galilean philosophy — and researches like the *Origin* itself: the same sentence forks toward the history of philosophy and toward intentional history of science.

A short paragraph raising, in interrogative form, a difficulty that the whole Introduction will keep reopening. If the Galilean discovery consists above all in a *formalizing infinitization* of the mathematics of Antiquity, then to return to the latter as to an origin — is that not to *tie originarity to a certain finitude*? Derrida answers that no simple answer is possible, and issues a promissory note: we shall see that *the infinite had already erupted, was already at work* (*il était déjà au travail*) when the first geometry began — a first geometry which was *itself already an infinitization*. The structure of the difficulty: the regressive question seems to move from the infinite (modern formalized mathematics) back to the finite (Greek geometry, the prescientific world); but if origin meant finitude, the *sense* of geometry — omnitemporal, open to infinite enrichment — could never have arisen from it. The resolution sketched: infinitization is not a late event befalling a finite geometry; the *first* geometrical act was already an opening to the infinite (the passage to the limit, the "and so on" of iteration), so that the origin is the origin *of* an infinite tradition only by being already in-finite in its sense. The theme — multiple revolutions within one infinite tradition; the infinite at the origin — will be developed in the later sections on the Idea in the Kantian sense.

Confusion B. But if one goes back on the hither side of Galileo, is it now a matter of studying *for itself* the heritage that was proposed to him? No more than before (*Pas davantage*). The question of origin will not be a "historico-philological inquiry" in search of the "determinate propositions" (O., 175\) that the first geometers discovered or formulated. That would be only a *history of science in the classical sense*, making an inventory of the already-constituted *content* of geometrical knowledge — in particular the first postulates, first axioms, first theorems — a content to be explored and determined as precisely and completely as possible from archaeological documents. Despite its incontestable interest, such an investigation can teach us nothing about the *geometrical sense of the first geometrical acts*. And the paragraph ends on the decisive epistemological point, a transcendental-circle argument: such an inquiry *cannot even recognize and isolate those acts as geometrical* except by *already presupposing known* the originary sense of geometry. The philological historian who picks out "the first theorems" from the documentary record has already used a criterion — the sense "geometry" — which his inquiry cannot itself supply. The intentional question is thus prior in principle to the philological one, not as a rival on the same plane but as the condition of the latter's object-identification.

Confusion C, completing the triad and radicalizing it. Even granting that one must return to the *founding sense of the first acts*, it is in no way a question of determining what *in fact* were the first acts, the first experiences, the first geometers who were *in fact* responsible for the advent of geometry. Such a determination, even were it possible, would flatter our historical *curiosity* — and everything Husserl attributes to a certain "romanticism" — and would enrich our knowledge with empirical circumstances, names, dates. But even if, at the limit, it embraced the *totality* of the historical facts that constituted the empirical milieu of a founding of truth, it would leave us *blind* to the very *sense* of that founding: a necessary sense, with regard to which these facts have, at best, only an *exemplary signification*. The argument is the limit-case version of ¶27: the inadequacy of factual history is not quantitative (incompleteness of the record) but qualitative; completeness would not help. *En droit*, this empirical knowledge can present itself *as* historical knowledge of things relating to geometry only by presupposing clarity about the sense of what is called *geometry* — that is, here, about its sense of origin. "The juridical priority of this question of phenomenological origin is therefore *absolute*." The footnote attached to "first" (*premier*) bears substantially on Husserl's lexicon and must be incorporated: "first" (*erste*) almost always designates in Husserl either an indeterminate primacy or, most often, a *de facto chronological priority in constituted cosmic time* — an *original facticity*; whereas *Proto-*, *Arche-*, *Ur-* refer to *phenomenological originarity* — that of sense, of foundation, of right, *after the reduction of all facticity*. The note supplies the reader with the key to a terminological discipline Derrida observes throughout (e.g., *proto-géomètre*, *archi-sol*, *Urstiftung* rendered with *proto-* or *archi-* compounds): the difference between the *first* and the *originary* is the entire difference between fact and sense.

Having established the absolute de jure priority of the phenomenological question of origin, Derrida immediately registers the counter-movement — the paragraph that introduces the Kant comparison and contains in germ the whole problem of the transcendental circle. *And yet* (*Pourtant*), this question can only be *posed* secondarily, at the end of an itinerary that, methodologically, enjoys in its turn an anteriority *of right*. For all the inquiries just dismissed installed themselves in the element of a *constituted* geometry; their object presupposed or merged with the results of *ready-made* geometry — which we had to *reduce* in order to attain a consciousness of origin that would at the same time be an *intuition of essence*. In other words: ready-made geometry, although its content is nothing but ideal essences, here *holds globally the status of a fact* that must be reduced in its facticity in order to be read in its sense. And Derrida adds a play on the French that is also a thesis: the *fait* (fact) has here the forgotten sense of the *tout fait* (the ready-made) — facticity, in the case of ideality, means *constitutedness*, finished-product character, not worldly reality. But — the crucial concession — this reduction *needs, as its point of departure, the constituted result that it neutralizes*. There must always *already have been in fact* a history of geometry for the reduction to be able to operate; I must already have a naïve knowledge of geometry and *must not begin at the origin*. "Here the juridical necessity of the method overlaps (*recouvre* — covers, coincides with, also conceals) the factual necessity of history." Whence the striking aside: despite appearances, *the philosophers of method are perhaps more profoundly sensitive to historicity*, even as they seem to withdraw the parenthesis of the path from history — Descartes and Husserl, the great proceduralists, testify to history precisely by needing a path, a *chemin*, an itinerary that takes time and starts from the already-there. The paragraph then generalizes: the necessity of proceeding *from the fact of constituted science*, the regression toward *non-empirical origins* that are at the same time *conditions of possibility* — these, "as we know," are the imperatives of every transcendental philosophy facing something like the history of mathematics. The footnote supports this with FTL p. 13: we *presuppose* the sciences, and logic itself, relying on the "experience" that delivers them; to that extent our procedure appears in no way radical, since what is in question is precisely the authentic sense of the sciences — yet, authentically or not, we *have* the experience of the sciences and of logic as *cultural formations already given*, carrying in themselves their intention, their "sense." (The note shows Husserl himself thematizing the circle: the regressive inquiry lives off the pre-givenness of what it questions.) The paragraph closes by opening the comparison that will occupy the next several pages: between the Kantian intention and the Husserlian intention, a fundamental difference remains — less easily graspable, perhaps, than one would first imagine. The stage is set: Kant too regressed from the fact of science to its conditions; the difference must therefore be sought elsewhere than in the regressive form.

The Kant comparison opens with the textual warrant: in a historical retrospection toward the origin, Kant *too* evokes (in the Preface to the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*) this mutation or transformation (*Umänderung*), this "revolution" which gave birth to mathematics out of the empirical "gropings" (*tâtonnements*, rendering Kant's *Herumtappen*) of the Egyptian tradition. The short paragraph establishes the prima facie parallel in its strongest form: like Husserl, Kant tells of a birth of mathematics, locates it as an event ("revolution") supervening on a prescientific practice, and treats the Egyptian land-measuring techniques as the pre-geometry from which geometry proper detached itself. Everything in the following pages will consist in measuring how deep the parallel goes and where exactly it breaks.

Derrida now weaves the Kantian text itself into his prose: "the history of this revolution," attributed to "the happy idea of a single man," in "an attempt from which the path one had to take could no longer be missed, and the sure course of a science was opened and prescribed (*eingeschlagen und vorgezeichnet war*) for all times and into infinite distances (*für alle Zeiten und in unendliche Weiten*)," was — Kant says — more "decisive" than the empirical discovery "of the route around the famous Cape." Derrida's footnote states that he is deliberately emphasizing these Kantian expressions because they are also *among the most frequent in the Origin of Geometry*: the prescription of a path, the *Vorzeichnung* (pre-delineation, pre-tracing) of a development, validity "for all times," the opening onto infinite horizons — Kant's vocabulary of 1787 anticipates, almost word for word, Husserl's vocabulary of 1936 for the *Urstiftung* of geometry and the omnitemporality of its acquisitions. The contrast with the Cape route is itself doing philosophical work: an empirical discovery (a sea passage) merely finds what was there; the mathematical revolution institutes a *normed infinite future* — and it is precisely the status of this institution (revelation or creation?) that will divide Kant from Husserl.

Derrida itemizes the genuine common ground, which is more extensive than a textbook opposition of Kant and Husserl would allow. Kant is, like Husserl, attentive to the *historical dimension of a priori possibilities* and to the *original genesis of a truth whose birth-act inscribes and prescribes omnitemporality and universality* — and this not only in the opening of its possibility but in each of its developments and in the totality of its becoming. Like Husserl, Kant neutralizes with the same indifference the *factual content* of this "revolution in the manner of thinking": it matters little to him that its "history" has "not come down to us." The sense of the first demonstration can be rigorously grasped even if we know nothing of the first factual experience and the first geometer — "whether he was called Thales or whatever you like," Kant specifies. The paragraph thus credits Kant with the entire methodological apparatus of ¶34: the reduction of names, dates, and circumstances; the exemplarity (non-foundational status) of the fact; the de jure independence of sense from documentation. If the difference between Kant and Husserl is fundamental, it cannot lie in the *reduction of empirical history* — both perform it identically. It must lie in what the reduction uncovers.

Here the difference begins to emerge, stated first as an asymmetry of *legitimacy*. Kant's indifference to the factual origin (and equally to the content of the example — the isosceles triangle — in connection with which he develops the discovery's implications) is *more immediately legitimate* than Husserl's. Why? Because the inaugural mutation that interests Kant *delivers* (*délivre*) geometry rather than *creating* it: it liberates, in order to hand over to us, a possibility that is *nothing less than historical* (*qui n'est rien moins qu'historique* — i.e., not historical at all). This "revolution" is at first only a "revelation *for*" the first geometer; it is *not produced by him*. It is heard, Derrida says in a fine grammatical observation, under a *dative category*: the activity of the geometer to whom "the happy idea" *came* is only the empirical deployment of a profound *reception*. And what translators have most often rendered as "revelation" is Kant's allusion to "a light given," "a day that dawns for": "*Dem ersten... dem ging ein Licht auf*" — to the first one, a light went up. The dative *dem* twice repeated in Kant's German is the grammatical trace of the receptive structure: the subject of the geometrical revolution is an indirect object. Derrida's footnotes here are scrupulous: one refers to the standard Tremesaygues–Pacaud French translation; the other concedes that such close attention to Kant's expressions is authorized only by the confirmation the whole of Kant's philosophy seems to give them — i.e., the reading does not hang on a metaphor but the metaphor condenses the doctrine (the a priori is not made but found; pure intuition is given, not generated).

The Husserlian counter-concept, and with it one of the Introduction's most important translation footnotes. No doubt, Derrida concedes, the Husserlian *production* (*Leistung*) also includes a stratum of *receptive intuition*. But what matters here is that this Husserlian intuition, where the ideal objects of mathematics are concerned, is *absolutely constituting and creative*: the objects or objectities it intends *did not exist before it*; and this "before" of ideal objectity marks more than the chronological eve of a fact — it marks a *transcendental prehistory* (*une préhistoire transcendantale*). In the Kantian revelation, by contrast, the first geometer simply *becomes conscious* that it suffices for his mathematical activity to remain within the *interiority of a concept he already possesses*; the "construction" to which he then devotes himself is only the *explicitation* of an already-constituted concept that he encounters, as it were, *in himself*. And Derrida adds the precision that converts the contrast into a diagnosis: this Kantian description would no doubt hold, *in Husserl's eyes as well*, for every act of a *non-creative* geometer — it instructs us about the sense of *ready-made geometry as such*, but not about *geometry in the process of founding itself* (*la géométrie en train de se fonder*). Kant's account of the origin is thus, in Husserlian terms, a phenomenology of *sedimented* geometrical activity projected back onto the founding instant: the constituted taken for the constituting. The footnote on *Leistung* must be incorporated in full, since it governs Derrida's entire translation of the *Origin*. Among all the renderings proposed for *Leistung* — so frequent in the *Origin* — "production" seemed to him to cover most properly all the significations Husserl recognizes in this act, which he also designates by complementary notions: *pro-duction*, the leading-forth into the light of day, constitutes the "before us" of objectivity; but this bringing-to-light is also, like all production (*Erzeugung*) in general, a *creation* (*Schöpfung*) and an act of *formation* (*Bildung*, *Gestaltung*), from which the ideal objectity issues as *Gebilde*, *Gestalt*, *Erzeugnis*. Derrida further specifies that he translates *Gebilde* — so frequent in the *Origin*, previously rendered in very diverse ways — as *formation*: the very vagueness of the French word suits the indeterminacy of Husserl's notion, and it accords with the *geological metaphor* that runs through the text, with its multiplied allusions to *sedimentation*, deposits, levels (*étages*), strata (*couches*), and substructures (*soubassements*) of sense. The act that engenders *das Gebilde*, namely *die Bildung*, he could likewise only render as "formation," inserting the German in parentheses whenever *Bildung* has this active sense. And finally — "particularly important here" — *Bildung* also has in German the general sense of *culture*: "formation" again seemed the least foreign to this virtual signification. The note is a small treatise: it shows Derrida choosing a single French word to hold together (i) the phenomenological act-sense of *Leistung/Bildung*, (ii) the product-sense of *Gebilde*, (iii) the geological figure of sedimented sense, and (iv) the *Bildung*\-as-culture resonance by which the constitution of ideal objects and the formation of humanity communicate — the entire semantic field of the *Origin* compressed into one translational decision.

The Kantian block quotation that the preceding analysis glossed in advance: "For, says Kant, he discovered that he had not to follow the trace of what he saw in the figure, or of the mere concept of that figure, but that he had to bring forth (*hervorbringen*) (his object) by means of what he himself put into it and represented in it a priori through concept (through construction); and that, in order to know something a priori with full security, he had to attribute nothing to the thing (*Sache*) except what followed necessarily from what he had himself put into it in conformity with his concept." Derrida's footnote registers a philological scruple: the Erdmann edition notes that *hervorbringen* lacks "its object" in Kant's text — the parenthetical "(son objet)" is the translator's supplement. The scruple is not idle: the very passage in which Kant seems closest to Husserlian *production* (*hervorbringen*, to bring forth — nearly *pro-ducere*) is grammatically incomplete on precisely the question at issue, *what* is brought forth; and the surrounding clauses make clear that what the geometer "puts into" the figure he puts there *in conformity with his concept* — production is subordinated to a possession. The quotation thus exhibits in Kant's own words the structure ¶40 diagnosed: a productive activity entirely enclosed within the interiority of an already-given conceptual norm.

A long paragraph drawing the full consequences of the contrast, culminating in the thesis that Kantian philosophy at once demands and forbids the notion of a transcendental history. No doubt, once the geometrical concept has revealed its freedom with respect to empirical sensibility, the synthesis of "construction" is irreducible — and it is indeed an *ideal history*. But it is the history of an *operation*, not of a *founding* (*l'histoire d'une opération et non d'une fondation*): it deploys gestures of explicitation within the space of a possibility *already opened* to the geometer. At the moment geometry as such is instituted — at the moment, at least, when something can be said of it — it would thus already be *ready to reveal itself* to the consciousness of the first geometer, who is *not*, as in the *Origin*, a *proto-geometer*, a proto-founding geometer (*urstiftende*) — at least in its first concept, the one whose aprioric objectivity will strike any subject whatever with the geometrical light. And since what interests Kant is the possibility of geometry *for a subject in general*, it is not only untroubling but *necessary in principle* that the de facto subject of such a "revelation" be "anyone whatever" and that the guiding geometrical example — the isosceles triangle demonstration — be indifferent. The aprioric nature of this concept, *within* which we operate, forbids its subject any historical investigation of whatever kind: unlike its synthetic explicitation, the concept itself, as a structure of aprioric prescriptions, *cannot have a history because it is not, as such, produced and founded by the act of a concrete subject*. The first footnote here is decisive and must be incorporated: it is the *absence of the decisive notion of the "material" or "contingent a priori"*, as Husserl defined it (Derrida refers to FTL §6), that thus seems to *uproot Kant's formalist apriorism from all concrete history* and to inhibit the theme of a transcendental history; and the level of geometry, as material ontology, is precisely that of such "material a prioris." (The point is architectonic: only if there are a prioris that are *contingent* — essential structures bound to factual content, instituted rather than formal — can the a priori itself have a birth; Kant's exhaustive division of the a priori into the formal and the empirical leaves no room for the historicity of essences.)
