Hence, in Kant: all history can only be empirical; and if there is a birth of geometry, it seems to be for Kant only the *extrinsic circumstance* of the appearance of an always-already-constituted truth to this or that factual consciousness. Likewise the *spontaneous eidetic reduction* that frees the geometrical essence from all empirical reality — from sensible figuration as much as from the geometer's psychological *vécu* — is for Kant *always already effected* (a second footnote generalizes: this seems true of the *totality* of Kant's transcendental analysis — the reduction is everywhere presupposed, nowhere performed). In truth, it is not effected *for or by a subject* who makes himself responsible for it in a transcendental adventure — archi-geometer, or philosopher reflecting on archi-geometry; it is always already made possible and necessary *by the nature of geometrical space and the geometrical object*. Whence the dilemma Derrida springs: short of a conventional, "barely reworked" Platonism, Kant's indifference to empirical history is legitimate only from the moment a *more profound history* has *already created* non-empirical objects. *This history remains hidden for Kant.* Can one not say, Derrida asks, that the theory of the ideality of space and time *demands and represses at the same time* (*exige et réprime en même temps*) the bringing-to-light of an intrinsic, non-empirical historicity of the sciences of space and motion? The reasoning behind the rhetorical question: if space and time were transcendental *realities*, the field would be open at once to an ahistorical metaphysics and to a historicist empiricism of science — two solidary possibilities Kant always denounced in one and the same gesture; but in order to avoid empiricism *first and at all costs*, Kant had to confine his transcendental discourse within a world of *constituted* ideal objects whose correlate was therefore itself a *constituted* subject. The attached footnote connects this local result to a famous global interpretation: one rediscovers here, locally and by a different route, the interpretation proposed by *Fink* and approved by Husserl concerning the *intra-worldliness* (*intra-mondanité*) of the Kantian critique compared with the Husserlian inquiry into the "origin of the world" (Fink's 1933 *Kant-Studien* essay) — Kant's regressive question moves within the already-constituted world toward its formal conditions; Husserl's question bears on the constitution of the world itself, hence on the constitution of the constituted subject-object correlation inside which Kant works. The paragraph's last sentence names the stakes: this *proto-history* — whose notion the whole of Kantian philosophy seems to render *contradictory at the same time as it calls for it* — *becomes a theme for Husserl*.

Husserl's task is thereby only the more perilous, and his freedom with respect to empirical knowledge more difficult to justify at first sight. For one now interrogates the sense of the *production* of geometrical concepts *before and on the hither side of* the Kantian "revelation" — before and on the hither side of the constitution of the ideality of a pure and exact space and time. The justification, when it comes, is the fundamental axiom of the whole Introduction: since *every ideal objectity is produced by the act of a concrete consciousness* — the *sole point of departure for a transcendental phenomenology* — every ideal objectity *has a history* which *always already announces itself in it*, even if we know nothing of its determinate content. History is thus written into the very sense of ideality as such: to be an ideal object is to bear the (possibly empty, contentless) reference to a production, a birth. Two footnotes qualify and support. The first is a single barbed sentence to be retained: by Husserl's difficulty — by his *failure, perhaps* — one will measure the depth of the vigilance in the Kantian "limitation." (The aside cuts both ways: Kant's refusal of transcendental history may be not blindness but lucidity about a genuine impossibility; Husserl's audacity is the experiment that will test whether the Kantian limit was wisdom. Derrida thereby reserves, very early, the possibility that the *Origin*'s project fails — and that its failure is instructive.) The second footnote documents the axiom: that the reference to a historical birth is inscribed in the very sense of every cultural ideality is something Husserl often underlines, in particular in *Beilage* XXVII of the *Krisis* (K, pp. 503–7).

Derrida now turns back to document the *preliminary* stage that the new question surpasses — and to show that it is never revoked. This history of geometry had remained in the shadows; its possibility was judged doubtful, its interest mediocre for the phenomenologist or the mathematician *as such*, so long as the methodological or constitutive analyses, up to *Ideas I*, remained *structural and static*, and so long as all history was "reduced" as facticity or as a science of constituted, intraworldly facticity. The truth of geometry, its normative value, is *radically independent* of its history — which, at this moment of the Husserlian itinerary, is considered only as a *history-of-facts* falling under the *Ausschaltung* (the "switching-off," the exclusion or suspension of the natural thesis). Husserl says so, at the time of *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* and *Ideas I*, in blunt formulas which — *if the levels of explicitation and the senses of the word "history" were not clearly distinguished* — would stand in flagrant contradiction with those of the *Origin*. The first footnote bears substantially on the scope of the early exclusion and should be incorporated: Derrida cites *Ideas I* (§1 and §56), where both *historical origin* and *history as a science of spirit* are "excluded," while the question whether the sciences of spirit are "natural sciences or sciences of an essentially new type" is "provisionally reserved"; and he stresses — quoting §56 at length — that historical "data" are bracketed *as facts and not as norms*: in suspending the natural world one excludes all individual objectivities constituted by the axiological and practical functions of consciousness — products of civilization, works of technique and fine art, *the sciences insofar as they intervene not as a standard of validity but precisely as cultural fact* (Derrida's emphasis), values of every kind, together with realities such as the State, morals, law, religion. The distinction fact/norm within the exclusion clause is exactly what allows the late Husserl to thematize the science-as-cultural-fact *without* ever revoking the bracketing of its factual authority over validity. The paragraph then completes itself in the first of the two "blunt formulas," quoted from *Philosophy as Rigorous Science*: the mathematician will certainly not turn to *history* to obtain information about the *truth* of mathematical theories; it will not occur to him to place the historical development of mathematical representations and judgments in relation with the question of their truth.

The second formula, introduced by a linking phrase — "or again, at the close of the critique of an empiricist theory of the origin of geometry" — and given as a block quotation from *Ideas I* §25: one must not indulge in philosophical or psychological speculations that remain *exterior* to geometrical thought and intuition; one must rather enter *vitally* into this activity and, on the basis of direct analysis, determine its *immanent sense*. It may be that we have inherited from the knowledge accumulated by past generations certain *dispositions* for knowing; but as for the *sense and value* of our knowledge, *the history of this heritage is as indifferent as, for the value of our gold, the history of its transmission* (Derrida's emphasis). The gold simile deserves the separate attention this unit's typographic independence gives it: it is the most concentrated statement of the position the *Origin* will have to *both preserve and overturn*. Preserve: no genealogy confers or removes validity — the assayer, not the archivist, determines the gold's worth. Overturn: the *Origin*'s whole discovery is that ideal sense, unlike gold, *is* its own transmission — that the "value" of geometrical truth includes, in its very *Seinssinn*, the reference to its institution and tradition (¶43), so that the history of the heritage, while still indifferent to validity *as fact*, becomes constitutive of sense *as intentional history*. The fiduciary metaphor of ¶24–25 ("credit") here meets its precursor: Husserl's own early image already thought sense as currency; the late work will ask how the currency was minted and how it stays in circulation (the *avoir-cours*, "being-legal-tender," of ¶63 below).

Derrida draws the moral, against any narrative of self-contradiction or conversion: in truth, the *continuity and coherence* of the position are remarkable. One must *first* reduce the history-of-facts in order to respect and make appear the *normative independence* of the ideal object with respect to it; *then, and only then* — having thus avoided every historicist *or logicist* confusion — the *original historicity of the ideal object itself*. (Note the symmetry of the two avoided errors: historicism would dissolve the norm into facts; "logicism" would freeze the norm into an ahistorical in-itself — the very Platonism Husserl rejects. The order of reductions wards off both.) That is why these first reductions of factual history will *never be lifted* — "in the *Origin* less than anywhere else." The sentence states the Introduction's governing hermeneutic rule, announced already at ¶2: the late philosophy of history is built *on top of* the anti-historicist reduction, not against it; whoever reads the *Origin* as Husserl's reconciliation with empirical history (the reading Tran-Duc-Thao's name will shortly represent) has mistaken the architecture.

An explanation of the early texts' motivation, leading to the hallucination passage. In *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* the point was to *react* against a historicism that reduced norm to fact; in *Ideas I*, to situate geometry *exemplarily* among the *pure eidetic sciences*, which were immediately freed from all facticity because no *existence-thesis* (*Daseinsthesis*) was either necessary or permitted in them. No sensible figuration in the *real* world (Derrida writes *réal* to mark Husserl's *real* — worldly-spatiotemporal reality as opposed to *reell* immanence and to ideality), no psychological experience, no event-content had, *as such*, a founding sense. And the closing sentence, with its memorable formula: the geometrical *eidos* was recognized by the fact that it *withstood the test of hallucination* (*il résistait à l'épreuve de l'hallucination*). The footnote attached to "real" should be incorporated: the essential uselessness and the "inadequation" of sensible illustration were already underlined in the *Logical Investigations* (Derrida gives the references to Élie's translation, hereafter "R.L."), in a passage where Husserl recalls the *Cartesian* distinction between *imaginatio* and *intellectio* in connection with the *chiliagon* — the thousand-sided polygon that can be conceived exactly but never imaged — and, Derrida notes, *announces very precisely the theory of geometrical "idealization"* that he will maintain up through the *Origin*. The note quietly extends the continuity thesis backward: the doctrine that geometrical sense exceeds every possible sensible fulfillment — the doctrine of idealization — is in place by 1901, borrowed from Descartes' Sixth Meditation.

The hallucination passage itself, *Ideas I* §7, as a standalone quotation: there are pure eidetic sciences — pure logic, pure mathematics, the pure theory of time, space, motion; in none of their steps do they posit *facts*; no *experience qua experience* — i.e., as a consciousness seizing or positing reality, existence — plays in them the role of *foundation*. When experience intervenes there, it does not intervene *as* experience. The geometer who draws his figures on the blackboard produces strokes that exist in fact on a board that exists in fact; but neither the physical gesture of drawing nor the experience of the drawn figure, *as experience*, in any way founds the intuition and thought of the geometrical *essence*. Hence it matters little whether, in drawing, he is hallucinating or not, and whether, instead of really drawing, he projects his lines and constructions into an imaginary world. It is otherwise, Husserl ends, with the natural scientist. The long footnote Derrida appends here is one of the interpretive summits of the early Introduction and must be incorporated at length. (i) He first observes that this autonomy of mathematical truth with respect to perception and natural reality is described *only negatively*: it is the *non-dependence* that is underlined; the *positive foundation* of the truth is not sought for itself — one simply reduces, within the mathematical "phenomenon," what cannot presently be retained as foundation. (ii) Husserl measures the eidetic intangibility of mathematical sense against *hallucination*; in the *Theaetetus* (190b) Plato had recourse to the *dream*. (iii) The Husserlian development stands on the same plane and in the same style as the *Cartesian* analysis of mathematical evidence *before* the hypothesis of the Evil Genius in the First Meditation — and Derrida quotes Descartes: arithmetic, geometry, and the other sciences of this nature, which treat only of very simple and very general things *without much caring whether they exist in nature or not*, contain something certain and indubitable; for whether I wake or sleep, two and three together will always make five, and the square will never have more than four sides. (iv) Only *after* this phenomenology of mathematical evidence does there arise for Descartes, with the Evil Genius, the *critical or juridical* question of the *foundation* guaranteeing the truth of a naïve evidence — whose description and "natural" value are, at their own level, never revoked. The originary foundation of these constituted truths is, in Descartes, *delegated to a veracious God*, creator of the eternal truths. (v) Husserl, after an analogous descriptive stage, will seek that foundation *in historical acts of originary founding* (*Urstiftung*). In this respect, Derrida proposes, the Cartesian God — like the God of the great classical rationalists — would be only *the name given to a hidden history*, and would "function" as the *necessary reduction of empirical history and of the natural world* — a reduction that belongs to the sense of these sciences. (The thought is vertiginous and characteristic: the doctrine of the creation of eternal truths is a theological cipher for transcendental historicity; God does the work that *Urstiftung* will do.) (vi) But — the note's final movement, anticipating the Introduction's conclusion — despite this extraordinary revolution which founds absolute and eternal truth *without the help of God or an infinite Reason*, and which thus seems to descend toward a certain *originarily founding finitude* while avoiding empiricism, *Husserl is less far from Descartes than he seems*. This hidden history will take its sense in an *infinite Telos* that Husserl, in his last unpublished writings, *will not hesitate to call God*. True, this infinite — which *always already works the origins* — is not a positive and actual infinite: it gives itself as an *Idea in the Kantian sense*, as a regulative "indefinite" whose *negativity leaves its rights to history*. And it is not only morality but *the historicity of truth itself* that would here be saved by this "falsification" of the actual infinite into an indefinite or an *à-l'infini* (a "to-infinity") — the very falsification of which *Hegel accused Kant and Fichte*. The note thus sketches, in advance and in miniature, the entire trajectory of the Introduction: from the static evidence of ideality, through *Urstiftung* as the truth of the rationalists' God, to the Idea in the Kantian sense as the non-actual infinite that keeps history open — together with the Hegelian objection (the "bad infinite") that Derrida will keep in reserve as the alternative against which Husserl's choice must be measured.

Returning from the footnote to the main line: here the hypothesis of hallucination takes over the role assigned to *fiction* in general — that "vital element of phenomenology" (*Ideas I* §70) — in eidetic determination. The paragraph then executes a tightly compressed inference in three conditional steps. If hallucination does not impair the *eidos* of the constituted ideal object — because the eidos in general and the ideal object in particular are "*irreals*" (*irréels*; non-real without being fantastical realities), and if hallucination even *reveals* them as such; if, on the other hand, the eidos and the ideal object do *not pre-exist every subjective act*, as in a Platonism; if, therefore, they *have a history* — then they must relate, as to their originary foundation, to *proto-idealizations on the substrate of a real, effectively perceived world* (*sur le substrat d'un monde réal effectivement perçu*). But — the crucial final sentence — they must do so *through the element of an original history* (*à travers l'élément d'une histoire originale*). The argument knots together everything established since ¶42: ideality's independence from the real (hallucination test) plus ideality's non-pre-existence (anti-Platonism) jointly entail a founding relation to perceived reality; but that relation cannot be the direct causal-psychological one (already excluded in ¶27), so it must pass through a *medium* of its own kind — original history, the historicity proper to sense. The word *élément* (as in: the element in which something lives, its ether — cf. ¶57's "ether of historical perception") is chosen with care: history is not a sequence of facts behind ideality but the milieu through which ideality relates to its perceptual ground.

A short paragraph that turns the preceding result into an aphorism. Hallucination is "accomplice of truth" only in a *static world of constituted significations*: so long as one interrogates ready-made ideality, the irreality of the hallucinated figure positively serves the exhibition of the eidos (¶48). But to pass to the *foundation and originary constitution* of truth, one must return to a *creative experience* starting from the real world — and even were it unique and buried (*enfouie* — the interment figure again), this experience remains, *de jure as in fact*, first. Whence the chiasmus on which the paragraph closes: one then recognizes that, *in the sphere of sense*, the true contrary of hallucination — as of the imaginary in general — is *not immediately perception, but history*; or, if one prefers, the consciousness of historicity and the *reawakening of origins*. The point is not rhetorical. Within static analysis, hallucination contrasts with perception (positing versus non-positing of reality), and the contrast is *irrelevant* to ideal sense. Where sense itself is in question, what opposes the free-floating image is not the present percept but the *anchorage of sense in its instituting history*: only the reference back to a real founding act distinguishes a tradition of truth from an arbitrary phantasm. History, not perception, is what keeps ideality from being hallucination writ large.

It is therefore only at the level and at the moment marked by *Ideas I* that Husserl *joins Kant* in the same indifference to a history that would be merely extrinsic and empirical — the convergence of ¶38 is now dated and delimited: it holds for static phenomenology only. Consequently, as soon as Husserl undertakes to account for the *genesis* of geometry and to pass beyond this preliminary stage, one might expect him *purely and simply to lift* the eidetic and transcendental reductions and to return to a *constituting history* in which taking the fact into consideration *as such* would become indispensable — because here, for the first time, *as singular historical origin, the founding fact would be irreplaceable, hence invariable*. The invariance of the fact — that is, of what, as such, can never be repeated — would take over, by right, in a history of origins, the *relay* of eidetic invariance — that is, of what can be repeated *at will and indefinitely*. Founding history would be the deep place of the *indissociability of sense and being, of fact and right*; and the notion of "origin," or genesis, could no longer be received there in the pure phenomenological acceptation Husserl had distinguished with such obstinacy. The footnotes must be incorporated. The second documents that obstinacy: the definition of phenomenological origin (distinguished from genesis in the worldly sciences of spirit and nature) opens *Ideas I*, was already clear in the *Logical Investigations* (Prolegomena §67), in the lectures on internal time-consciousness (§2), in *Philosophy as Rigorous Science*, and is reaffirmed in *Erfahrung und Urteil* (§1), FTL (§102), CM (§37), and of course the *Origin* itself — a chain establishing that the term "origin" never, at any period, slides toward worldly genesis. The first footnote, attached to the expectation of a "return to constituting history," names the interpretation that yielded to that expectation, and is the Introduction's one extended engagement with a rival reading: *Tran-Duc-Thao* (*Phénoménologie et matérialisme dialectique*, 1951). For Thao, at the end of the Husserlian itinerary the return to the "technical and economic forms of production" — in Husserlian language, Derrida glosses, the return to *real, factual, extrinsic causality, outside all reduction* — appears inevitable; Husserl, Thao writes, "obscurely sensed" this when, in the celebrated fragment on the *Origin of Geometry*, he sought to *found geometrical truth on human praxis*, the phenomenological explicitation thus orienting itself toward "the determination of the real conditions in which truth is engendered." Derrida's response is compressed but firm: although the reduction never had the sense — quite the contrary — of a negation, an ignorance, or a forgetting that "quits" the real conditions of sense and facticity in general, in order then to "return" to them or not (*for sense is nothing other than the sense of reality or of facticity*), the Husserlian reduction could thus be made to *appear* vain and dissimulating, and the "return" to an empiricist historicism fatal. It would be nothing of the sort, it seems — since dialectical materialism itself, in Thao's own words, claims to stand "on a plane posterior to the reduction," the reduction having suppressed the abstract conception of nature but not effectively real nature, which implies in its development the whole movement of subjectivity. Derrida lets Thao's concession do the work: if even the materialist reading must situate itself *after* the reduction, then the reduction is not an idealist evasion to be discarded but a permanent acquisition — and the *Origin* cannot be read as Husserl's capitulation to the factual history he had bracketed. The note thereby defends, against the most serious existing interpretation of the *Origin*, the continuity thesis of The expectation of ¶51 is now given its full prima facie force before being dismantled. Invariable, the *total fact* marking the establishment of geometry would be so because it has the character that, in Husserl's eyes, *defines* the fact: singular and empirical existence — the irreducibility of a *hic et nunc*. And Husserl indeed says that the upsurge of geometry interests him here insofar as it took place "one day" (*dereinst*), "for the first time" (*erstmalig*), from "a first acquisition" (*aus einem ersten Erwerben*) (O., 175–7). Now — the difficulty stated in its sharpest form — what *authorized* the essence-reading of and in constituted geometry was precisely the possibility of *imaginatively varying* the natural *hic et nunc* of the figure or of the geometer's psychological experience, which, as was seen (¶47–48), was *not founding*. Here, on the contrary, the *hic et nunc* of the "first time" *is founding and creative*. An experience unique of its kind — is it not a *singular fact* for which one ought not to be able to substitute another fact, *by way of example*, for a deciphering of essence? The paragraph thus brings the tension of ¶12 (eidetics versus the once-only) to its point: eidetic method lives on the substitutability of examples; the founding event is defined by non-substitutability; an eidetics of the origin therefore seems to be a contradiction in terms.

A single-sentence paragraph, entirely interrogative, that raises the stakes of ¶52 to the level of the whole project: is this to say that this *inseparability of fact and sense in the unicity of a founding act* will forbid all phenomenology access to history — and access to the *pure eidos of a forever-engulfed origin* (*une origine à jamais engloutie*)? The question gathers the two threats into one: if the founding fact and its sense cannot be dissociated, then neither the eidetic method (which needs to free sense from fact) nor any retrospection (since the origin is empirically lost, "engulfed") can reach the origin — phenomenology of history would be impossible on both the essence side and the access side. The dramatization is deliberate; the next paragraph's first word answers it.

"*Nullement*" — not at all. The indissociability of fact and sense *itself has a rigorously determinable phenomenological sense*. The resolution is not to deny the unicity of the founding act but to redescribe the method: the imaginative variation of *static* phenomenology presupposed a type of reduction *whose style will have to be renewed* in a historical phenomenology. The static reduction, in its eidetic moment, was the *iteration of a noema*: the eidos being constituted and objective, the series of acts intending it could only ever *restore indefinitely the ideal identity* of a sense obscured by no historical opacity — a sense that had only to be elucidated, isolated, determined in its evidence, invariance, and objective independence. The *historical* reduction — which also operates by variation — will be *reactivating and noetic*: instead of repeating the constituted *sense* of an ideal object, one must *reawaken the dependence of sense* with regard to an inaugural and founding *act*, dissimulated beneath secondary passivities and infinite sedimentations — the originary act that *created* the object whose eidos the iterative reduction determines. The noema/noesis distinction is here promoted into a methodological periodization: static eidetics varies and iterates on the object-side correlate; intentional history varies on the act-side, reactivating the productive intention buried under what Husserl calls secondary passivity (the automatic availability of acquired sense) and sedimentation. The paragraph ends with another suspended question of priority: here again, "as we shall see," there is *no simple answer* to the question of the priority of one reduction over the other — the iterative reduction must precede (one must know *what* geometry is before asking after its origin: cf. ¶61 below), yet the reactivating reduction reaches the founding level that the iterative one presupposes; the *Wechselspiel* (interplay) of ¶62–64 is already announced.

A one-sentence paragraph announcing a structure: the *singularity of the invariable first time* already has a *necessity* whose eidetic basis is itself "rather complex." The sentence converts the paradox of ¶52 (a founding, unrepeatable fact) into a program: this very singularity is not an eidetic scandal but an eidetic *structure*, to be analyzed in three numbered steps (*Premièrement*, ¶56; *deuxièmement*, ¶60; *troisièmement*, ¶61). The "first time" will turn out to be necessary three times over: that there was one; that it arose on pre-geometrical ground; that it had the sense it had.

First necessity, and the introduction of one of the Introduction's signature concepts: there is an *essence-of-first-time in general*, an "*Erstmaligkeit*" ("first-time-ness") — an inaugural signification *always reproducible whatever its factual example*. Whatever the empirical content of the origin may have been, it is necessary — with an apodictic and aprioric necessity — that geometry *had* an origin and thus appeared *a first time*. The footnote on the term must be incorporated: the substantive *Erstmaligkeit* seems not to have been employed by Husserl himself; it appears, in place of the adverbial *erstmalig*, in the redaction of the *Origin* published by *Fink* in the *Revue internationale de Philosophie* (1939), where Fink — who also emphasizes *erstmalig* — speaks of an *Erstmaligkeitsmodus*, thereby giving "thematic value to a signification intended by a profound intention of Husserl." (Derrida thus knowingly adopts a Finkian thematization rather than a Husserlian token — a small but exemplary case of the Introduction's method: developing what the text "intends" beyond what it says, while flagging the difference.) The justification follows: the ideal objects of geometry cannot have their original place in some *topos ouranios* — Husserl underlined this already in the *Logical Investigations* in connection with all significations and all ideal objects; the second footnote quotes the passage (LI, II/1, §31): while condemning, in the Platonic manner, those who like the "sons of the earth" can understand by being only "real" being in the world of natural reality, Husserl *simultaneously* rejects the hypothesis of the intelligible heaven — significations are not objects existing, if not in the "world," then in a *topos ouranios* or in a divine mind, "for such a metaphysical hypostasis would be absurd." (The double refusal — neither earth nor heaven, neither real immanence nor hypostasized transcendence — is exactly the space in which historicity becomes the only possible "place" of ideality.) Therefore: the *historicity* of ideal objects is one of their *eidetic components*, and there is no concrete historicity that does not necessarily imply in itself the reference to an *Erstmaligkeit*. Derrida then returns to the objection of ¶52 — that no other fact could be substituted for the unique fact of the first time. Granted, he says; *but only if "other" then qualifies the essence and not the empirical existence as such*. For a unique fact *already has its essence of unique fact*, which — while being nothing other than the fact itself (this is the thesis of the *non-fictive irreality of essence*) — is nevertheless not the *facticity* of the fact but the *sense* of the fact: that without which it could not appear and would give rise to no determination and no discourse. The unrepeatable, in other words, has the repeatable structure *unrepeatability*; unicity is itself an eidetic determination, and what the eidetics of origin varies is not the founding fact (which would destroy it) but factual content *within* the invariant sense "unique founding." Derrida supports this with the famous sentence of *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* — for phenomenological subsumption "the singular is eternally the *apeiron*"; phenomenology can recognize in objectively valid fashion only essences and relations of essence — and immediately delimits it: Husserl there meant by singularity only the unicity of the fact *in its pure facticity*, not the *eidetic singularities* defined in *Ideas I* (§§11, 14–15) as ultimate material essences, which exclude (Ricœur's note, quoted) "only empirical individuality, 'facticity'" — the *tode ti* of brute existence. The paragraph's final movement raises the difficulty proper to *historical* eidetic singularities: the problem of the dependence or independence, the abstract or concrete character, of eidetic singularities — posed in *Ideas I* on the basis of the Third Logical Investigation's notions (whole/part, founding/founded, abstract moment/concrete piece) — is much harder to decide for *historical* singularities, whose empirical fact is *never immediately present*. One could say that the eidetic phenomenology of history, having as such to treat only singularities, is in one sense the most *dependent and abstract* of sciences; but conversely — since certain non-empirical singularities can in certain respects be considered, Husserl says, the most concrete and most independent, and since the singularities of origins are those of the *founding acts of all ideal signification*, in particular of the possibilities of science and philosophy — their history is *the most independent, the most concrete, and the first of the sciences*. The reversal is total and earned: the discipline that seemed methodologically impossible (¶52–53) is now, by the mereology of essences, the *first* science — because its singular objects (founding acts) are what every other ideal signification is founded *on*.

A paragraph of methodological honesty that introduces the doctrine of tradition. The theme of eidetic singularities is, certainly, already delicate enough in *Ideas I*; but there the guiding thread was the immanent *vécu* or the sensible thing perceived *originaliter*, so that singular facticity was *always present* — though reduced — to *guide and control* the intuition of the ultimate material essence. It is no longer so in the inquiry into origins, as soon as *historical distance* interposes itself: the founding facts are irrevocably absent, never given in person, and eidetic intuition loses its perceptual control. *A doctrine of tradition, as the ether of historical perception, then becomes necessary: it is at the center of the Origin of Geometry.* The formula repays attention. "Ether" (*éther*): the invisible medium through which alone the absent origin can affect the present — tradition is to historical intuition what the perceptual field is to perception, the element of givenness itself (cf. *élément*, ¶49). Historical "perception" — the quasi-perceptual evidence with which sedimented sense can be reactivated — is possible only *in* tradition; hence the *Origin*'s analyses of language, writing, and transmission are not auxiliary topics but the constitution of the very medium that makes an eidetics of the absent origin more than a speculation.

Only on these conditions — the eidetics of *Erstmaligkeit*, the doctrine of tradition — can Husserl write what the block quotation now gives (O., 175): our concern must rather be a *question-in-return* about the *most originary sense* in which geometry was *one day born* and, from then on, remained present as a *millennial tradition*; we ask about the sense in which it *entered history for the first time* — *must have entered it* (Derrida's emphasis) — although we know nothing of the first creators and do not even ask about them. Derrida's italics select the modal verb on which his entire preceding reconstruction hangs: *doit y être entrée*, "must have entered it." The quotation is allowed to stand as the textual vindication of ¶¶54–57: Husserl himself couples the confessed ignorance of all factual content (first creators unknown, not even asked about) with an apodictic *must* bearing on the past — exactly the structure of an *a priori of history*.

Commentary on the modal: here "must have entered it" clearly reveals the Husserlian intention and *summarizes the sense of every reduction*. This *devoir* (having-had-to) marks the necessity of an *eidetic pre-scription* and of an aprioric normality *presently recognized and intemporally assigned to a past fact*. Independently of all factual knowledge, I can state this value of necessity. And the necessity is *double*: that of a *Quod* and of a *Quomodo* — the necessity of having had a historical origin, and of having had *such* an origin, such a *sense* of origin. (The scholastic pair compresses ¶56 and anticipates ¶61: the *that* of the first time and the *how* of its sense are both apodictically legible from the constituted result.) But then the counter-stroke, which gives the paragraph its depth: an *irreducible historicity* is recognized in the fact that this "must" *announces itself only after the fact of the event* (*après le fait de l'événement*). I could not define the necessary sense and the necessity of the origin *before* geometry had in fact been born and been in fact delivered to me. "Absolutely free with regard to what it governs, the legality of sense is *nothing in itself*" (*la légalité du sens n'est rien en elle-même*). The a priori of history is thus an *a priori that comes after* — a retroactive apodicticity; norm is unconditioned in validity yet absolutely conditioned in its appearing by the factual event it norms. The footnote must be incorporated, for it generalizes precisely this point: this notion of a *devoir*, an aprioric requisite *concerning a past*, is frequently used in the *Origin*; it marks the possibility of a *recurrent structural determination in the absence of all material determination*. And if this aprioric normativity of history is recognized *from* the fact, *after* the fact, this *after* is not the index of a dependence: the fact does not inform us by its factual content but *as example*. It is in the *specificity of this after* — in the necessity of keeping reduced transcendence or facticity *as guiding thread* — that *the original historicity of phenomenological discourse itself announces itself* (*s'annonce l'historicité originale du discours phénoménologique*). The note's last clause is far-reaching: not only does history have an a priori; the discourse that states this a priori is itself structurally belated, essentially "after" — phenomenology's own historicity is legible in the very form of its method (cf. ¶35: the philosophers of method as the most sensitive to historicity). The themes of delay and of the *après* will return at the Introduction's end as Derrida's own.

Second necessity (resuming the enumeration of ¶55), stated in a single compact paragraph: whatever the first geometrical idealities produced or discovered *in fact* may have been, it is a priori necessary that they *succeeded a non-geometry* — that they arose on a *ground* (*sol*) of *pre-geometrical experience*, the phenomenology of which is possible thanks to an appropriate reduction and *de-sedimentation* (*dé-sédimentation*). Three things are fixed here: (i) the origin is essentially *relative* — a first time is necessarily a first time *after*, and "before geometry" is itself an apodictic structure, not an empirical conjecture; (ii) this pre-geometrical stratum (the lifeworld of measurable shapes, surfaces, edges — what the *Origin* and the *Krisis* analyze as the world of *Körper* and practical measurement) is accessible *now*, by a methodical operation; (iii) that operation is named with a word — *de-sedimentation* — that condenses the geological figure (¶40) into a method: the reduction, in its historical style, is an excavation, an undoing of deposited strata to lay bare the founding soil. The term will have a notable posterity in Derrida's own later work (where "de-sedimentation" stands beside "deconstruction" in *Of Grammatology*); here it names Husserl's own regressive procedure.

Third necessity, completing the triad and folding the method back on itself: whatever *in fact* the first geometers and the empirical content of their acts may have been, it is a priori necessary that the *instituting gestures* had a sense such that geometry issued from them *with the sense we know it to have* (*avec le sens que nous lui connaissons*). For — and here the circle of ¶54 is run in the other direction — the *reactivating* reduction *presupposes* the *iterative* reduction of static and structural analysis, which teaches us once and for all what the "phenomenon" geometry *is*, once its possibility is constituted. That is to say: by a necessity that is anything but an accidental and external fatality, *I must set out from ready-made geometry*, as it is current now and as its phenomenological reading is always possible for me, in order to interrogate *through it* its sense of origin. It is thus that I can — *at once thanks to the sedimentations and in spite of them* — restore to history its *traditional diaphaneity* (*diaphanéité traditionale*: the becoming-transparent of the tradition, the seeing-through of deposited strata down to the founding evidence; "thanks to and in spite of" states the constitutive ambivalence of sediment, which both transmits and conceals — vehicle and screen of sense, exactly the "threatening value" of ¶29). The paragraph then dwells on the word Husserl uses for this procedure and on Derrida's translation of it: Husserl speaks of "*Rückfrage*" — a rather common notion, no doubt, but one that here takes on a sharp and precise sense. Derrida has translated it as *question en retour*, "question in return," and he unfolds the rendering's motivation in a celebrated passage: like its German counterpart, the question-in-return is marked by the *postal and epistolary* reference or resonance of a *communication at a distance* (*Rückfrage* is, in ordinary German, the back-inquiry one addresses to the sender of a letter or order for clarification). Like the *Rückfrage*, the question in return is posed *on the basis of a first sending* (*à partir d'un premier envoi*). On the basis of the *document* received and *already readable*, the possibility is offered to me of inquiring *anew and in return* about the *originary and final intention* of what has been *delivered* to me by tradition. Tradition — "which is only *mediacy itself* and the opening to a *tele-communication* in general" — can then, as Husserl says, "*let itself be questioned*" (O., 176). The postal figure is exact, not ornamental: it captures (i) the priority of reception (one always begins as addressee, never at the origin — ¶35, ¶59); (ii) the distance constitutive of tradition (sense reaches us only across an interval — the "ether" of ¶57); (iii) the legibility-condition (only a received *and readable* document permits the back-question: writing will accordingly become central in later sections); (iv) the bidirectionality of the relation (the heir can write back — reactivation is the return post). That Husserl's word for the deepest gesture of transcendental history is borrowed from postal commerce confirms ¶45's fiduciary insight: the constitution of ideal sense is thought, by Husserl himself, in figures of transmission, circulation, and correspondence.

These analogies — postal sending, currency, sediment — which are, Derrida says, at the *metaphorical focus* (*foyer métaphorique*) of our text, confirm how necessary the "zig-zag" procedure is: proposed in the *Krisis* as a sort of necessary "circle," it is "only the pure form of every historical experience." The footnote gives the *Krisis* passage (§9l) and should be incorporated: we thus stand in a sort of circle; the understanding of the *beginnings* can be fully gained only from the science *given in its present form*, by retrospection on its development; but *without* an understanding of the beginnings, this development is *mute as a development-of-sense* (*muet en tant que développement-de-sens*); we have no other choice — we must *pro-ceed and retro-cede* in "zig-zag." The circle here is not the vicious circle of logic but the productive circularity already analyzed in ¶35 and ¶61: present result and originary sense are each legible only through the other, so method must shuttle. Derrida's one-sentence elevation of this into "the pure form of every historical experience" generalizes beyond Husserl's methodological remark: all relation to the past — not only the historian's — has this to-and-fro structure; the zig-zag is the schema of traditionality as such. (Readers of Derrida will recognize in *foyer métaphorique* an early instance of his attention to the constitutive — not decorative — role of metaphor in philosophical texts, the program later executed in "La mythologie blanche.")

The question in return is now characterized, with a deliberately political pun, as the "*reactionary* and therefore *revolutionary* moment" of this *Wechselspiel* (interplay): re-actionary in the literal sense of acting-back toward the sending, and revolutionary because only this return can de-sediment and re-found — the *Rückfrage* turns back in order to turn over. It would be *impracticable*, Derrida argues, if geometry were not by essence something that *never ceases to have currency* (*avoir cours*) *in the ideality of value*. The monetary idiom of ¶45 is now made into a concept: *avoir-cours*, being-legal-tender, names the omnitemporal validity-in-circulation of ideal sense. No doubt — Husserl's gold simile is recalled verbatim — "no more than the history of its transmission founds the value of gold" can any *worldly* history deliver the sense of this having-currency, since worldly history on the contrary *presupposes* it. Rather, it is the *maintenance* of the having-currency that *permits the neutralization of worldly history*; and this neutralization then *opens the space* of an *intentional and intrinsic history of the having-currency itself*, and allows one to understand how *a tradition of truth* is possible in general. The dialectical sequence deserves restatement: (i) ideal validity is independent of factual transmission (early Husserl, ¶45); (ii) precisely this independence, once secured by reduction, makes visible a *new* historical question — not "how did the facts produce the value?" but "how does validity itself endure, circulate, traditionalize?"; (iii) the answer to that question is the theory of truth-tradition. The early reduction of history is thus the *enabling condition* of the late phenomenology of history — the continuity thesis (¶46) now fully cashed out. The paragraph closes with a balanced formula on what matters most to Husserl: as much an *operation* — reactivation itself, insofar as it can open a hidden historical field — as the *nature of that field itself*, insofar as it makes something like reactivation possible. Noetic and noematic faces again (¶3): the act of reactivating and the structure of traditionality are strictly correlative, and the *Origin* investigates both at once.

Section II's concluding paragraph, gathering the methodological harvest and naming the stakes. It is therefore only *under the shelter of the reductions of static phenomenology* (*à l'abri de* — both "sheltered by" and "under the protection of") that we can perform *other reductions, infinitely more subtle and perilous*, which will deliver at once the *singular essences of the acts of institution* and, in their *exemplary weave* (*trame exemplaire*), the *total sense of an open history in general*. Without the *Wechselspiel* of this *double reduction* — iterative and reactivating, static and historical — the phenomenology of historicity would be struck with vanity, *and with it the whole of phenomenology* (*et avec elle toute la phénoménologie*). The escalation in that clause should not be missed: it is no longer merely that intentional history would fail; since ¶¶13 and 20 established that the historicity question engages phenomenology "through and through" and touches the ultimate horizon of sense, a failure here would be a failure of phenomenology *as such*. And the final sentence weighs the alternatives: if one takes as established the *philosophical non-sense of a purely empirical history* and the *impotence of an ahistorical rationalism*, one measures the gravity of what is at stake (*la gravité de l'enjeu*). The two discarded positions are the two horns between which the entire Introduction moves — historicism without sense, rationalism without history — and the double reduction is the only proposed passage between them. On this measured note of high stakes, Derrida turns (section III) to Husserl's own warnings about the generality and poverty of what such a method can deliver.

## **III**

Section III opens by converting the methodological vigilance accumulated in section II into a sober estimate of what the method can actually yield. All these precautions, Derrida writes, have made us sensitive to the *extreme difficulty* of the task — and Husserl himself underlines the *preliminary and general* character of the meditation, in a sentence that seems borrowed *word for word* from *Formale und transzendentale Logik* (Derrida's note: the Introduction, p. 9 of that work): the *question en retour* "inevitably holds itself to generalities; but, as will soon become manifest, these are generalities susceptible of a fecund explicitation" (O., 175). Two things matter in this brief unit. First, the verbatim echo of the *Logic* is not a philological curiosity: it certifies that the *Origin* continues the program of transcendental logic by other means, so that the historical *Rückfrage* inherits both the ambitions and the self-limitations of that earlier critique of logical reason. Second, the paradoxical pairing — inevitable generality, fecund explicitation — sets the problem the whole section will work through: how can what is essentially poor be nonetheless fruitful?

Derrida immediately hardens the paradox. As an *a priori* determination, phenomenology will *never* be able to enrich these generalities, whose *indigence is essential*: poverty is not a provisional defect awaiting empirical supplementation but the very mode of being of principial knowledge. Their "explicitation" will be "fecund" only within work of a *prospective, regional, and in a certain sense naïve* style — that is, within the positive sciences themselves and the regional inquiries that take the generalities for granted. But — and here is the gain — this naïveté would *no longer have the sense it had before* the *prise de conscience* of these generalities, a becoming-conscious Husserl qualifies as "critical" and which will have a *regulative and normative* value for that work. By recalling us ceaselessly to the unperceived presuppositions of problematics that have always already arisen (*survenues*), it will guard us from *confusion, forgetting, and irresponsibility*. The triad is precise: each term has been thematized earlier — confusion in the three confusions of section II (¶¶27, 33, 34), forgetting in the great footnote on the three significations of *oubli* (¶29), responsibility as the ethico-theoretical register of reactivation. Critique does not produce content; it transforms the *sense* of naïveté, converting unavoidable presupposition into assumed presupposition.

A standalone quotation from the same Introduction to the *Logic* (pp. 9–10) gives Husserl's own formulation of this regulative function: when science has made decisions issuing from a *principial responsibility*, these decisions can engrave in life *normative habitualities* that direct the will, insofar as they trace forms within which individual decisions must and can maintain themselves, provided they are really assumed. For a rational praxis, a priori theory can only be a *delimiting form*; it can only set up *barriers* whose crossing signifies *countersense or confusion*. The passage supplies the juridical model of the a priori that Derrida needs: principial generalities legislate negatively, marking the boundary beyond which sense collapses, rather than positively generating results. Note also how the quotation knots together the vocabulary of the preceding analyses — decision, responsibility, habituality — with the sedimentation theory to come: a principled decision *sediments* as a normative habitus, which is precisely the structure of tradition that the *Origin* will describe at the level of science as a whole.

The first of these principial generalities is the one that *authorizes the question en retour itself*: the unity of sense of geometry is that of a *tradition*. Derrida's formulation turns on the play of articles, which he italicizes: the becoming of geometry is *a history* (*une histoire*) only because it is *one history* (*une histoire*) — the indefinite article read a second time as a numeral. However far its edification progresses, however generous the proliferation of its forms and metamorphoses, these will never put in question the unity of sense of what, in this becoming, remains to be thought as *la* géométrie. The foundation of this unity is the *world itself* — not as a finite totality of sensible beings but as the *infinite totality of possible experiences in a space in general*. Hence the unity of *la* géométrie, which is also its *unicity*, is not confined to the systematic coherence of *une* géométrie whose axioms are already constituted; it is the unity of geometrical sense of a tradition *infinitely open to all its revolutions*. To pose the question of this traditional unity is to ask: under what *historical* conditions have all geometries been, or will all geometries be, geometries? The paragraph thus restates the infinitization analysis of section II (¶32) as a thesis about identity: revolution-proof unity is possible only because the ground of geometry is the infinite world-horizon, not any constituted axiomatic system — and the question of that unity is irreducibly historical-transcendental in form, a quid juris asked of history.

Derrida now clears away two candidate identifications of this unity. It is *not* the general concept extracted or abstracted from the various known geometries: on the contrary, only the *concrete originary essence* of geometry could give rise to such a generalizing operation in the first place — abstraction presupposes the essence it claims to produce. Nor does it coincide with the concept Husserl himself *in fact* determined concerning the ideal that orients geometrical practice within the *objective thematic field* of the science. (A footnote here, incorporating *Logic* §9, recalls the two "faces" of a science's thematic and the scientist's exclusive orientation toward the objective face: "The geometer... will not think to explore, besides geometrical forms, geometrical thought as well" — the very forgetting of the noetic side that the *Origin* reverses.) That historically marked concept is the ideal of a "definite" nomology and an *exhaustive deductivity*: from an axiom system that "dominates" a multiplicity, every proposition is determinable either as analytic consequence or as analytic contradiction — a supposedly unsurpassable alternative. (Derrida's notes send the reader to Cavaillès, Tran-Duc-Thao, and above all Suzanne Bachelard for the dossier, and to the canonical Husserlian definitions in the *Prolegomena* §70, *Ideen* I §72, and *Logic* §31.) The vulnerability of this confidence has been amply shown: it was belied, in particular, by Gödel's discovery in 1931 of the *rich possibility* of "undecidable" propositions. The adjective is deliberate — *riche* — already hinting that undecidability will be treated not as a catastrophe for the question of origin but as a positive mathematical phenomenon to be situated within it.

This is the decisive move of the section. All the questions about whether the Husserlian requirement of definiteness can be maintained — whether as a regulative ideal inaccessible by essence, or as an actual methodological and technical rule (which, Derrida notes, no longer seems possible in general) — are posed precisely *within* the unity of the geometrico-mathematical horizon in general, as the open unity of *a science*. To this horizon, which is what Husserl interrogates here, belong the preoccupations with decidability *themselves*. In its very negativity, the notion of the *un-decidable* — besides having such a sense only through some irreducible reference to the *ideal* of decidability — also holds a *mathematical value*, drawn from some unique source of value deeper than the project of definiteness itself. The whole debate is intelligible only within something like *la* géométrie or *la* mathématique, whose unity is *to come* on the basis of what announces itself in its origin. Whatever answers the epistemologist or the working scientist gives to the important intra-mathematical questions of definiteness and saturation, those answers can only *integrate themselves* into the unity of the mathematical tradition questioned in the *Origin*; and they will only ever concern, within the "objective" thematic sphere where they must exclusively remain, the determinate nature of axiom systems and of the deductive chains they do or do not authorize. But the objective thematic field of mathematics must *already be constituted in its mathematical sense* for values of consequence and non-consequence to be problematized at all — and for one to be able to say, against Husserl's classical affirmations, *tertium datur*. The footnotes here are of the first importance. The second simply documents the target: *Logic* §31's definition of the definite multiplicity, governed by a "saturated" (*vollständig*) axiom system on the model of an ideal Euclid, where every well-formed proposition is either true (analytic consequence) or false (analytic contradiction) — *tertium non datur*. The first footnote, however, is one of the philosophical summits of the Introduction: Gödelian undecidability, Derrida argues, *confirms* the *Origin*'s analyses of the synthetic style of mathematical traditionality. The movement that enriches sense retains, at the bottom of the new sense, the *sedimentary reference* to the antecedent sense and cannot do without it; the intention aiming at the new sense has originality only insofar as it is still *inhabited* by the anterior project which it does not merely "succeed." Thus undecidability "has a revolutionary and disconcerting sense, it is itself, only if it remains essentially and intrinsically *haunted* in its sense of origin by the *telos* of decidability whose *disruption* it marks." The sentence deserves to be read twice: the structure it describes — a negative term that lives only off the ideal it disrupts, a disruption internally constituted by what it disrupts — is the exact matrix of what Derrida will later generalize under the names of trace and différance. Here it serves a strictly Husserlian purpose: to show that Gödel does not touch the question of origin, because the very sense of "undecidable" presupposes the open unity of the mathematical tradition whose origin is in question.

The consequence is drawn for the legitimacy of the *Origin* itself. *If* the unity of sense and the origin of mathematics that interest Husserl were essentially tied to the ideal of exhaustive deductivity — a fortiori if they were confused with it — then the question of the *Origin* would be tainted from the start by a certain historical relativity, whatever Husserl himself thought and whatever interest the question might retain as a period piece. In other words: if the *fondation originaire* Husserl wants to solicit here were the institution of an axiomatic and its deductive field, or even the institution of axiomatics in general and of the deductive ideal as such, and if this institution were described as that of *mathematicity itself*, then the Husserlian project would be gravely menaced by the evolution of axiomatization toward total formalization, where one necessarily runs up against the limits stated by Gödel's theorem and its kin. *Mais il n'en est rien* — nothing of the sort. Even if Husserl at one moment made the axiomatic-foundation conception his own, and even proposed it as an ideal for "all the 'exact' eidetic disciplines" (*Ideen* I §7), he saw in it, it seems, only a *second* foundation. In any case there is no doubt that the originary evidences sought here are in his eyes *anterior* to those of the axioms and serve as their *foundation*. And Derrida lets the *Origin* say so itself (192–3), in a quotation whose parenthesis he italicizes: one must also take into consideration the constructive activities operating with geometrical idealities which, though "elucidated," have not been brought to originary evidence — "(Originary evidence cannot be interchanged with the evidence of the 'axioms'; *for the axioms are in principle already the results of an originary formation of sense* (Sinnbildung) *and always already have this formation itself behind them*.)" The axioms — the absolute beginnings of the deductivist imagination — arrive late, trailing behind them the sense-formation that produced them. The long footnote attached here is a small treatise on Husserl's stratification of evidences. *Verdeutlichung* ("elucidation," kept for grammatical convenience, though Suzanne Bachelard renders it more rigorously as the "process of rendering distinct") must be confused neither with *Klärung* (clarification) nor with reactivation: it remains *within* the constituted sense, bringing it to distinctness without restoring the fullness of its clarity — its value as actual knowledge — and above all without reactivating its originary intention. And Fink's elaborated version of the *Origin*, Derrida adds, sharpens the architecture by distinguishing two moments of reactivation in general: reactivation as "logical elucidation," and reactivation of the internal *Sinnbildungstradition* present in a thematic sense-formation; only when the first is achieved does reactivation as *question en retour* toward the proto-foundation begin. This, Derrida concludes, confirms the *necessary anteriority of static analysis* and the static fixation of sense, which must control every genetic procedure — the lesson of ¶64's double reduction, now certified from within the theory of evidence itself.

A short, lapidary paragraph seals the argument. Axiomatics in general — that on the basis of which alone any ideal of exhaustive and exact deductivity can take on sense, and any problem of decidability can subsequently arise — therefore *already supposes a sedimentation of sense*: an originary evidence, a radical foundation, *which is also a past*. The axiomatic is thus *already exiled* from the origins to which Husserl now wants to return. Two formulations carry the weight. First, the equation of originary evidence with a past: the radical foundation is not a timeless ground but an event that has *already* receded — foundation and sedimentation are the same structure seen from two sides. Second, the word *exilée*: formal systems do not merely come after the origin; they are banished from it, constitutively unable to recover by their own means the sense-history they presuppose. The exile motif quietly prepares the later analyses of sedimentation as loss and of reactivation as return.

Derrida now reinterprets Husserl's own earlier doctrine in this light. If, from the *Logical Investigations* through *Ideen* I to the *Logic*, Husserl indeed assigned to geometrical determinability the narrow sense of decidability, it is because in those *non-historical* researches he let himself be guided by the present state of a *ready-made science* (*une science toute faite*). But as soon as the question of origin awakens, geometrical determinability appears to have the sense of *determinability in general*, as the infinite horizon of a science, whatever forms its future developments may take. (A footnote enlarges the point to its maximum: this broad geometrical determinability would itself be only the regional and abstract form of an *infinite determinability of being in general*, which Husserl so often calls the ultimate horizon of every theoretical attitude and of all philosophy.) When the *Origin* speaks of a "horizon of geometrical future *of the same style*" (O., 177), this style is not deductivity but *geometricity in general*, mathematicity in general — under which the undecidables, and any other mathematical formation to come, will still and always fall. Which means: in a research of origin, the ideal of decidability is *itself reduced*, like every factual stage of the history of mathematics, like every determinate factual tradition in an unveiling of pure traditionality. Hence Husserl's repeated precision in the *Origin* that the exact sciences are sciences "*called* deductive" — "so named although they in no way content themselves with deducing" (O., 193). There is therefore a truth, or rather a *sense* of geometrico-mathematical truth in general, that does not let itself be enclosed in the alternative of "true" and "false" prescribed by the ideal of the definite multiplicity (where, as *Ideen* I §72 has it, "true" is equivalent to "formal consequence of the axioms" and "false" to its formal contrary). The originary unity of sense of geometrical truth that orients the *Origin* could thus announce itself in a question of this type: *what is mathematical determinability in general, if the undecidability of a proposition — for example — is still a mathematical determination?* And by essence, such a question cannot be the expectation of a determinate answer; it must *only indicate the pure opening and unity of an infinite horizon*. The closing sentence (its last words fall at the top of the next page) recalls the structure of the Idea in the Kantian sense already met in ¶48: the question of origin is regulative, not constative — it opens the horizon within which every answer, including Gödel's, has its place.

A pivot paragraph announcing the section's organizing tension. Since the opacity of the fact could be reduced *from the origin onward* by the production of ideal objects, historical enchainments are enchainments *of sense and of values* that can never cease to *avoir-cours* (to hold currency — the term established in ¶63), capitalizing their sedimentary deposits *to infinity*, according to an original mode. The economic metaphor — capitalization, deposits, currency — is now fully assumed: the tradition of truth is an economy in which nothing is ever lost and everything accrues. But Derrida immediately marks the modal status of this picture: it is a *possibility, not a necessity*. And the interest and difficulty of Husserl's analysis lie precisely in the fact that it develops *on two planes at once*. The rest of the section will alternate between these planes, signaled by the paired adverbs *tantôt... tantôt* (at times... at other times): science as one cultural form among others, and science as the archetype of culture as such.

First plane. *Tantôt* Husserl considers geometry and science in general as forms *among others* of what he calls the world of culture — from which they indeed borrow all their characteristics. This world is entirely "arisen from tradition" (*issu de la tradition*, O., 176), and the sciences are *traditions among others*. About tradition in general we possess certain a priori evidences that no ignorance of factual history can dent. On the one hand, we know — with "a knowledge whose evidence is irrecusable," an "implicit knowledge" that inhabits "always and essentially" the factual non-knowing (O., 176\) — that cultural formations always refer back to *human productions*; hence, Husserl concludes *immediately*, to *spiritual acts* — "a gesture to which we shall return further on," Derrida notes, discreetly flagging for future scrutiny the speed with which Husserl passes from human production to spirit, leaping over body, language, and inscription. This reference to the producing act is *inscribed in the formation itself*, but it can pass unnoticed precisely because of the *autonomy* of the ideal formation — whence the necessity of recalling these *a priori banalities* buried by science and culture (the imperative of *Trivialität* that Husserl frequently justifies, e.g. *Krisis* §9h: the deepest truths of the origin have the form of the trivial). On the other hand, we know likewise that humanity has a past, and that within it were found the "*first creators*" (O., 176), who instituted new spiritual forms — but who could only do so by disposing of materials that were raw *or already traditional*, that is, already informed by spirit. Even the absolute beginners inherit: the *Urstifter* works on pre-given material, so that institution is never creation ex nihilo. The a priori of tradition thus has two faces: backward reference of every formation to productive acts, and backward reference of every production to prior material.

On the other hand — still within the a priori of tradition — the traditional becoming whose *totality* every culture gathers at each moment, in a mediate or immediate synchrony, does *not* have the style of *causal genesis*. In the world of natural reality, subject to development of the causal type, sedimentation is not that of a sense that gathers itself and ceaselessly *resumes itself in its interiority*. No more than for Hegel, and *for the same reasons*, is there a *natural history* for Husserl. And Derrida promises that the analogy will prove deeper still: for Husserl as for Hegel, culture itself, in its finite empirical unities, does not suffice to constitute the *pure unity of a history* — this will be the case of all the *anthropological cultures* that do not participate in the *European eidos*. The paragraph makes two moves of very different range. The first is classical: sense-sedimentation versus causal stratification, interiorizing memory versus external accumulation — the Hegelian *Erinnerung* discreetly invoked. The second is explosive and deliberately left hanging: the exclusion of "merely anthropological" cultures from pure historicity, and the disquieting phrase "European eidos," which Derrida here neither endorses nor criticizes but flags as the necessary consequence of the distinction between empirical culture and culture of truth — a problem to which the Introduction will return when it confronts Husserl's remarks on Europe, India, and China.

A compact historical placement: here the *Origin* repeats the critique of Diltheyanism mounted in *Philosophy as Rigorous Science*. Husserl accepts from Dilthey both the critique of the causalist naturalization of spirit and the principle of an original typo-morphology of cultural totalities — *Weltanschauungen* have their own structural types, irreducible to nature. But he wants to wrest the idea of science — "that is, first of all philosophy" — from the *subjective immanence* of the *Weltanschauung*. The 1911 front is thus redrawn in 1936: against naturalism, with Dilthey; against historicism, beyond him. The insertion matters architectonically, since it certifies the continuity Derrida has claimed since ¶47 between the *Origin* and *Philosophy as Rigorous Science*: the hallucination argument there, the Weltanschauung argument here, are two faces of the same defense of ideal sense against its reduction to fact.

This long and extraordinarily dense paragraph concedes everything that can be conceded to the first plane, then erects upon the concession the entire temporal infrastructure of traditionality. No doubt, *as a form of culture*, the idea of science also belongs to the Weltanschauung; no doubt the content of science and philosophy is transmitted by the *same process* as every other form of culture and tradition in general. And this process is *analogous, if not identical*, to those of the internal consciousness of time, described from a noematic point of view in the lectures of 1904–1910. Derrida then compresses the time-lectures into a few lines: the present appears neither as *rupture* with nor as *effect* of a past, but as *retention of a past present* — that is, retention of retention, and so on. The retentional power of living consciousness being *finite*, consciousness keeps past significations, values, and acts in the form of *habitus* and *sediments*. And here is the function of traditional sedimentation in the communal world of culture: *to surpass the retentional finitude of individual consciousness*. Tradition is the prosthesis of retention — culture does for the community what retention can no longer do for the ego, a thought that silently prepares the role writing will play later in the Introduction. But sedimentary retention, Derrida continues, is not only the condition of possibility of protention: it belongs *essentially* to the general form of protention, itself comprised under the absolutely unique and universal form of the *Living Present* (*Présent Vivant*). The Living Present, primordial absolute of temporality, is nothing but the *maintenance* of what must indeed be called the *dialectic* of protention and retention — "despite Husserl's repugnance for that word," Derrida adds, in a phrase that both registers the heterodoxy and insists on it (the vocabulary descends from his own early work on genesis and from Tran-Duc-Thao). In the movement of a protention, the present retains itself and *exceeds itself* as past present, in order to constitute *another* absolute, originary and original — another Living Present. Without this "extraordinary absolute alteration" of what nevertheless always remains in the concrete, lived form of an absolute Present — without this *always renewed originality of an absolute originarity*, always present and always lived as such — no history would be possible. The formulation is among the most consequential in the Introduction: the absolute of presence maintains itself only through its own incessant alteration; self-identity is constituted by self-exceeding. (Readers of Derrida's later *La voix et le phénomène* will recognize the exact point where his deconstruction of the Living Present will be inserted; here the alteration is still presented as what Husserl's absolute *requires*, not what undoes it.) The consequence for history follows directly: what is true of the Living Present is true of what presupposes it as its foundation, the *historical present*, which always refers, more or less immediately, to the totality of a past that inhabits it and that always appears under the general form of the *project*. At every moment, every historical totality is a cultural structure animated by a project, which is an "idea." Thus — the sentence breaks off into quotation — "the *Weltanschauung* too is an 'idea'" (*Philosophy as Rigorous Science*, Lauer translation, p. 113), the ellipsis suspending us before the decisive counter-stroke of the next paragraph.

Second plane. *Tantôt au contraire*: at other times Husserl describes science as a *unique and archetypal* form of traditional culture. Beyond all the characteristics it shares with other cultural formations, it enjoys an essential privilege: it cannot be enclosed in any determinate historical culture *as such*, for it has the *universal value of truth*. A cultural form that is proper to *no* factual culture, the idea of science is the *index of pure culture in general*; it designates the *eidos of culture par excellence*. In this sense, the cultural form "science" (of which geometry is an example) is itself "*exemplary*" in the *double sense, eidetic and teleological*, of that word: it is the *particular example* that guides eidetic reduction and intuition — the specimen from which the essence of culture is read off — but it is also the *model example* that must orient culture as its *ideal*. Derrida's isolation of this double exemplarity is one of the Introduction's quiet theoretical inventions: the same term holds together a logical function (instance of an essence) and a normative-teleological function (norm for a becoming), and the slippage between them is constitutive of Husserl's whole discourse on Europe and on philosophy. Science is the idea of what, from the first instant of its production, *must hold for always and for everyone*, beyond every given cultural area: the *infinite idea* opposed to the *finite idea* that animates the Weltanschauung. The block quotation from *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* completes the antithesis begun at the end of ¶78: the Weltanschauung too is an "idea," *but* of a goal lying *in the finite*, realizable in principle in an individual life by way of constant approach; the "idea" of Weltanschauung is therefore *different for each epoch*, while the "idea" of science is *supratemporal*, limited by no relation to the spirit of an epoch. Science is a title for *absolute, intemporal values*; each such value, once discovered, belongs thenceforth to the *treasure of values* of all subsequent humanity, and evidently determines in turn the material content of the idea of culture, of wisdom, of Weltanschauung itself. The economic figure returns — the treasury — now with its full teleological charge: the finite ideas of the Weltanschauungen are themselves progressively determined by the infinite accumulation they cannot contain.

Derrida now draws the structural distinction that the two planes demand. In a *pure, non-descriptive* science, the mode of sedimentation is such that *at no moment does any signification cease to avoir-cours*: it can always be re-understood and *reawakened* in its currency. If it was necessary earlier to distinguish natural reality from spiritual culture, we must now, in order to understand pure culture and pure traditionality in general, discern *within* culture: between *empirical culture* — factual historical culture, where the sedimentation of sense does not exclude the *péremption* (lapsing, expiry) of *value*, rooted as value is in a language, a soil, an epoch — and the *culture of truth*, whose ideality is *absolutely normative*. The footnote here is doctrinally important: as Husserl had already underlined in the *Logical Investigations* (II/1, §32), *ideality is not always normative*; value is a *superior ideality* that may or may not attach itself to ideality in general — and, Derrida adds in anticipation, we shall see later that *the sense of error has its own ideality*. (False geometries, refuted physics, lapsed currencies of thought remain ideal formations, repeatable and identifiable forever; what they have lost is validity, not ideality.) The culture of truth, Derrida grants, would doubtless be impossible *in fact* without empirical culture. But, on the one hand, it is empirical culture's *highest and most irreducible possibility*; and on the other hand, it is itself only the possibility of a *reduction of empirical culture* — it appears to itself only through such a reduction, which became possible through an *irruption of the infinite*, as a *revolution within empirical culture*. The infinitization of ¶32 thus returns at the level of culture: the culture of truth is empirical culture having undergone, historically, something like its own transcendental reduction.

*Du même coup*, culture and tradition of truth are marked by a *paradoxical historicity*. In one sense they can appear *disengaged from all history*, since they are not intrinsically affected by the empirical content of real history or by determinate cultural enchainments — Pythagoras' theorem does not age. And this emancipation can be *confounded* with a liberation from history *in general*. Derrida's diagnosis of the confusion is symmetrical, recalling the two horns of ¶64: for those who hold to historical *facticity* (the historicists) just as much as for those who enclose themselves in the *ideality of value* (the logicists or conventional Platonists), the historical originality of the *récit* of truth — the story, the account of truth's advent — can only be that of *myth*.
