If truth has no intrinsic history, then any narrative of its origin is mere fable: this is the shared conclusion of empiricism and Platonism, and it is exactly what the *Origin*'s notion of an intrinsic sense-history is built to refuse.

But in *another* sense — the one that answers to Husserl's intention — the tradition of truth is *the most profound and the purest history*. Only the pure unity of sense of such a tradition can found that *continuity* without which there would not even be authentic history, history *thinking itself and projecting itself as such*, but only an empirical aggregate of finite and accidental unities. Once phenomenology has freed itself from *conventional Platonism* as well as from *historicist empiricism*, the movement of truth it wants to describe is indeed that of a *concrete and specific history* — one whose foundations are the *acts of a temporal and creative subjectivity*, themselves founded on the sensible world and on the *Lebenswelt* as world of culture. The paragraph completes the chiasmus: far from being history's exception, the tradition of truth is history's *eminent case*, the only becoming that fully satisfies the concept of history (self-related, sense-transmitting, projective); and far from floating free of the world, this purest history is anchored, by the now-familiar relation of *Fundierung*, in temporal subjectivity and the life-world.

The mode of this history is then named: progress by *totalization and permanent repetition of its acquisitions*. Geometry was born — and the sentence opens directly onto the *Origin*'s own words — "from a *first* acquisition, from first creative activities. We understand its persisting mode of being: it is not only a continuous movement from acquisitions to acquisitions, but a *continuous synthesis* in which all acquisitions *persist in their value*, all form a totality, such that at each present the total acquisition is, one might say, the *total premise* for the acquisitions of the following stage... It is the same for every science." The quotation is the charter of everything section III has been arguing. Three features deserve underlining: the *Erstmaligkeit* motif returns ("a *first* acquisition," Derrida's italics) binding the analysis back to ¶¶52–56; the figure of the *total premise* gives logical form to the economic figure of capitalization — at every present, the whole past of the science functions as a single premise from which the next step is drawn; and the closing generalization ("the same for every science") confirms geometry's exemplary status (¶79): what is described through the example of geometry is the traditionality of science as such.

Derrida glosses the quotation's closing generalization with a restriction and a reference. "It is the same for every science" — *understand: for every non-descriptive science*. The continuous synthesis of total acquisition characterizes the pure, ideal, deductive-style sciences, not the descriptive disciplines tied to the contingencies of their material. And these syntheses do not unfold in a *psychological* memory, were it even collective, but in that "*rational memory*" so profoundly described by Gaston Bachelard — a memory founded on a "*recurrent fecundity*," alone capable of constituting and retaining "*events of reason*" (the note cites *Le rationalisme appliqué*). The appeal to Bachelard — the epistemologist of rupture enlisted on behalf of Husserlian continuity — is pointed: even a philosophy of recommencement must posit a memory proper to reason itself, irreducible to psychology. Husserl, Derrida adds, had drawn the underlying distinction as early as the *Philosophie der Arithmetik*: between psychological temporality as mere successivity — the kind Hume described — and the temporality of the *synthetic enchainments of sense*. He never ceased explicating this difference, and in the *Origin* (190) he underlines that a scientific stage is not merely "*a sense that in fact arrived later*," but the *integration into a new project of the totality of the antecedent sense*. Succession in fact versus implication in sense: the whole theory of traditionality holds in that distinction.

From this becoming, which totalizes itself ceaselessly in an absolute Present, *egological* subjectivity cannot be responsible. Only a *communal subjectivity* can produce the historical system of truth and *answer for it totally*. Yet — and the paragraph immediately balances the claim — this total subjectivity, whose unity can only be absolute and a priori (without which the least truth would be unimaginable), is *only the common place* of all egological subjectivities, actual or possible, past, present or future, known or unknown. The structure is carefully suspended between two errors: hypostasizing the community as a substantial super-subject, and dissolving it into a mere collection of egos. The total subject is nothing *outside* the egos ("common place" — *lieu commun*, with its ironic overtone deliberately audible), and yet it is presupposed a priori by every truth any ego utters.

A standalone quotation from the *Origin* (O., 177\) gives Husserl's own formulation: every science is related to an *open chain of generations* of researchers, known or unknown to one another, working *with* one another and *for* one another, insofar as they constitute, for the totality of living science, *the productive subjectivity*. The sentence deserves its isolation: it defines the noetic correlate of the "continuous synthesis" of To the open totality of acquisitions on the noematic side corresponds, on the noetic side, an open chain of generations functioning as a single productive subjectivity — generationality, working-with and working-for, as the very flesh of theoretical reason.

Derrida unfolds the quotation's implications in one of the section's most intricate paragraphs. The totality of science being *open*, the total community too has only the *unity of a horizon*. Moreover, the image of the "open chain" does not exhaust the depth of this communal subjectivity, for it has not merely the unity of solidarity and co-responsibility: each scientist does not only feel himself *bound* to all the others by the unity of an object or a task. His *own subjectivity as scientist* is constituted by the idea, or horizon, of this total subjectivity that *renders itself responsible in him and through him* for each of his acts as scientist. *In him and through him* — that is, without substituting itself for him: for at the same time he remains *absolute origin*, constituting and present source of truth. Phenomenologically, Derrida writes, the transcendental *we* is *nothing other than* the transcendental *I*: the acts of the latter, at the very moment they can appear mandated by an ideal community, never cease to be irreducibly those of a monadic "I think," from which it suffices to reduce the empirical egological content in order to discover the dimension of the "we" *as a moment of the eidos "ego."* One would even be tempted to think, Derrida ventures, that it is the *we* that makes possible the reduction of the empirical ego and the appearing of the eidos "ego" — were it not that such a hypothesis would, against Husserl's most manifest intentions, place the egological monad in a position of *abstraction* with respect to the total subjectivity. (The temptation is registered and withdrawn in the same breath; its trace remains, marking a point where Derrida sees the Husserlian order of foundation trembling.) In any case: if there is a history of truth, it can only be this *concrete implication and reciprocal envelopment of totalities and absolutes* — possible only because the implications in question are *ideal and spiritual*. And the description of these two characters, ideality and spirituality, so frequently invoked in the *Origin*, answers to no metaphysical assertion: they are, moreover, "founded" in the sense of *Fundierung*. A footnote discreetly sets aside the "redoubtable difficulties" of the Fifth Cartesian Meditation — the constitution of the other — into which the Introduction declines to enter here; the gesture is honest, since the whole edifice of communal subjectivity presupposes precisely what that Meditation struggles to secure.

The *irreducible historicity* of geometrical becoming is then marked by a quotation: "*the total sense of geometry*" — and Derrida interpolates: with its necessary noetic correlate, the total subjectivity — "*could not have been there already at the beginning as a project, pursuing itself in a movement of fulfillment*" (O., 178). The counterfactual is decisive. If the history of geometry were only the development of a design present *from the origin in its totality*, we would be dealing merely with an *explicitation* or a *quasi-creation*: on one side a *synchronic or uchronic* foundation — outside time altogether — on the other a purely empirical *diachrony* serving as mere revelator, with no unity of its own. And *neither pure diachrony nor pure synchrony makes a history*. The hypothesis refused is, once again, that of a *complicity between "Platonism" and empiricism*: the timeless deposit of all geometry and the merely factual sequence of its discoveries are two faces of one and the same denial of history. (The Saussurean-sounding pair synchrony/diachrony, supplemented by the rarer *uchronie*, lets Derrida formulate with maximal economy what an intrinsic history of sense must be: neither structure without becoming nor becoming without structure, but the traditionality in which sense is totalized only by being produced, stage after stage.)

Section III closes by letting the *Origin* name what such a history requires at its head. In truth, *before even the open project of geometry was possible*, "a more primitive formation of sense (*Sinnbildung*) \[as a preliminary stage\] had necessarily preceded, such that, indubitably, it appeared for the first time in the evidence of a successful effectuation" (O., 178). Every word of the quotation gathers a thread of the section: *Sinnbildung* (the term whose translation Derrida weighed in ¶40), the *necessity* of the antecedent (an a priori of tradition, not an empirical conjecture), the *first time* (the *Erstmaligkeit* of ¶¶52–56), and *evidence of a successful effectuation* (the act-character of the origin). The project of geometry — the infinite task — is thus itself preceded by a pre-projective institution of sense whose form is evidence. It is exactly here, at the threshold of this primitive evidence, that section IV will find Husserl executing his "disconcerting detour."

## **IV**

Section IV opens on what Derrida calls a *détour* that "can seem disconcerting." Arrived at the threshold of the primitive *Sinnbildung*, Husserl does *not* describe this genesis in itself and in its *Erstmaligkeit*. He considers it *tacitly and provisionally as already effectuated*, the sense as already evident, and contents himself with recalling that we know the *general form* of this evidence (in terms, a footnote notes, that recall *Ideen* I but above all *Logic* §59). Like every evidence, perceptive or eidetic, of natural reality or ideal object, it must be — cannot not be — "the grasping of a being in the consciousness of its being-there, originally and in person" (O., 178): the "principle of principles" of *Ideen* I. However little we know of the *first* geometrical evidence, we know a priori that it *had to* take this form. But Derrida immediately marks the status of this knowledge: bearing though it does on a historical origin, it is *anything but historical*. Defining a "source of right" for the knowledge of any object whatsoever, it belongs to those *formal* a prioris presupposed by every material science — here by geometry *and by history*. It is because the first geometrical evidence had to conform to this form that we can have a first certainty about it *in the absence of any material knowledge*; for, for the moment, the *content* of the geometrical evidence — which *is* historical, because created a first time — remains undefined, treated by Husserl as already acquired. The detour is thus a strategic regression from content to form: history is approached first through the unhistorical form of all evidence.

This abstention before the content of the originary act is *provisional* — a methodological limitation, and once again (cf. ¶¶54, 64, 71\) the necessity of taking one's departure *in the constituted*. But Derrida insists that the methodological necessity is legitimate only on the basis of a *profound philosophical decision*. Once this step is taken, Husserl pursues the meditation, now protected by this *formal jurisdiction*, *as if* his theme were no longer the origin of geometrical *sense* but the genesis of the *absolute — that is, ideal — objectivity* of sense, the sense being already presented to some consciousness. Again and again he returns obstinately to one question: how can the *subjective, egological* evidence of sense become *objective and intersubjective*? How can it give rise to an ideal and true object with all the characteristics we know — omnitemporal value, universal normativity, intelligibility for "everyone," *uprooting from all facticity of the hic et nunc*? This, Derrida observes, is the *historical repetition* of the question of objectivity posed throughout the five lectures of *Die Idee der Phänomenologie*: how can subjectivity go outside itself to encounter or constitute the object? The footnote attached here is among the Introduction's richest. Husserl had posed the same question, in its most general extension and with a more critical, less historical inflection, in *Logic* §100 — but limited there to the egological sphere. Here it is oriented toward the possibility of *objective spirit as condition of history*, and thereby takes the exact *contre-pied* of Dilthey's question. Dilthey *starts* from objective spirit as already constituted, asking how the significations and values of this objective milieu can be interiorized and assumed by individual subjects — above all by the historian working from testimonies. That question led Dilthey, like Husserl, to discover a *non-psychological* dimension of the subject; Derrida quotes the remarkable passage from the *Plan der Fortsetzung* (Gesammelte Schriften VII): how can a nexus that was never produced in any single head, never directly lived, be constituted as such in the historian? — "This presupposes that *logical subjects*, which are not psychological subjects, can be constituted." The *Origin*, Derrida says, *turns this question around*, in formulas strangely resembling Dilthey's: its "reverse side" concerns the radical origin and conditions of possibility *of objective spirit itself*. How, starting from the sense-enchainments and evidences of a *monadic ego* — from which we cannot not start, in right as in fact — can an objective spirit in general constitute itself as the place of truth, tradition, co-responsibility? And the note closes on a thesis to come: no more than the psychological subject will a "*logical*" subject be able, for Husserl, to answer for such a possibility — only the transcendental subject, neither psychic fact nor logical form, can.

The detour's rationale is now stated as a thesis. Husserl abstained provisionally before the historical content of the *Erstmaligkeit* only in order to pose *first* the question of its *objectivation* — that is, of its *mise à flot* in history: its being set afloat, launched. For a sense *has entered history only if it has become an absolute object*: an ideal object which, *paradoxically, must have broken all the moorings* (*amarres*) that held it to the empirical ground of history. *The conditions of objectivity are therefore the conditions of historicity itself.* The nautical figures (setting afloat, casting off moorings) carry the paradox the whole Introduction turns on: entry into history is achieved only by a rupture with history's empirical soil; what makes geometry historical — transmissible, traditional, alive across generations — is exactly what makes it omnitemporal. Empirical attachment would condemn sense to sink with its circumstances; only the unmoored object can travel.

From this follows a rule of method that Derrida raises to an essential law. When Husserl later devotes a few lines to the production and evidence of geometrical sense *in its proper content*, he will do so only *after* having determined in general the conditions of its objectivity. The pure sense of the subjective praxis that engendered geometry can be illuminated only *retroactively* and *from its result*; the sense of the constituting act can be deciphered only *in the weave* (*trame*) *of the constituted object*. And this necessity is not an external fatality but an *essential necessity of intentionality* itself: the originary sense of every intentional act is nothing other than its *final* sense, namely the constitution of an object (in the broadest sense of these words). "*That is why only a teleology can open a passage toward the beginnings.*" The sentence condenses the deepest methodological claim of the Introduction: archaeology is possible only as teleology; the *arche* is legible only in the light of the *telos*, because intentionality is, in its very structure, oriented toward the accomplished object. The *Rückfrage* of ¶61 here receives its ultimate justification — the question must travel backward along intentionality's own forward-directed line.

Derrida then widens the lens to a vertiginous degree. If the sense of geometrical sense is objectivity (or the intention of objectivity); if geometry is the exemplary index of scientificity; and if the history of science is the highest, most revelatory possibility of a universal history whose very concept would not exist without it — then what is being determined here is *the sense of sense in general*, and it is determined *as object*: as the thing *disposed and available* in general, and first of all *for a gaze* (*pour un regard*). Derrida is careful about the direction of dependence: the mundane image of the gaze is not the unnoticed model of the theoretical attitude of pure consciousness; on the contrary, it *borrows its sense from* that attitude. And this determination, he adds, is *profoundly attuned to the initial direction of phenomenology*: the object in general is the *last category* of everything that can appear — that is, *be* for a pure consciousness in general; it is upon the object in general that all the regions are articulated with consciousness, which is the *Ur-Region* (*Ideen* I §76). The paragraph should be read twice: once as faithful exposition (Husserl's own architectonic does make objectivity the universal form of appearing), and once as the discreet laying of a charge that Derrida's later work will detonate — the determination of the sense of sense *as object for a regard*, as availability for presence, is precisely what *La voix et le phénomène* will name the metaphysical decision inhabiting phenomenology. Here the observation remains entirely internal, even approving; but the sentence "it is the sense of sense in general that is here determined as object" is one of the Introduction's most heavily freighted.

A crucial clarification of the genetic narrative's status. When Husserl affirms that a production of sense must *first* have presented itself as evidence in the *personal consciousness of the inventor*, and then poses the question of its *subsequent* objectivation — in a factitious chronological order — he is evoking *a sort of fiction*, destined to problematize the characteristics of ideal objectivity and to show that they *do not go without saying*. In truth, there is not first a subjective geometrical evidence that would *then* become objective. There is geometrical evidence only "*from the moment when*" it is evidence of an ideal objectity; and the ideal objectity is such only "*after*" having been put into *intersubjective circulation*. The scare quotes do the philosophical work: the narrative sequence (private insight, then publication) is a methodological fable whose moral is structural, not chronological — geometrical evidence and ideal objectivity and intersubjectivity are co-originary, each "preceding" the others only in the order of exposition.

A standalone quotation from the *Origin* (O., 178–9) states the structure in Husserl's own voice: geometrical existence is *not psychic existence*, not the existence of something personal in the personal sphere of consciousness; it is the existence of a being-there *objectively, for "everyone"* (for the real and possible geometer, or for whoever understands geometry). Better still: from its *proto-foundation* onward, it has a *specifically supratemporal* existence, accessible — we are certain of it — to all human beings, first of all to the real and possible mathematicians of all peoples and all centuries, and in all its particular forms. The quotation fixes the explanandum of the next three sections: an existence that is neither psychic nor physical, indexed to no one and no time, yet *instituted* — datable in principle to a proto-foundation. The tension between "since its proto-foundation" and "supratemporal" is the entire problem.

A brief, sharp hinge. The "before" and "after," then, must be *neutralized in their facticity* and used *in quotation marks*. But — and here is the question that governs the rest of the section — can they be *simply replaced* by the intemporal "if" or "provided that" of the *condition of possibility*? If the answer were yes, the *Origin* would dissolve into ordinary transcendental philosophy, and its narrative form would be mere ornament. Derrida's entire reading wagers that the answer is no: the quotation marks suspend empirical chronology without converting genesis into timeless logical implication.

The objection is given its full force. The language of genesis can indeed appear *fictive* here: it would be called for, not by the description of a real becoming — neutralized from the start — but by the exhibition of *formal conditions of possibility*, implications *de jure*, eidetic stratifications. Is this still *history*? Does one not fall back into a *classical transcendental regression*? And is not the enchainment of transcendental necessities, even though it is *recited in the mode of becoming*, at bottom the *static, structural and normative schema* of the conditions of a history, rather than history itself? The vocabulary (static schema, structure, norm) deliberately echoes the earlier oppositions (¶¶54, 64, 88): if the objection held, the *Origin* would be synchrony in diachronic costume — Kant narrated, not history thought.

Derrida's answer occupies one long, decisive paragraph. Such questions could seem grave, putting in question the very originality of the attempt; but they remain, it seems, *exterior to the Husserlian intention*. Granted: there is in this *récit* "not the least grain of history" *if one means by that the factual content of a becoming* — but the necessity of this reduction was justified from the outset. The "irritated disappointment" of those who would expect Husserl to tell them *what really happened*, to tell them a story, may be lively and easily imaginable — a footnote names Tran-Duc-Thao, for whom the "subjectivist point of view" confined the *Origin* to "a few common-sense remarks" — but it is *in no way legitimate*. For Husserl wanted only "to *decipher in advance the text hidden beneath every empirical narrative* about which we might be curious." (The metaphor of the hidden *text* — sense-history as a writing legible under factual history — should be carefully noted; it will be literalized when writing itself becomes the theme in section VII.) Fact-history may afterward give itself free rein: whatever its style, its method, its philosophy, it will always, more or less naively, *presuppose* the possibility and necessity of the enchainments Husserl describes. And now the reconciliation: these enchainments are indeed always marked with a juridical and transcendental signification, *but* they refer to *concrete and lived acts* within a *unique* system of founding implications — a system *originarily produced only once* and *irreversible in fact as in right*. They are therefore the enchainments of what is, *in the fullest sense of the word, history itself*. Thus, before what is through and through a *historical adventure*, whose fact is *irreplaceable*, an a priori and eidetic reading, an a priori discourse, must be possible. And such a possibility was not *invented* by Husserl but simply *unveiled*, as what has always implicitly conditioned the existence of the ideal objects of a pure science — hence of a pure tradition, hence of a pure historicity, *model of history in general*. The argument's center of gravity is the pair *unicity-irreversibility*: what distinguishes this transcendental enchainment from a Kantian table of conditions is that it was produced *once*, as an unrepeatable adventure, so that the a priori here is the a priori *of* an event — an eidetics of the irreplaceable.

Section IV closes on the objection's final form and its dialectical resolution. *Pure-enchainments-of* history, *a-priori-thought-of* history — does this not mean that these possibilities are not themselves historical? *Nullement* — not at all: for they are *nothing but* the possibilities of the *appearing of history as such*, *outside of which there is nothing*. "It is history itself that founds the possibility of its own appearing." The chiasmus is complete and deliberately vertiginous: the transcendental of history has no site outside history; the conditions of history's appearing are themselves engendered by what they condition. This self-founding — history producing the very opening in which it appears — is Derrida's formulation of what makes Husserl's enterprise neither historicism nor classical transcendentalism, and it hands the baton directly to section V: the *first name* of this possibility, of this self-appearing of history, will be *language*.

## **V**

"This possibility is first called '*language*.'" The opening sentence of section V picks up ¶100's self-founding of history and gives it its first concrete name. If we ask how the subjective evidence of geometrical sense *conquers its ideal objectivity*, we must first remark that ideal objectivity is *not only* the character of geometrical or scientific truths: it is *the element of language in general*. The word *élément* should be heard in its strong, almost pre-Socratic sense — the medium, the ether in which language lives and moves — anticipating the description of tradition as the "ether" of historical perception (¶57). Before ideality can distinguish science, it must already pervade speech as such: the strategy of the section will be to begin from this common ideality and ascend, degree by degree, to the absolute ideality proper to geometry.

A standalone quotation from the *Origin* states the breadth of the claim: ideal objectivity "is proper to a whole class of spiritual products of the cultural world, to which belong not only all scientific formations and the sciences themselves, but also, for example, the formations of *literary art*." The inclusion of literature is no ornament. By Husserl's own testimony, the ideal objectivity to be explained is the ideality of *works* in general — of everything that is repeatable as the same across its sensible instantiations — so that the *Pythagorean theorem* and the *novel* initially stand on the same side of the line, over against natural realities. The whole labor of the section will be to find, *within* this common class, the differences of degree that single out geometry.

Derrida notes that the movement is analogous to the one analyzed earlier (¶¶75, 79): just as science was first presented as one cultural form among others before its "exemplary" privilege was defined, so the ideal objectivity of geometry is first presented as a character *common to all forms of language and culture* before its specific eminence is determined. In an important note, Husserl specifies that "*literature in the broadest sense*" (O., 179\) comprehends *all ideal formations*, since these, to be what they are, must always be *expressible in a discourse* and *translatable*, directly or not, from one language into another. Which is to say — and Derrida draws the consequence with precision — ideal formations are rooted *only in language in general*, not in the *facticity of a particular language* and its own linguistic incarnations. The distinction between *le langage* (language in general, speech as such) and *une langue* (a factual, historical tongue) here begins the work it will perform throughout the section: ideality needs the first and must be independent of the second.

A single-sentence paragraph of orientation: through these themes — already present in the *Logical Investigations* and the first paragraphs of *Formale und transzendantale Logik* — there appears "the finest specificity of the Husserlian question." The sentence is a claim about where to look: not in the grand architecture but in the most delicate stratum of the doctrine of ideality and expression, where Husserl's question separates itself from every neighboring philosophy of language or of essence.

The ideal object, Derrida now asserts, is the *absolute model of the object in general*. It is always *more objective* than the real object, than the natural being: for if the latter resists and opposes more — if the stone is more stubborn than the theorem — it always resists *a factual, empirical subjectivity*, and therefore can never attain that absolute objectivity which is *proposable to every subjectivity in general in the intangible identity of its sense*. Hence the question "how is an object in general possible?" assumes its *sharpest and most adequate form* in the *Origin*, when Husserl asks: "*How is ideal objectivity possible?*" — and there too it attains its greatest difficulty, since no recourse remains to the natural objectivity of the worldly being. Moreover, once the level of ideal objectivity is reached, *several degrees* are still to be encountered: the announcement of the tripartite analysis to come (¶¶106–113). The footnote attached to "ideal object" is a small doctrinal monument: this ideality of the object — here, of the mathematical thing itself — must not be confused with the *non-reellity of the noema* described in *Ideen* I (§§88, 97 ff.), which characterizes the intentional inclusion of *every* noema in the lived experience, whatever the type of being aimed at and whatever the mode of aiming, even in the perception of a real thing. And yet, Derrida adds, there is no doubt that this non-reellity of the noema — "a notion of the most difficult and the most decisive" — is what, in the last analysis, *permits the repetition of sense as the "same"* and *makes possible the idealization of identity in general*; this could be shown precisely from *Logic* §62, on the "ideality of all species of objectities over against constituting consciousness." The note quietly states one of Derrida's deepest theses about Husserl: beneath the regional distinction between ideal and real objects lies a universal proto-ideality — the noema's non-reell status — which is the root of all repeatability; *La voix et le phénomène* will make this the lever of its entire reading.

First degree. No doubt the *language* (*la langue*) is itself "built through and through... out of ideal objectities; for example, the word *Löwe* (Lion) occurs only once in the German language, and is the identical pole of innumerable utterances by which anyone aims at it" (O., 180). The example — Husserl's own lion — gives the section its emblematic animal: the *word* is already ideal, since "Löwe" is not this or that vocalization but the one word aimed at through them all.

Derrida unfolds the lion's first lesson. The word has an *ideal objectivity and identity*, since it is not to be confused with any of its empirical, phonetic, or graphic *materializations*: it is always *the same word* that is intended and recognized through all possible linguistic gestures. Insofar as it is this ideal object that always *faces* language as such, language presupposes a *spontaneous neutralization of the factual existence* of the speaking subject, of the words, and of the thing designated. "Speech is thus only the practice of an *immediate eidetics*." And Derrida seals the point with André de Muralt's just formula: "the reduction is implicitly operated, simply exercised and not yet explicated, as soon as one considers language for itself." To speak is already, unknowingly, to reduce: every speaker treats the word as an ideality and brackets the existence of its instances. The footnote attached to "immediate eidetics" is one of the Introduction's most literary and most quietly subversive. The idea of the linguistic neutralization of existence, Derrida observes, is original in phenomenology only by its technical and thematic signification — was it not dear to Mallarmé and to Valéry? Above all *Hegel* had explored it amply: in the *Encyclopaedia* (one of the rare Hegelian works Husserl seems actually to have read), the lion already bore witness, "an exemplary martyr," to this neutralization — "Given the name *lion*, we need neither the intuition of such an animal nor even the image: the name, when we understand it, is the simple imageless representation; *it is in the name that we think*" (§462, cited from Hyppolite's *Logique et existence*, a book which, Derrida notes, reveals on many points "the profound convergence of Hegelian and Husserlian thought"). And further, from the *System* of 1803–04 via Blanchot's *La part du feu*: "The first act, by which Adam made himself master of the animals, was to impose a name on them — that is, *he annihilated them in their existence* (as existents)." Name as the death of the thing: by aligning Husserl's spontaneous neutralization with Hegel's annihilating nomination (and with the poets of the absent flower), Derrida inscribes the phenomenology of language within a lineage in which ideality and negativity, signification and the murder of the existent, are the same operation — a convergence whose full consequences his own later work will assume.

But the de Muralt line must not be overdrawn — and here Derrida turns the screw. It is the *eidetic* reduction that speech spontaneously practices. For precisely that reason, paradoxically, it seems *more difficult* to say, as de Muralt also does, that "a thought moving solely at the level of language is necessarily in the attitude of *the phenomenological reduction* (Derrida's emphasis): it is on a level footing with the eidetic world of significations, of pure lived experiences." For if the phenomenological reduction has its *full* sense, it must also be the reduction *of the constituted eidetics* — and therefore *of its own language*. An imperative, Derrida adds dryly, that the precaution of "*quotation marks*" satisfies only equivocally. This transcendental reduction of the eidetics — which, at its most radical moment, must still fold us back toward a *new* eidetics, irreducibly necessary, that of pure consciousness — raises considerable difficulties, of which Husserl was aware and which he exposes with the greatest clarity in *Ideen* I. The footnote quotes §59: "we cannot exclude transcendences without ever encountering limits; transcendental purification cannot signify the exclusion of *all* transcendences, since otherwise a pure consciousness might indeed remain, but no longer the possibility of a *science* of pure consciousness." Husserl there concludes that formal logic and ontology can be reduced provided the "logical axioms" (such as non-contradiction) are conserved, their absolute validity being illustratable from pure consciousness's own data. *But*, Derrida observes, "about the *language* of this ultimate science of pure consciousness — about the language that seems to presuppose at least the sphere of formal logic just excluded — *nothing is said*." Univocity of expression and certain precautions taken within and by means of language itself — distinctions, quotation marks, neologisms, the revalorization and reactivation of old words — will *always* remain, for Husserl, sufficient guarantees of rigor and non-worldliness. That is why, the note concludes, despite the remarkable analyses devoted to it from the *Investigations* to the *Origin*, "the specific problem of language — of its origin and of its use in a transcendental phenomenology — has always been *excluded or deferred*": explicitly so in *Logic* §§2 and 5, and in the *Origin* itself, which declares that it will not enter "the general problem of the origin of language." The diagnosis is fundamental for everything that follows: phenomenology lives on a linguistic credit it never audits.

The main text draws the paradox tight. It is *in the very measure that it is not "natural"* that language offers *the most dangerous resistance* to the phenomenological reduction; and transcendental discourse will remain *irreducibly obliterated* — struck through, defaced as by an over-stamping — by "a certain ambiguous worldliness." And if one recalls that in the *Origin* the possibility of history is *first indicated as possibility of language*, one measures the difficulty of any attempt to reduce, in some ultimate and radical transcendental regression, a phenomenology of historicity "whose non-dependence is thus confirmed once more": history, like the language that carries it, cannot be made simply to appear *before* a tribunal whose own discourse it conditions. The immense footnote that runs beneath these lines is among the most celebrated pages of the Introduction, and its threads deserve enumeration. (a) Fink: the reduction "cannot be expounded by means of the simple sentences of the natural attitude — one can speak of it only by *transforming the natural function of language*" (letter of 1936); and in the Royaumont lecture on *operative concepts*, Fink attributes the equivocation of notions like "constitution" to the fact that "Husserl never posed the problem of a '*transcendental language*,'" asking whether after the reduction one can still "dispose of the Logos in the same sense as before." (b) Suzanne Bachelard: "language is ignorant of the phenomenological reduction and keeps us in fee to the natural attitude" — whence the danger of a surreptitious return to psychologism. (c) Derrida's own radicalization: starting from the *Origin*'s problematic, one may ask what is the *hidden, non-thematic, dogmatically received sense* of the very words "history" and "origin" which, as the common focus of their significations, *permits* one to distinguish fact-"history" from intentional "history," "origin" in the banal sense from phenomenological "origin." What is the *unitary foundation* on the basis of which this *diffraction of sense* is possible and intelligible? So long as the notion of origin in general is not critiqued *as such*, "the radicalist vocation is always menaced by that *mythology of the absolute beginning* so remarkably denounced by Feuerbach" in his 1839 critique of Hegel. (d) These questions could show the necessity of a renewed and rigorous *philological or "etymological" thematic* that would *precede* the discourse of phenomenology — a redoubtable task, since it presupposes as resolved all the problems it ought to precede, in particular the prejudicial problem of history itself and the possibility of a historical philology. It never seemed urgent to Husserl, even when the idea of the "reactivation" of language took on such importance for him; unlike Heidegger, he almost never indulges in etymological variations, and when he does, they *follow* rather than determine the research — for in his eyes it would be absurd that sense should not precede, *in right* ("and here the right is a difficult evidence"), the act of language, whose proper value will always be that of *expression*. (e) The generalization: it is significant that *every* critical, juridical, transcendental enterprise renders itself vulnerable through "the irreducible facticity and natural naïveté of its language"; attention to the *fact* of language in which a juridical thought lets itself be transcribed is "a return to facticity as the *right of right* (*droit du droit*)" — "a *reduction of the reduction*, opening the field to an *infinite discursivity*." (f) The Kantian parallel: Herder's *Metakritik* already reproached Kant for ignoring the intrinsic necessity of language and its immanence to the most a priori act of thought, concluding that language, rooted in cultural experience and history, made the apriority of synthetic judgments impossible or illusory; and the unavowed dogmatism thus denounced — "this non-thematicity of received language that precedes every critical regression *like its shadow*" — is perhaps that *geschichtlose Naivität* which Fink wondered whether it was not the very basis of phenomenology's methodological revolution. There are, Derrida remarks, many such analogies between the aftermaths of Kantian and of Husserlian transcendental idealism "as they are already taking shape." (g) The conclusion, deliberately suspended: in any case there is signified here "an irreducible proximity of language to originary thought, in a zone that by nature escapes all phenomenal or thematic actuality. Is this immediacy the neighborhood of thought with itself? *It would be necessary to show why it is impossible to decide.*" In this last sentence — undecidability lodged at the heart of the relation between thought and language — the Introduction's most far-reaching program is stated in miniature; the "reduction of the reduction" and the "infinite discursivity" it opens are, almost to the letter, the future program of deconstruction.

The ascent through the degrees begins. The word's degree of ideal objectivity is, "one might say," only *primary*. It is *within* a facto-historical language that the name "Löwe" is free — hence ideal — *with regard to its sensible, phonetic or graphic incarnations*. But it remains essentially bound, *as a German word*, to a real spatio-temporality: it remains solidary, *in its very ideal objectivity*, with the factual existence of a given language, hence with the factual subjectivity of a certain speaking community. Its ideal objectivity is therefore *relative*, and distinguishes itself from that of the word "lion" in French *only as one empirical fact from another*. The formulation is exact and consequential: ideality admits of degrees of freedom, and the first degree frees the word from its instances while leaving it chained to its language — the German lion and the French lion are two ideal objects, not one.

Second degree. A higher level of ideal objectivity is crossed — "call it *secondary*" — as soon as one passes from the *word* to the *unity of sense* "lion": from "expression" to what the *Logical Investigations* call the "intentional content" or "*unity of signification*." The *same* content can be aimed at from several languages, and its ideal identity *secures its translatability*: the ideal identity of the sense expressed by *Lion*, *Leo*, *Löwe* is thus *liberated from all factual linguistic subjectivity*. Translation here receives its transcendental definition: not a transfer between containers but the traversal of many tongues by one identical sense. The footnote grounds the doctrine philologically and architectonically: the analyses of linguistic ideality in the *Origin*, like those of the *Logic*, directly presuppose the "subtle and indispensable" distinctions of the First and Fourth Investigations; and the notion of "intentional content" *announces*, in the sphere of language, the notion of *noematic sense* — the *Kernschicht*, nuclear stratum, of the noema — fully elaborated only in *Ideen* I §90. Just as the nuclear unity of noematic sense (which is not the reality of the object itself) can be aimed at in diverse intentional modes — the sense "tree" reached in a perception, a memory, an imagining — to constitute, with all its characters, a *complete* noema, so the ideal identity of the signification makes itself accessible to several languages and lets itself be "translated." Husserl himself, Derrida recalls, acknowledged in the 1913 Preface that the First Investigation lacked the notion of noema and of noetico-noematic correlation: the *Origin*'s linguistic stratification thus rests on the mature doctrine of the noema retrojected into the theory of meaning.

But the "*object*" itself is neither the expression nor the sense-content (the distinction, a note recalls, first analyzed with abundant examples in the First Investigation §12). The lion *in flesh and bone*, aimed at through the two layers of idealities, is a *natural, hence contingent reality*; and since the perception of the sensible thing, immediately present, *founds* the idealities in this case, the lion's contingency *reverberates upward* onto the ideality of the expression and onto that of the sense. The translatability of the word "lion" will therefore not be absolute and universal in principle: it is *empirically conditioned* by the contingent encounter, in a *receptive intuition*, of something like the lion — which is not an "intelligible objectity" but an "*object of receptivity*." (The footnote develops the distinction from *Erfahrung und Urteil* §§63–64: intelligible objectities are of a "higher level," preconstituted not in the pure passivity of sensible receptivity but in *predicative spontaneity* — "the mode of their originary pre-givenness is their *production* in the predicative act of the I"; and their temporality differs accordingly: where the real object has its individual place in objective world-time, the irreal object is "totally free" with respect to it, "intemporal" — but its *Zeitlosigkeit* or *Überzeitlichkeit* is itself only a *mode* of temporality, namely *omnitemporality*, *Allzeitlichkeit*. The point is capital and will return: ideal timelessness is not the absence of time but a way of being at all times.) The ideality of the lion's sense is thus held by an *irreducible adherence to an empirical subjectivity* — and this, Derrida specifies, would remain true *even if all human beings had in fact encountered and designated the lion*: the bond to a factual *anthropological generality* would be no less a bond. What is at stake is the distinction, "only implicit in the *Origin* but indispensable to its intelligence," between "*bound*" idealities (*idéalités enchaînées*) and "*free*" idealities (*idéalités libres*). The third footnote gives the distinction its most direct explication, from *Erfahrung und Urteil* §65, quoted at length: even cultural formations are not always totally free idealities; free idealities (logico-mathematical formations and pure essence-structures) are bound to *no territory* — "or rather, they have their territory in the *whole-of-the-world* and in every possible whole-of-a-world"; they are *omnispatial and omnitemporal with respect to their possible reactivation* — while bound idealities carry reality in their being-sense: they are *earth-bound, Mars-bound*, bound to a particular territory. And Husserl immediately adds — Derrida underlines the addition — that by their "apparition," their entry on the scene, their "discovery" in a historically determined territory, *the free idealities too are factual and worldly*: "he thereby states the crucial difficulty of his whole philosophy of history: *what is the sense of this last facticity?*" The note thus hands the Introduction its central enigma in Husserl's own terms: absolutely free ideality is nonetheless *born somewhere*.

Third degree. The ideal objectivity of geometry is *absolute*, without limit of any kind. Its ideality — *tertiary* — is no longer merely that of the expression, nor that of the intentional content: it is *the ideality of the object itself*. All adherence to real contingency is suppressed; the possibility of *translation*, "which merges with that of *tradition*," is *open to infinity*. The quotation gives Husserl's canonical statement (O., 179–80): the Pythagorean theorem, geometry entire, *exists only once*, however often and in whatever language it may be expressed; it is *identically the same* in Euclid's "original language" and in all "translations," the same again in each language, from its original oral enunciation or written notation, through innumerable oral utterances and written *consignations* (*Dokumentierungen*). Derrida's equation deserves emphasis: translation \= tradition. Because the geometrical object is nothing but the unity of its sense, its passage between languages and its passage between generations are the same operation — unlimited translatability is the linguistic name of omnitemporal traditionality. (Note too the quiet first appearance, in this quotation, of *written* documentation alongside speech — the seed of section VII.)

The phrase "*only once*," "*once for all*" — essential mode of the ideal existence of the object, thus distinguished from the multiplicity of the acts and lived experiences that relate to it — was, Derrida notes, sharply defined *in these very terms* by Herbart (the note cites the *Psychologie als Wissenschaft*) and taken up by Husserl, who acknowledged owing him much and praised him for having distinguished better than Kant between the logical and the psychological — while reproaching him, however, for having *confused ideality and normativity* (*Prolegomena* §59). The brief historical paragraph plants exactly the distinction the next paragraph will harvest: Herbart saw the *once-for-all* of the ideal, but collapsed the ideal into the valid, identity into worth.

This reproach, Derrida says, "is here very illuminating" — and there follows one of the section's great analytic paragraphs, on the ideality of the *false*. For the absolutely objective, translatable, traditional ideal identity is not *just any* geometrical objectity but the *true* objectity. Once the bound idealities of expression and sense-content are surpassed, once the ideal objectity itself is attained, one can still encounter a *factual restriction*: that of *non-value* — falsity, lapse (*péremption*). No doubt the objective sense of a *false* judgment is also ideal: as such it can be indefinitely repeated and thus become *omnitemporal*. (The footnote documents this from *Erfahrung und Urteil* §64c, where the omnitemporality of simple ideality is minutely distinguished from the omnitemporality of *value*: a judgment once true can cease to be true — "the automobile is the fastest means of transport" loses its validity in the age of aviation — yet it can be formed *again as this one identical proposition*, by anyone, at any time, in the evidence of distinctness, and it has, as *intended entity* (*Vermeintheit*, rendered after S. Bachelard's translation), "its supratemporal and irreal identity." The note adds that the *Origin* alludes even to the ideal identity of judgments not merely anachronistic in value but *contradictory and absurd in their sense-content* — analyses that announce "a phenomenology of the specific ideality of *negative values* (the false, the absurd, the evil, the ugly, etc.)" while assigning limits to their "freedom": such idealities will always remain *bound* to a determinate empirical temporality or facticity; for "what absolutely liberates and accomplishes the ideality of sense... is the ideality of *positive value*" — the evidence not merely distinct but *clear* — which alone gives sense access to infinite universality and omnitemporality.) The main text works the same material: the *anachrony of value in no way affects the uchrony or panchrony of ideality* — I can indefinitely repeat, as *the same*, the proposition about the automobile while knowing it false and lapsed. Likewise in a non-descriptive science such as geometry, *error* — whether due to the logico-deductive manipulation of symbols *emptied of their sense*, into which a sensible facticity surreptitiously re-enters, or to some psychological contingency meaningless with regard to geometrical truth — also has a content that can become ideal and omnitemporal, even though, in error or hypothesis, one has not attained the truth of the geometrical *Sachverhalt* (a note despairs gently over the "heavy, strange and inexact" but consecrated translation *état-de-choses*) and the theme of the statement remains in itself chained to facticity. The ideality of sense accommodates a truth-intention *deceived* or *inauthentically, symbolically satisfied* (the long footnote here quotes the First Investigation §11: the content "the three altitudes of a triangle intersect in a single point" is not something that appears and disappears — each utterance is a new judgment, but *what* is judged is everywhere the same, "one and the same geometrical truth"; and the same holds for all statements, *even false or absurd ones*; where "possibility" or "truth" is lacking, the statement's intention can be realized only "*symbolically*," deprived of the intuitive fullness that constitutes its cognitive value — the difference between *intentional* and *fulfilling* signification). But — the decisive turn — if the omnitemporality of non-value is possible, it is always in the sense of *empirical* possibility, contingent eventuality; and even so it is maintained in its eventuality only by a sense that *keeps an essential relation to the missed or surpassed truth-intention*. It is because I *know* that the lapsed proposition *was* true, that it remains unified and animated by an intention of truth, of authenticity, of "*clarity*" (*Klarheit* — terms in certain respects synonymous for Husserl), that I can maintain and repeat the ideal unity of its sense. An eventual *intention of absurdity* — as "non-sense" or as "counter-sense" — *in order to be what it is*, must ceaselessly signal, as if in spite of itself, toward this *telos* of authenticity, and let itself be guided symbolically by it *in the very gesture in which it claims to disorient itself*. It must, "in the *Euryclean* language of which the Stranger speaks in the *Sophist*, say the telos in order to unsay it" — like Eurycles the ventriloquist, who carried in his own belly the voice that contradicted him. The structure is exactly that of ¶70's undecidable haunted by the telos of decidability: negativity lives parasitically on the positivity it negates. Error, falsity, absurdity have ideality only on credit from truth.

This "transgression of linguistic ideality" describes, Derrida notes, a movement analogous to the one traced earlier (¶¶75–79): science was *one* of the forms of culture, but its pure possibility appeared as the pure possibility of culture only after a reduction of every factual culture; likewise here, truth is, *as* language and *like* language, one of the forms of ideal objectivity — but its pure possibility appears only through a reduction of *all language*: not only of every factual tongue, but of *the fact of language in general*. And Husserl makes this precise "in an absolutely decisive sentence" (O., 180): the idealities of geometrical *words* and *propositions*, the theories *considered purely as linguistic formations*, are *not* the idealities which, in geometry, constitute what is stated and accredited as truth — the ideal geometrical objects and states-of-affairs. In every statement, the *thematic object*, that of which one speaks (its sense), distinguishes itself from the *stating* (*l'énonciation*), which, in itself, *is not and can never be the theme* in the course of stating. And here the theme consists precisely in ideal objectities *wholly other* than those falling under the concept of language. The footnote attached to this quotation is a philological revelation of the first order: this sentence — which gives the *Origin*'s central question "its greatest and most exemplary acuity" — *was added after the fact by Husserl himself* to Fink's typed transcription of the manuscript, and *does not appear in the version published in 1939*. The distinction on which everything turns is thus a late, deliberate insertion, invisible to the *Origin*'s first readers. (At the close of an analysis of the same type, the note adds, the *Logic* §5 had said: "Expressions are not thematic *ends* but thematic *indices*." Language points to the theme; it is never the theme.)

Derrida's commentary on the decisive sentence proceeds in two steps. First: in this sentence, *the sense of the statement, the "theme" "of which one speaks," and the object itself are conflated* — something that could never happen with real objects or with *bound* ideal objectities. For the first time, with the *absolute* ideality of an object — the geometrical object, which is *through and through nothing but the unity of its true sense* — "we surpass or deliver" the ideal but still bound objectivity of language, and attain an objectivity *absolutely free with regard to every factual subjectivity*. That is why the exemplary question of the origin of objectivity could not be posed about *linguistic* ideality as such, but only about what is aimed at *through* it and *beyond* it. Second — and the section pivots on this — since the absolute ideal objectity does *not* inhabit a *topos ouranios* (the Platonic heavenly place already dismissed in ¶56), two consequences follow, which Derrida sets out as numbered theses: (1) its liberation from every *factual* subjectivity only *lays bare its de jure bonds with a transcendental subjectivity*; (2) *its historicity is intrinsic and essential*. The double consequence is the whole Introduction in two lines: stripped of empirical attachments, the geometrical object does not float free into a Platonic heaven — it stands revealed as *constituted*, and as constituted *historically*. Freedom from facts is bondage to transcendental genesis.

Section V closes by naming what has been won: thus is prescribed, "in all its enigmatic profundity, the space of a *transcendental historicity*." It is only after having determined, and prepared in its difficult specificity, *the access to this space* that Husserl can pose "the historico-transcendental question that concentrates all the disquiet of the text" — and the section ends on the *Origin*'s own formulation (O., 181): our problem now concerns precisely the ideal and thematic objectities of geometry: *how does geometrical ideality (like that of all the sciences) come to its ideal objectivity from its originary intra-personal surgissement — its upsurge — in which it presents itself as a formation within the conscious space of the first inventor's soul?* Every term of the question has now been prepared: "ideal objectivity" through the three degrees, "intra-personal upsurge" through the fiction of ¶95, "the first inventor" through the *Erstmaligkeit* analyses. The question is no longer naïve; it is asked from within the space of transcendental historicity that sections III–V have opened. Section VI will give Husserl's answer — language as the flesh of ideality — and discover in it the Introduction's "most interesting difficulty."

## **VI**

Husserl's answer to the historico-transcendental question "is direct, and it comes very quickly" — with, Derrida says, "the allure of a *vire-volte*," an about-face, "that can surprise." It is "*by the mediation of language, which procures for it, one might say, its linguistic flesh*" (*chair linguistique*) (O., 181\) that ideality comes to its objectivity. And this, Husserl notes, "*we see in advance*." The only question is therefore that of a "*Quomodo*" — a *how* — and the paragraph closes on the *Origin*'s own formulation of it (O., 181): how does *linguistic incarnation* produce, out of a purely intra-subjective formation, *the objective* — that which, as geometrical concept or state-of-affairs, is effectively present, intelligible for everyone, now and forever, being already accredited in its linguistic expression as geometrical discourse, as geometrical proposition in its ideal geometrical sense? Two terms in this answer carry the whole sequel. *Flesh*: language is not the clothing of a sense already constituted elsewhere but its incarnation — a word whose theological resonance (the Word made flesh) Derrida will let vibrate throughout sections VI and VII. And *we see in advance*: the *that* is apodictically certain before the *how* is understood; the entire analysis to come is the patient unfolding of a Quomodo whose Quod was never in doubt.

Derrida voices the astonishment the about-face invites. After having so patiently *wrested* the thematic truth of the *Sachverhalt* from linguistic ideality and from all the "bound" idealities (¶¶110–117), Husserl now seems to *descend again* toward language as the indispensable medium and condition of possibility of absolute ideal objectivity — of truth itself, which would be what it is only through its historical and intersubjective being-put-into-circulation. Does Husserl not thus *return* to the language, the culture, the history he had reduced precisely in order to let the pure possibility of truth emerge? Does he not "*bind*" anew, to induct it into history, that whose absolute "*freedom*" he had just described? And will he not then be constrained to lift, step by step, *all* the reductions, to recover at last "the *real* text of historical experience"? The objection is given maximal force because it is the hinge on which the misreading of the *Origin* turns: if the return to language were a relapse, the whole edifice of sections III–V would collapse into a vulgar sociology of knowledge. The word *texte* in the final question — the "real text of historical experience" — again quietly anticipates where the analysis is headed.

"In reality — and here, we believe, is *the most interesting difficulty of this text* — Husserl does exactly the contrary." The sentence is the keystone of section VI. This return to language, as re-entry into culture and into history in general, *carries the design of the reduction itself to its final accomplishment*. The surpassing of "bound" ideality toward the theme of truth was itself a reduction, which made appear the independence of truth from every *factual* culture and language. But — once more — what is thereby unveiled is a *juridical and transcendental dependence*. Geometrical truth indeed holds itself beyond every *particular and factual* linguistic hold as such, for which some subject speaking a determinate language and belonging to a determinate cultural community would be responsible *in fact*. But the *objectivity* of this truth *could not be constituted without the pure possibility of an information* — an in-forming, a giving-of-form — *in a pure language in general*. Without this pure and essential possibility, the geometrical formation would remain *ineffable and solitary*: it would then be *absolutely bound* — the vocabulary of ¶112 returns with its valence reversed — to the psychological life of a factual individual, of a factual community, indeed to a particular moment of that life. It would become neither omnitemporal nor intelligible for everyone: *it would not be what it is*. That geometry can be *said* is therefore not the extrinsic and accidental possibility of a *fall* into the body of speech or of a *lapsus* in the movement of a history. Speech (*la parole*) is no longer simply the expression (*\-Äusserung*) of what, without it, would already be an object: "recaptured in its originary purity, *it constitutes the object*"; it is a *concrete juridical condition of truth*. The paradox, Derrida concludes, is that without what *appears* as a relapse into language — and thereby into history — a relapse that would alienate the ideal purity of sense, sense would remain an empirical formation, *imprisoned as a fact in a psychological subjectivity, in the inventor's head*. "Instead of chaining it, *historical incarnation delivers the transcendental*. This last notion must therefore be rethought." The footnote performs the same reversal on temporality itself: omnitemporality and universal intelligibility, though concrete and lived as such, are *only the reduction* of factual historical temporality and geographical spatiality; *Überzeitlichkeit* and *Zeitlosigkeit* are defined in their transcendence or negativity *only relative to mundane temporality*; once the latter is reduced, they appear as *Allzeitlichkeit* — omnitemporality, "*a concrete mode of temporality in general*." Supratemporality is thus not an escape from time but time's own universal mode — just as linguistic embodiment is not ideality's prison but its deliverance. The chiasmus *incarnation délivre le transcendantal* states the Introduction's profoundest claim about Husserl, and the closing imperative — the transcendental "must be rethought" — announces, beyond the *Origin*, a program: a transcendental that *needs* flesh, language, history is no longer the transcendental of Kant, and perhaps no longer quite Husserl's either.

Is this "ultimate reduction," which thus opens onto a *transcendental language*, "revolutionary within Husserlian thought"? Does this return to the *speaking subject* as the subject constituting the ideal object — and hence absolute objectivity — *belie* an earlier philosophy of language? Merleau-Ponty spoke of a "*striking contrast*" between the *Origin* and the *Logical Investigations* (Derrida's note cites "Sur la phénoménologie du langage" and the lectures on the human sciences, reprinted in *Signes*). Derrida's answer is a measured no. Granted, the *Investigations* were more interested in what corresponds only to a *first phase* of description in the *Origin*: the autonomy of *constituted* ideal objects with regard to a language *itself constituted*. But what was at stake there, above all, in reaction against a subjectivist psychologism, was to dissociate the ideal object from every empirical subjectivity and every empirical tongue, which could indeed only "*blur*" (*brouiller*) the transparent, univocal, objective significations of a pure logic. The return to the originarity of the speaking subject is *no more* in contrast with this first approach to language than the "idealism" of *Ideen* I was — as was once believed — with the apparent "logicism" or "realism" of the *Investigations*. It is simply a matter of *bracketing constituted language* — which Husserl continues to do in the *Logic* and in the *Origin* — in order to let the originality of *constituting language* be discovered later. The footnote on "transcendental language" makes a distinction of real consequence: the expression as Derrida uses it does *not* have the sense of Fink's "*transcendental discourse*" (the discourse adapted to transcendental description, evoked in the great footnote of ¶109). Derrida speaks of transcendental language insofar as, on the one hand, it is *constituting* with regard to ideal objectivity, and, on the other, in its pure possibility it *merges with no factual empirical tongue*. Fink's problem concerned the language *of* phenomenology; Derrida's concerns language as a transcendental *operator* — the linguistic condition of objectivity itself. The two problems communicate, but they are not the same, and the Introduction holds them carefully apart.

What does constituting language *do*? "To constitute an ideal object is to put it at the *permanent disposition of a pure regard*." Now, *before* being the constituted and surpassed auxiliary of an act that moves toward the truth of sense, linguistic ideality is *the milieu in which the ideal object deposits itself* — sediments, or is *put on deposit*. And here Derrida's economic-juridical figures attain their full precision: the act of *originary consignation* (*consignation originaire* — registration, but also entrusting to a depository) is *not the recording of a private thing*; it is *the production of a common object* — "that is, of an object whose *original proprietor is thereby dispossessed*." Language then *guards* the truth, so that it can be regarded "in the henceforth *non-ephemeral lighting of its sojourn*" — but also *so that it can grow there*. For there would be no truth without this *thésaurisation* (treasuring-up), which is not only what consigns and retains truth but also that *without which a project of truth and the idea of an infinite task would be unimaginable*. That is why language is *the element of tradition*, in which alone, *beyond individual finitude*, the *retention and protention of sense* are possible. The paragraph gathers and fuses nearly every prior motif: the gaze and availability of ¶94; the capitalization and treasure of ¶¶74, 79; the surpassing of retentional finitude of ¶78 — and adds the decisive new note of *dispossession*. Truth begins when the inventor *loses* his property: publication is expropriation. The first proprietor must be dispossessed for there to be an object at all — a thought that already contains in germ everything Derrida will later say about writing as the structural absence of the author.

Against Merleau-Ponty's "contrast," Derrida now documents continuity. There is *so little* discontinuity between Husserl's first and last thought that in the *Logical Investigations* one finds, on the essential function of "*Dokumentierung*," on the "*spiritual corporeality*" of language, and on the statement as accomplishment of the truth-intention, "pages that could be inscribed *without modification* in the *Origin*." The footnote quotes one at length (Introduction to volume II, §2): every theoretical investigation, though it in no way unfolds simply in acts of expression or in complete statements, nevertheless *terminates in statements*; "it is only in this form that truth, and especially theory, become *the permanent property of science*, *the treasure constituted by the sum of documents*, always available, of knowledge and of research open to constant progress"; and whether or not the linkage of thought and language is necessary for essential reasons, it is in any case certain that judgments of the higher intellectual sphere, especially the scientific sphere, "*can hardly be effected without being expressed in a language*." This is even more true, Derrida adds, of the *Logic* (§§1–5) and the *Cartesian Meditations* (§4). Each time, Husserl begins by *extirpating* thought from what it would be "simply in acts of expression," only to specify *afterward* that it could not become "truth" without that "*exposition*" and that "*communication to others*" of which the *Investigations* already spoke. The philological point is polemically decisive: the 1900 Husserl already possessed the doctrine of the document and the treasure; the *Origin* radicalizes, it does not recant. The pattern — first dissociate thought from language, then restore language as truth's condition — is not an evolution between books but the constant internal rhythm of each.

The deeper identity of the question is now named. To recognize in language *what constitutes* absolute ideal objectivity, as much as it expresses it — is this not another way of announcing, or repeating, that *transcendental intersubjectivity is the condition of objectivity*? At bottom, the problem of the origin of geometry brings to the surface (*fait affleurer*) the problem of the *constitution of intersubjectivity* and that of the *phenomenological origin of language*. Husserl is quite conscious of this — the footnote quotes the *Logic* §73, where, concerning the "idealizing presuppositions of logic," Husserl announced that the constitutive problem "widens anew" once one recalls that *verbal expression* — excluded from the logical considerations — "is an essential presupposition for an intersubjective activity of thought and for an intersubjectivity of theory whose existence is valid *idealiter*," so that the ideal possibility of identifying the expression *as* expression must itself carry a constitutive problem. But Husserl *will not attempt*, in the *Origin*, this difficult regression, although, he says, it "*announces itself here*" (O., 181). For the moment it suffices to know — if not *how*, at least *that* — language and the *consciousness of co-humanity* (*co-humanité*) are solidary possibilities, already given at the moment when the possibility of science is instituted. The horizon of co-humanity *presupposes the horizon of world*: it detaches itself from, and articulates its unity upon, the unity of the world. And of course, world and co-humanity designate here the *one but infinitely open totality of possible experiences* — not *this* world and *this* co-humanity, whose facticity is never, for Husserl, more than a variable example. The consciousness of *being-in-community in one and the same world* founds the possibility of a *universal language*: humanity first becomes conscious of itself "*as an immediate or mediate community of language*" (O., 182). The paragraph thus installs the chain that the three numbered points will examine: world-horizon → co-humanity → language-community → possibility of science.

A single-sentence unit of pure articulation: "We must, in this regard, note three important points." As at ¶¶11–13, Derrida signals that what follows is not a digression but a structured series of reservations — and these three points, on normality, translatability, and the object, contain some of the Introduction's most original and most personal pages.

First point: within the horizon of this consciousness of co-humanity, it is "*normal and adult*" humanity that is "privileged" — "both *as horizon of humanity and as language-community*." The theme of adult normality, which occupied an ever larger place in Husserl's analyses, is here, Derrida notes, "treated as going without saying." He declines to dwell on it ("we propose to return to it elsewhere," says a footnote — a promissory note Derrida's later work on madness and reason may be said to honor), *despite the gravity of the problems* it seems bound to pose for a transcendental philosophy: how can *maturity* and *normality* give rise to a rigorous eidetic-transcendental determination? Could adult normality ever be considered otherwise than as an *empirical and factual modification* of universal transcendental norms — in the *classical* sense — under which those other empirical "cases," *madness and childhood*, would continue to fall? But it is precisely this classical notion of the "transcendental," Derrida answers, that Husserl has *overturned* — "to the point of giving a sense to the idea of a *transcendental pathology*." The footnote supporting this astonishing phrase reads the *Crisis of European Humanity*: there the phenomenon of crisis is presented as an "*illness*" of European society and culture — an illness that is *not* "natural" and falls under no "natural art of healing"; and this "pathology" has the profound *ethical* sense of a fall into "*passivity*," an incapacity to make oneself "*responsible*" for sense in an authentic activity or "reactivation" — so that technical activity, *that of science included*, is as such "a passivity with regard to sense; it is *the agitation of the sick and, already, the trembling of a delirium*." (Sedimented, unreactivated science as delirium: the diagnosis could hardly be more violent, and Derrida lets Husserl's own metaphors carry it.) The main text then resolves the apparent scandal of the "privilege": this notion denotes "the *pré-ingérence* of a *telos* in the *eidos*" — the pre-interference, the anticipatory intrusion, of the goal within the essence. To accede to the eidos of humanity and language, certain humans and speaking subjects — the mad and children — are not good examples: first, no doubt, because they have no rigorously determinable pure essence *of their own*. But if that is so, does *adult normality* have one — it which begins where childhood ends and ceases where madness is born? The answer: "adult normality" is here *not a given eidetic determination* but "the *index of an ideal normativity* that is on the horizon of factually normal adults." And the general law follows, one of the Introduction's great theoretical statements: "*as one rises in the world of spirit, and hence in that of history, the eidos ceases to be an essence in order to become a norm, and the concept of horizon progressively substitutes itself for that of structure and essence*." Spirit has no fixed essence to intuit; it has a telos to pursue — so that the eidetics of history, announced since ¶99, must be an eidetics of horizons and norms, not of static structures. The exclusion of the mad and the child is thereby explained, though hardly neutralized: they fall outside not an essence but a teleology — a displacement whose stakes Derrida registers without yet contesting.

Second point — the longest and most consequential: the possibility of a universal language-horizon "risks encountering essential difficulties and limits." It presupposes, *first*, as resolved, the perilous problem of the possibility of a "*pure grammar*" and of "*a priori norms*" of language — a possibility, Derrida notes with an edge, "that never ceased to go without saying for Husserl" (the footnote points to the Fourth Investigation and to Suzanne Bachelard's pages on Husserl's fidelity to this theme and the philosophical option orienting it). It presupposes, *second*, that "*everything is nameable* in a very broad sense, that is, expressible in a language," and that "everyone can also speak, as of an objective being, of everything that is there in the surrounding world of his humanity" (O., 183). In other words: however heterogeneous the essential structures of several constituted languages or cultures, *translation is in principle an always possible task*: two *normal* men will always have, *a priori*, consciousness of their common belonging to one and the same humanity, inhabiting one and the same world. (The footnote attached to this *a priori* is a small masterpiece of reservation: "*they must still meet*. It is therefore a question here only of a *material* a priori — hence, in a certain sense, a *contingent* one." The universality of translation hangs on the contingency of an encounter.) The differences of language — and what they imply — will appear to the two speakers *against the ground* of an a priori horizon or structure: the community of language, that is, the immediate certainty of being, both of them, speaking subjects who can only ever designate things belonging to the horizon of their world as the irreducibly *common* horizon of their experience. Which implies that they can always, immediately or not, find themselves together *before the same natural being*, which one will always have been able to *strip* of the cultural superstructures and categories founded (*fundiert*) upon it — and whose unity would always furnish "*the ultimate arbitral instance of every misunderstanding*." The consciousness of being before *the same thing*, an object perceived *as such*, is the consciousness of a *pure and pre-cultural we*.
