(The footnote on "as such" is of great phenomenological delicacy: it is the "as such" of the substantial and objective unity of the object that is decisive, distinguishing *human* intersubjectivity from the communities formed among animals, between men and animals, among children — finite communities that also rest on the feeling of presence to one same world, before the same things, but in a *non-objective, non-theoretical* consciousness that does not posit the object "as such," in its independence, as pole of infinite determination. These "inferior communities," Derrida notes, can also give rise to a specific phenomenology, to which important unpublished fragments of Husserl are devoted.) And Derrida is careful to specify the *sense* of this return to pre-culture: it is *not* a regression toward cultural primitivity but *the reduction of a determinate culture* — "a theoretical operation that is one of the highest forms of culture in general." The purely natural objective being is the being of the *sensible world*, which becomes "the first foundation of communication, *the permanent chance of a reinvention of language*." And since the most universal element, the most objectively exposed before us, the one furnishing the raw material of every sensible object, is *the earth itself* as *exemplary element* — naturally more objective, more permanent, more solid, more rigid than the other elements, and in a broader sense *comprehending* them — it is normal that the earth should have furnished *the soil of the first idealities*, hence of the first absolutely universal and objective identities: those of *calculation and geometry*. The paragraph thus closes the immense arc from the pure we to the Greek origin: arithmetic and geometry are born of the earth, not as Ionian mythology but as transcendental pedigree — the most solid stratum of the sensible world grounding the first idealities to be detached from it. (The earth as "exemplary element" and universal soil here makes its first major appearance; it will return at the Introduction's center when Derrida reads the famous fragment on the earth that does not move.)

But — the objection comes at once, and it is Derrida's own — "pure pre-cultural nature is *always already buried*" (*toujours déjà enfouie*). As the ultimate possibility of communication, it is therefore "a sort of *inaccessible infra-ideal*." The phrase is built to disturb: the pre-cultural ground, which should be the most accessible thing in the world — bare nature beneath every cultural sediment — has the mode of being of an *ideal*, an infinite pole, and can never be *given*. Whence the question: "Can one not then say *the contrary* of what Husserl says? Are not *non-communication and misunderstanding* the very *horizon* of culture and language?" Derrida immediately grants the Husserlian rejoinder its full strength: no doubt misunderstanding is always a *factual* horizon and "the finite index of a good understanding as infinite pole"; the *malentendu* presupposes, in its very sense, the *bonne intelligence* it falls short of — the structure of ¶115 again, negativity living on the telos it negates. But, the paragraph ends, "although the latter always announces itself in order that language may begin, is not the *finitude* essential which can never be *radically surpassed*?" The "always already" (*toujours déjà*) — making here one of its earliest load-bearing appearances in Derrida's corpus — does its characteristic work: if the arbitral instance of pure nature is *always already* buried, then the appeal procedure it was to ground never closes, and an essential finitude inhabits the infinite task of translation. Husserl is not refuted; he is shown to have an *underside* that his own descriptions generate.

The reservation deepens along a second line. Absolute translatability "would be *suspended* from the moment the *signified* could no longer be led back, directly or indirectly, to the model of an *objective and sensible being*." All the dimensions of a language escaping this absolute translatability would remain marked by the *empirical subjectivity* of an individual or a society. The model of language, for Husserl, is *the objective language of science*: "a *poetic* language, whose significations would not be *objects*, will never have, in his eyes, any transcendental value." And this restriction, Derrida observes, would be without consequence inside Husserlian thought — *if* that thought were not *also* "the deepening of subjectivity." Now it is *subjectivity in general* — empirical *and* transcendental — that very early appeared to Husserl as *inaccessible to a direct, univocal and rigorous language*: "*fundamental subjectivity is ineffable*." Already in the lectures on internal time-consciousness, alluding to the ultimate identity of the constitutive flux of immanent time and absolute subjectivity, Husserl concluded: "*for all that, names fail us*" (§36). (The footnote adds the *Investigations*' subtle analyses of expressions "deprived of objective sense," such as the personal pronouns, which "indicate" mediately but can never *give to see*: "The word *I* does not in itself have the power of directly awakening the particular representation of the self that determines its signification in the given discourse. It does not act like the word *lion*, which can by itself awaken the representation of the lion. Rather, an *indicative* function serves in it as mediation, warning the hearer, as it were: the one facing you intends himself" — First Investigation §26. The lion returns, now as the counter-example to the *I*: the word that shows versus the word that only points — a distinction on which *La voix et le phénomène* will build an entire chapter.) And in the unpublished C-manuscripts on proto-temporality, Husserl asks whether pre-objective temporality, the *Vorzeit*, is not *beyond all discourse* (*unsagbar* — unsayable) for the "*phenomenologizing I*" (C 13/15-II, 1934). The conclusion is lapidary and double-edged: "*There is therefore language, tradition and history only in the measure that objects come to the surface*" (*font surface*). Everything that does not objectify itself — the depths of constituting subjectivity, the living flux, the poetic — remains beneath the surface, outside history. The sentence states Husserl's position with perfect fidelity and, by its very perfection, exposes the cost: the transcendental source of all objectivity is itself condemned to silence by the objectivist concept of language that it grounds.

Third point. The world, as infinite horizon of every possible experience, is therefore "*the universum of objects, insofar as it is expressible according to its being and its being-such*." Thus is clearly explicated the signification of the world as *horizon*: as the *common place*, open to infinity, of everything that can come to meet us, *before us and for us*. "Before us and for us — hence *as given object*." The world is therefore principially determined by "the *dative and horizontal* dimension of being-perceived *in a regard* whose object must always be able to be a *theorem*." The formula compresses the whole third point: *dative* — the world is given *to*; *horizontal* — it is given within a horizon; and the regard to which it is given is one for which every object is in principle theorizable, a possible theorem. Geometry's exemplarity, Derrida continues, doubtless holds to the fact that, as an "*abstract*" material science, it treats *the spatiality of bodies* (which is only one of the body's eidetic components) — "that is, of *what gives its sense to the notion of horizon and of object*." Space is not one theme among others: it is the very dimension of horizonality and objectivity. And then the diagnosis widens to phenomenology as a whole: despite all the antagonistic motifs animating it, "the *privilege of space* is in certain respects remarkable" in phenomenology. It bears witness there to that "*objectivist*" tendency that Husserl *simultaneously combats with such vigor* — and which is "only a period, an *essential*, hence irreducible, *movement* of thought." This tension between *objectivism* and the *transcendental motif*, so remarkably described in the *Krisis*, "also imprints on phenomenology its deep rhythm." The problem of geometry is in this respect *revelatory*: phenomenology fights objectivism with one hand while its own concept of world, of language, of sense — the object for a regard (¶94), the nameable universe, the theorem — works objectivistically with the other. Derrida here generalizes to Husserl's thought the very structure Husserl diagnosed in European history: the struggle between objectivism and transcendentalism is not an external accident befalling phenomenology but its internal pulse.

The third point culminates in the Introduction's first sustained meditation on the Earth. Geometry is the science of what is *absolutely objective* — spatiality — in the objects that *the Earth, our common place*, can indefinitely furnish as "ground of agreement" (*terrain d'entente*) with other men. (A footnote refers to *Erfahrung und Urteil* §38 on "our Earth" as "life-world in the broad sense" for a humanity living in community, where one can "come to an understanding" only in a communication that must always pass through the things of our Earth — a paragraph, Derrida notes, of the same inspiration as the famous fragment about to be cited, and which illuminates it by its higher degree of elaboration: there the unity of the Earth is founded in the unity and unicity of *temporality*, "fundamental form," "form of all forms.") But — and here is the paradox the paragraph exists to state — if an objective science of *terrestrial things* is possible, an objective science of *the Earth itself*, ground and foundation of these objects, is "as radically impossible as that of transcendental subjectivity." *The transcendental Earth is not an object and can never become one*; the possibility of a geometry is "rigorously complementary" to the impossibility of what one might call a "*geo-logy*," an objective science of the Earth itself. This, Derrida says, is the sense of the fragment that "*reduces* rather than 'refutes' the Copernican naïveté" and shows that the Earth, in its archi-originarity, *does not move*. The footnotes here amount to a small essay on Husserl's May 1934 fragment, "Foundational Investigations on the Phenomenological Origin of the Spatiality of Nature" (published by Farber in 1940), and they must be incorporated, since Derrida's entire later relation to this text — down to the title of his first book project on Husserl — is decided here. The fragment, "very spontaneous and very little elaborated in its redaction," is presented as preface to a "science of the origin of spatiality," of "corporeality," of "nature in the sense of the natural sciences," and to a "transcendental theory of natural-scientific knowledge." Husserl there opposes to every determinate world-representation — that of "the Blacks," that of "the Greeks" — the Copernican one: "We Copernicans, we men of modern times, say: the earth is not 'the whole of nature'; it is one of the planets in the infinite space of the world. The earth is a globe-shaped body which, certainly, is not perceivable in its totality by one person and at one stroke, but in a primordial synthesis, as the unity of singular experiences enchained one to another. *But it is no less a body\!* — even though it is for us the *experiential ground* for all bodies in the experiential genesis of our world-representation." Husserl then "*reduces*" the Copernican thesis by making appear, as its transcendental presupposition, the certainty of an Earth as *origin of every objective kinetic determination*: "it is a matter of *exhuming the Earth*, of laying bare the originary ground *buried under the sedimentary deposits of scientific culture and objectivism*" — the *Origin*'s archaeology of geometry repeated as an archaeology of the ground itself. For the Earth cannot become a mobile body: "It is *on* the Earth, *toward* the Earth, *from* it, but still *on* it, that movement takes place. The Earth itself, in the originary form of representation, *neither moves nor is at rest*; it is in relation to it that movement and rest first take on sense. Only *afterward* does the Earth 'move' or rest — and the same holds entirely for the stars, and for the earth insofar as it is one of the stars." The Earth is the last foundation of our *Mitmenschheit* — for all it is "the same Earth," bearing the same bodies and the same corporeal subjects (*leiblichen* subjects, subjects of *Leiber*, which for everyone are *Körper* in a modified sense): "but for us all, the Earth is *ground* (*sol*) and not *body* in the full sense." Toward the fragment's end, Derrida notes, the Earth takes on a *more formal* sense: no longer *this* Earth (an originary here whose facticity would be at last irreducible) but a *here* and a *ground in general* for the determination of object-bodies in general. If I reached another planet in flight and could perceive the earth as a body, I would have "two Earths as ground-bodies. But what does that mean, two Earths? *Two pieces of one single Earth with one single humanity*." From then on it is *the unity of total humanity* that determines the unity of the ground as such — a unity correlative of the unity of the *world* as infinite horizon of experience, not of *this* earth. The World, which is not the facticity of this historical world, is "the ground of grounds, the horizon of horizons," and *to it* returns the transcendental immutability attributed to the Earth, which is then only its *factual index*; correlatively, humanity would be only the *facto-anthropological index* of subjectivity and intersubjectivity in general, from which every originary here can appear — on the foundation of the *Living Present*, "absolute rest and maintenance of the origin in which, by which and for which all temporality and all movement appear." And just as he reduces the Copernican "relativity" of the earth, Husserl elsewhere reduces the Einsteinian: Derrida quotes the *Crisis of European Humanity* (Ricœur's translation) — Einstein's upheavals bear on the formulas that treat the idealized and naively objectivized *Physis*; but we are not taught how formulas in general, how mathematical objectivation in general, *take on sense upon the substructure of life and the intuitive environment*: "thus Einstein does not reform the space and time in which our living life (*unser lebendiges Leben*) unfolds." (A second note quietly corrects Tran-Duc-Thao, who had spoken of an "intrepid refutation of the Copernican system": Husserl contests at no moment the proper truth, at its proper level, of objective Copernican science; he only recalls that it *presupposes* an originary Earth "that it will never be able to integrate into its objective system.") The main text then unfolds the analogy that organizes everything: just as the *corps propre*, the lived body, as originary *here* and *zero-point* of every objective determination of space and movement, is not itself in movement within that space as an object, so — analogically — the Earth, as originary body, *ground-body* (*Bodenkörper*), on whose basis alone a Copernican determination of the earth as object-body becomes possible, *is not itself one body among others* in the mechanical system. Originarily it no more moves than our body moves or quits "the permanence of its here, founded in a present." The Earth thus knows "the rest of an *absolute here*" — a rest that is *not* the rest of an object, not rest as a "mode of movement," but "*the Rest on the basis of which movement and rest can appear and be thought as such*": the rest of a ground and a horizon "in their common origin and end." For the Earth is at once *on this side of* and *beyond* every object-body: it *precedes* every object-body — in particular the Copernican earth — as the ground, the here, of its relative appearing; and it *exceeds* every object-body as its infinite horizon, "for it is never exhausted by the work of objectivation that proceeds upon it." Husserl's own sentence seals it: "*The Earth is a Whole whose parts... are bodies, but as a Whole it is not a body.*" Whence the closing aphorism, among the most beautiful in the Introduction: "*There is therefore a science of space in the measure that its point of departure is not in space.*" Geometry is possible only because its ground is not geometrical; the condition of objectification cannot itself be objectified — the Earth standing to geometry exactly as transcendental subjectivity stands to psychology, two names for the same structural withdrawal of the origin from the field it opens.

The thread of section VI's main argument is now retied. If the possibility of language is *already given* to the proto-founding geometer, it suffices that he should have produced *in himself* the ideal identity and permanence of an object in order to be able to communicate it. *Before* taking place between several individuals, recognition and communication of the "same" take place *within individual consciousness*: after the living and transitory evidence, after the vanishing of a finite and passive retention, the sense can be *re-produced as the "same"* in the activity of *recollection* (*ressouvenir*); "it has not returned to nothingness." It is in this *recovery of identity* (*recouvrement d'identité*) that ideality *as such and in general* first announces itself, in an egological subject; only later will one ask what makes this ideality a *geometrical* ideality — the order of description will be respected, and in the interval the conditions of ideality in an intersubjective community will have been defined. The footnote attached to "not returned to nothingness" is a deep methodological aside: this passage from passive retention to active recollection — the passage that "produces" ideality and pure objectivity as such — is *always described by Husserl as an essential possibility already given*, "a structural power whose source is not problematized." And perhaps, Derrida adds, it is *not problematizable by a phenomenology*, "for it merges with the possibility of phenomenology itself" — phenomenology lives entirely on the power of reactivating the same, and so cannot get behind it. In its "facticity," moreover, this passage is also the passage from the inferior forms of nature and life *to consciousness*; it can thus become the thematic site of what is today called a "*dépassement*," an overcoming — here phenomenology would be "surpassed," or accomplished, in an *interpretative philosophy*. It is thus, the note concludes, that Tran-Duc-Thao, after a remarkable interpretation of phenomenology, expounds the "dialectic of real movement" on the basis of the concepts of retention and reproduction "and of the difficulties attached to them in a phenomenology which alone, however, can give them a rigorous sense." The double-edged tribute defines Derrida's position between phenomenology and dialectical materialism: the Marxist overcoming borrows its concepts from what it overcomes.

Section VI closes on a paragraph whose few lines contain one of Derrida's most consequential theses. Before being the ideality of an identical object *for other subjects*, sense is such *for other moments of the same subject*: intersubjectivity is therefore *first*, "in a certain way," *the non-empirical relation of me to me* — of my actual present to *other presents as such*, that is, *as other and as presents* (as past presents); "of an absolute origin to other absolute origins, *which are always mine* despite their radical alterity." It is thanks to this *circulation of originary absolutes* that the same thing can be thought through absolutely other moments and acts. One always returns, Derrida writes, to the last instance: *the unique and essential form of temporalization*. By its very *dialecticity*, the absolute originarity of the Living Present permits "the reduction, *without negation*, of all alterity; it constitutes the other as other *in itself* and the same as same *in the other*." Temporalization is thus proto-intersubjectivity: the structure by which I am already other to myself — my past present an absolute origin both mine and irreducibly other — is the root of the possibility of the alter ego. The supporting footnote assembles the dossier. In the *Cartesian Meditations*, this dialectic of temporalization is invoked only as an *analogical example* — an "instructive comparison" (§52) — to illuminate the extraordinary constitution of "another monad in mine." But in the unpublished manuscripts Husserl "seems to go further": the *Urhyle*, the temporal hyle, is there defined as the "*kernel of the foreign-to-the-I*" (*ichfremde Kern* — C 6, August 1930); on this "first-foreign-in-itself" or "first non-I" in the constitution of the alter ego, see *Meditations* §§48–49. And pre-objective, pre-exact temporality — which was to become the principal theme of the *transcendental aesthetic* Husserl projected (*Logic* conclusion; *Meditations* §61) — is therefore "the *root* of transcendental intersubjectivity": it is in the identity of the concrete and universal form of the Living Present that all egos can, beyond every possible difference, "meet, recognize one another, and understand one another" (*Erfahrung und Urteil* §38: time as form of sensibility, "foundation" of the necessary connection of the intentional objects of all the perceptions and positional presentifications of an I *and of a community of I's*). The architecture is now complete and the chiasmus with ¶78 closed: tradition was modeled on time-consciousness; now intersubjectivity itself is rooted in temporalization, whose "dialecticity" — alteration without negation, the other constituted in the same — is the single form underlying ego, community, language, and history. When *La voix et le phénomène* later argues that this auto-affection of the Living Present is already a *hetero*\-affection, it will be radicalizing exactly the sentence with which this section ends.

## **VII**

"A decisive gesture remains to be made." Section VII opens by measuring the insufficiency of everything section VI established. By himself, the *speaking* subject, in the narrow sense of the term, is *incapable of absolutely founding the ideal objectivity of sense*. Oral communication — that is, *actual, immediate, synchronic* communication among the proto-geometers — does not suffice to give ideal objectities their "*being-in-perpetuity*" and "*perduring presence*," thanks to which they "persist even in the times when the inventor and his associates are no longer awake to such an exchange, or in general are no longer alive." For the object to be *absolutely* ideal, it still lacks liberation from *every* bond with an *actual subjectivity in general* — it must remain "*even if no one has effectuated it in evidence*" (O., 185–6). Oral language freed the object from *individual* subjectivity; "but it leaves it *bound* to its beginning and to the synchrony of an exchange *within the instituting community*." The gradations of bondage and freedom established in section V (¶¶110–113) here receive their final application: speech itself, the great liberator, turns out to be one more *bound* medium — bound to the living presence of a generation of speakers. So long as truth depends on someone, anyone, being alive to think it, it has not yet won the omnitemporality that is its very sense.

The answer is named without delay: "It is the possibility of *writing* (*l'écriture*) that will assure the absolute traditionalization of the object — its absolute ideal objectivity, that is, *the purity of its relation to a universal transcendental subjectivity*." Writing will do this "by emancipating sense from its *actual* evidence for a *real* subject and from its *actual* circulation within a *determinate* community." The sentence should be slowed down, because each clause performs a reversal of the millennial hierarchy of speech and writing. Absolute ideality — the freest, most spiritual accomplishment of sense — is achieved not despite but *through* the most external, most material, most "dead" of linguistic media; and the purity of the object's relation to *transcendental* subjectivity (rather than to any actual one) is purchased precisely by its consignment to paper, where no actual subject need attend it. Writing emancipates by absenting.

A standalone quotation from the *Origin* (O., 186\) gives Husserl's own formulation: "It is the decisive function of *written* linguistic expression, of *consigning* expression, to render communications possible *without personal allocution*, mediate or immediate, and to have become, so to speak, *communication on the virtual mode* (*virtuel*)." The two negations and the one modality define writing exactly: no addresser present, no addressee addressed — communication subsisting as pure *virtuality*, a saying that no one is currently saying to no one in particular, and which is for that very reason sayable by anyone, always.

A brief paragraph that drops a long shadow: this *virtuality*, Derrida observes, "is moreover an *ambiguous* value: it renders possible at the same stroke (*du même coup*) passivity, forgetting, and all the phenomena of crisis." The single sentence is the hinge of the entire Introduction. The same virtualization that perfects ideality — sense available without any actual consciousness — is what permits sense to circulate *without* being reactivated: to be handled, transmitted, built upon in the very emptiness of its sedimented forms. Writing is at once the condition of the infinite tradition of truth *and* the condition of its forgetting; the pharmakon-structure that Derrida will analyze a decade later in Plato is here read off from Husserl's own text, where the remedy for finitude and the source of crisis are demonstrably one and the same possibility.

Derrida now repeats, for writing, the reversal performed in ¶121 for speech. Far from making a truth that had been won *against* real history *fall back* into it, "scriptural spatio-temporality — whose originality will have to be determined — *achieves and consecrates the existence of a pure transcendental historicity*." Without the ultimate objectivation that writing permits, *all* language would remain captive of the *factual and actual* intentionality of a speaking subject or community of speaking subjects. "By *absolutely virtualizing* the dialogue, writing creates a sort of *autonomous transcendental field from which every actual subject can absent itself*." The last sentence is one of the most quoted in the Introduction, and rightly: it defines writing not as a region of the world but as a *transcendental field* — the structured space of sense itself — whose distinguishing feature is that it functions in the *absence* of any actual subjectivity. Presence is no longer required; absence has become an enabling structure. Everything Derrida will later say about the text, the trace, and iterability is the patient elaboration of this sentence.

The formula calls up a contemporary echo. Apropos of the general signification of the *epoché*, Jean Hyppolite had evoked the possibility of a "*transcendental field without subject*," in which "the conditions of subjectivity would appear, and where the subject would be *constituted on the basis of* the transcendental field" (the note locates the remark in Hyppolite's intervention after Van Breda's Royaumont lecture on the reduction). It is certain, Derrida comments, that writing — "as the place of absolutely permanent ideal objectities, hence of absolute objectivity" — *constitutes such a transcendental field*; and that it is *on the basis of this field, or of its possibility*, that transcendental subjectivity "can fully announce itself and appear to itself." Writing is indeed in this way a "*condition*" of subjectivity. The inversion could not be more pointed: the transcendental subject, classically the constitutor of all fields, is here said to constitute *itself* from a field — the scriptural archive — that precedes and exceeds every actual subject. Derrida endorses the Hyppolitean formula, but the quotation marks around "condition" signal that its sense is about to be carefully delimited.

The delimitation follows at once, and it is rigorous. *All of this can be said only* on the basis of an *intentional analysis* — one that retains of writing only "the pure relation to a consciousness that founds it as such, and not its facticity, which, abandoned to itself, is *totally insignificant*" (*insignifiante*: both meaningless and sign-less). For the absence of subjectivity from the transcendental field — the absence whose possibility liberates absolute objectivity — "can only be a *factual* absence, even if it removed forever the totality of real subjects." The field of writing has as its originality the power to *dispense*, in its sense, with every *actual* reading in general; but — and here the paragraph turns into one of the most extraordinary passages Derrida ever wrote — *without the pure juridical possibility of being intelligible for a transcendental subject in general*, without the pure relation of dependence on *a writer and a reader in general* announcing itself in the text, "if a *virtual intentionality* does not *haunt* it, then, in the *vacancy of its soul*, it is no more than a *chaotic literality*, the sensible opacity of a *defunct designation*" — a designation deprived of its transcendental function. And the examples are unforgettable: "the silence of prehistoric arcana and buried civilizations, the entombment of lost intentions and guarded secrets, the *illegibility of the lapidary inscription*" — these disclose "*the transcendental sense of death*: in that which unites it to the absolute of intentional right *in the very instance of its failure*." The structure is exquisitely balanced. Against any empiricism of the document: marks without the haunting of virtual intentionality are not even *dead* language, merely opaque matter; the graphic body is sign only insofar as a possible consciousness in general can reanimate it. Against any idealism of presence: this animating intentionality is *virtual*, never required to be actual — so that writing is essentially exposed to the death of every reader and every writer, and this exposure belongs to its sense. Death is thereby drawn *inside* the transcendental: the possibility of total extinction of actual subjects is the negative face of the juridical "for everyone, always" — truth's omnitemporality and the silence of dead civilizations are revealed in the same structure. *La voix et le phénomène*'s thesis that the relation to my death is the condition of the sign's functioning is, almost verbatim, the radicalization of this paragraph.

It is "in thinking of the *juridical purity* of this intentional animation," Derrida writes, that Husserl always says of the linguistic or graphic body that it is *a flesh* — *un corps propre* (*Leib*) — or a *spiritual corporeality* (*geistige Leiblichkeit*; the note cites *Logic* §2). The terminological point is decisive: the sign's body is never, for Husserl, a *Körper*, a mere physical thing, but a *Leib*, a lived-animated body — body inhabited by intention as my flesh is inhabited by my life. From this it follows that writing is "no longer only the worldly and *mnemotechnical* auxiliary of a truth whose sense of being would in itself dispense with all consignation." Not only is the possibility or necessity of being incarnated in a graphie no longer *extrinsic and factual* with regard to ideal objectivity: "it is the condition *sine qua non* of ideal objectivity's *internal* achievement." So long as truth is not *engraved* in the world — "or rather, so long as it *cannot* be" — so long as it is not able to lend itself to an incarnation which, in the purity of its sense, is *more than a signaling or a garment*, ideal objectivity is *not fully constituted*. Whence the sentence that crowns the whole analysis: "*The act of writing is therefore the highest possibility of all 'constitution.'*" And it is against this, Derrida adds, that "the transcendental depth of its historicity is measured." Every word of the tradition that subordinated writing — mnemotechnique, auxiliary, clothing, signal — is enumerated and revoked. Note the precision of the modal correction ("or rather, so long as it cannot be"): it is *incorporability*, not factual incorporation, that belongs to truth's essence — the same shift from act to possibility that governed ¶121's "pure possibility of an information."

In his "beautiful transcription" of the *Origin*, Fink writes of speech what holds *a fortiori* of writing — and Derrida quotes: "In sensible embodiment occurs the '*localization*' and the '*temporalization*' (*Temporalisation*) of what is, by its sense of being, non-local and non-temporal." The formula states the mystery in its strictest form: incarnation gives a *where* and a *when* to what has neither, without thereby revoking its omnipresence and omnitemporality.

But Derrida, having praised the formula — it "remarkably sharpens the difficulty and reawakens the unwonted virtue of language," translating Husserl's exacting effort not to miss the ideality of the thematic sense and the ideality of the word in their relations with the linguistic *event* — turns it against itself. Does it not *let it be understood* that incorporation by language is *exterior* to the sense-of-being of the ideal objectity? that incorporation "happens" to it, "supervenes" on it *par surcroît*, as a surplus? Does it not give one to think that the ideal objectity is *fully constituted as such before and independently of its incorporation — or rather, before and independently of its incorporability*? The question marks are the argument: Fink's sentence, for all its beauty, still speaks the language of a Platonism in which embodiment befalls a sense complete without it — exactly what ¶¶121 and 142 have shown Husserl's deepest analysis to deny. The long footnote attached here is one of the Introduction's most technically inventive, and it deserves full incorporation. The strangeness of sensible embodiment, Derrida observes, is double: it is both the *habitation of the word by the sense* and the *hic et nunc use of the word's ideality*. In the first case, the incorporation is, at the limit, the *inscription of an absolutely "free" and objective ideality* — that of geometrical truth — *in the "bound" ideality of the word*: in general, of a *freer* ideality in a *less free* one. In the second case, it is the incorporation of a necessarily bound ideality — the identity of the word within a language — *in a real-sensible event*. But this last incorporation, Derrida argues, still passes through *another stage of mediate ideality* that Husserl does not directly describe but that can be situated with strictly Husserlian concepts: *the ideality of vague morphological forms or types* (a notion to be made precise later, in the analysis of the pre-geometrical world) *proper to the corporeality of graphic and vocal signs themselves*. The forms of signs must have a certain identity that imposes itself and is recognized *each time* in the pure sensible facticity of language: without this ideal identity — that of *letters and phonemes*, for example — always aimed at and approached, "no sensible language would be possible *as* language," none would be intelligible, none could aim at higher idealities. This morphological ideality is naturally still *more* "bound" than the word's. The precise place of realizing incorporation properly speaking, in its ultimate moment, is thus "the union of sensible form and sensible matter, traversed by the language-intention which always aims, explicitly or not, at the highest ideality." Linguistic incarnation and the constitution of *scriptural space* therefore suppose an *ever tighter "binding" of ideality in reality* across a series of less and less ideal mediations, in the synthetic unity of one aim; and this intentional synthesis is "an incessant movement of going and returning, working to *bind* the ideality of sense and to *liberate* the reality of the sign, each of the two operations being always *haunted* by the sense of the other, which already announces itself or still retains itself there." The conclusion is a perfect chiasmus: "Through language, the ideality of sense thus *liberates itself in the very labor of its 'binding.'*" The note accomplishes three things at once: it discovers a stratum Husserl never thematized (the ideality of the signifier's form — in later terms, the iterability of the mark); it shows that incarnation is not one event but a layered passage through degrees of ideality; and it formalizes the *Origin*'s paradox as a single two-way operation in which liberation and binding are each other's labor.

Now the counter-thesis is documented from Husserl himself. Husserl *insists* on it: so long as it *cannot* be said *and written*, truth is not fully objective — not ideal, intelligible for everyone, indefinitely perdurable. Since this perdurability is *its very sense*, "the conditions of its *survival* (*survie*) are implied in those of its *life*." No doubt truth never *derives* its ideal objectivity or identity from this or that factual linguistic incarnation, and remains "free" with regard to all linguistic facticity. But this freedom is possible precisely only from the moment truth *can in general* be said or written — "*on condition that it be able to be*." Paradoxically, then, "it is the *graphic possibility* that permits the *ultimate liberation* of ideality." And Derrida concludes: one could therefore "almost invert the terms of Fink's formula": *non-spatio-temporality comes to sense only through its linguistic incorporability*. The inversion is total and exact: where Fink had embodiment supervene on a ready-made supratemporal sense, Derrida has supratemporality itself *accrue* to sense only insofar as sense can be embodied. Omnitemporality is an effect of inscribability — the highest freedom, a function of the capacity to be bound. (The word *survie*, survival or living-on, slipped in here as if in passing, will have in Derrida's later work the career one knows.)

The consequence for the medium of history. That ideal objectivity is radically independent of *sensible* spatio-temporality *because* it can essentially in-form the body of speech and writing, and depends on a *pure intention of language* — this "prescribes to communication, hence to pure tradition and history, an *original spatio-temporality* that escapes the alternative of the sensible and the intelligible, of the empirical and the metempirical." Sense-history occupies a dimension for which the inherited ontological grid has no name: not in the world, not out of it. From this point on, truth "is no longer simply *exiled* in the originary event of its language" — the exile motif of ¶72 is revoked at the level of language itself. Its historical *habitat authenticates it*: as the *archi-document* authenticates "if it is the depositary of an intention, if it refers, without falsifying it, to the original and originary act" — that is, to the *authentic* act in the Husserlian sense of the word, the act that "founds a value of truth, makes itself responsible for it, and *can appeal to the universality of witness* (*témoignage*)." Embodiment, which a residual Platonism would read as banishment, is re-described juridically as *attestation*: the written trace is not the tomb of the intention but its credentials, the standing possibility of summoning every future subject as witness.

Husserl thus "indicates the direction of a *phenomenology of the written thing* (*la chose écrite*)," which would specify itself as a *description of the book*: as unity of an enchainment of significations. This unity can be more or less ideal and necessary — hence universal — according to the book's sense-content; its ideality itself can be not only more or less "bound" to facticity, but bound *according to diverse and wholly original forms and modalities*. Moreover, the relation of the "*exemplars*" (copies) to the *archetypal unity* is doubtless "of a unique genre" among the reproductions of other cultural formations, those of the non-literary arts in particular. Finally, "the *volume* and the proper *duration* of the book are neither purely sensible phenomena nor purely intelligible noumena": their specificity appears irreducible — the book as object inhabits precisely that third spatio-temporality named in The vast footnote develops the program through *Erfahrung und Urteil* §65. Husserl distinguishes in the *Origin* between literature in the broad sense (the place of all written discourse) and literature as *literary art*; the literary work serves him repeatedly as guiding thread for the ideality of cultural objectities, since its ideal identity is never confused with, and never derives its individual identity from, its sensible incorporations. Indeed *the origin of identity is the very criterion* discriminating the real from the ideal — and Derrida quotes the canonical definition: we call *real*, in the broadest sense, "everything that, conformably to its sense, is essentially *individualized by its spatio-temporal situation*," and *irreal* "every determination that is doubtless *founded* in a specific real by virtue of its spatio-temporal apparition, but can appear in *different* realities *as identical* — and not merely as similar" (Husserl's emphasis). Goethe's *Faust* "has its event in as many real books as one likes" — the exemplars of *Faust* — and the spiritual sense determining the work is "incorporated" in the real world but *not individualized* by the incorporation; "from another point of view: the same geometrical proposition can be uttered as often as one wants." But then — Derrida presses the program to its hard cases — how determine the ideality of a work that has *only one* spatio-temporal incorporation, to which its *proto-individualization* is bound? How make its ideality appear by variation of factual exemplars, when these can only *imitate a facticity* and not express or "indicate" an ideal sense? What, in short, of the ideality of the *plastic arts*, of *architecture* — and of *music*, "whose case is still more ambiguous"? Though repetition here is of a different nature, demanding in each case an appropriate and prudent analysis, it remains *possible in principle* and thereby reveals an incontestable ideality — and Husserl says so: an ideal object can, "like *Raphael's Madonna*, have in fact only one worldliness (*Weltlichkeit*), and not be in fact repeatable in sufficient identity (that of the complete ideal content). *But this ideal is no less principially repeatable, just like Goethe's Faust*." There is thus, from the *first* perception of a plastic artwork as such — whose ideal value is originarily and intrinsically rooted in an event — "a sort of *immediate reduction of facticity*," which afterward permits neutralizing the necessary imperfection of the reproductions. And Husserl, the note ends, proposes analogous distinctions in the *political* sphere: the ideality of the constitution of the state, of the state-will, together with the originality of its "binding" to the facticity of a territory, a nation, within which it can be indefinitely repeated in the ideality of its value. The footnote quietly completes the system of bound and free idealities across the whole field of culture — artwork, score, monument, constitution — and shows the *Origin*'s question radiating outward: everywhere the same problem of a unique event founding an indefinitely repeatable identity, of which geometry is only the purest case.

A brief paragraph of corroboration from the other Bachelard: Gaston Bachelard calls "*bibliomène*" this "being of the book," this "instance of printed thought" whose "language is not natural" (*L'activité rationaliste de la physique contemporaine*). The neologism — the book-phenomenon, on the model of *noumène* and *phénomène* — confirms from the side of historical epistemology what ¶147 had drawn from Husserl: the printed book is an entity of its own kind, neither natural thing nor pure idea, the proper *milieu* of scientific rationality.

Derrida then secures the point philologically and states the section's governing difficulty. Already in the *Logical Investigations*, Husserl had recognized the importance and the "difficult signification" of this *milieu* of writing, which the *Origin* illuminates more directly. The footnote quotes the *Prolegomena* §6 at length: science has objective existence "*only in its bibliography*"; only in the form of written works does it have an existence of its own, perpetuating itself "through the millennia" and surviving "individuals, generations and nations"; it represents a sum of "*extrinsic devices*" (*dispositifs extrinsèques* — Derrida's emphasis) through which the cognitive acts of many individuals can pass into the identical acts of innumerable others. Derrida's gloss on his own emphasis: at *that* level of analysis, concerned above all to disengage the objective autonomy of significations, the written exemplars are indeed *extrinsic* — neither the ideality of sense nor the clear intention of knowledge depends on them; "but this neither forbids nor contradicts in any way the later theme of writing as *intrinsic* possibility and condition of the acts of objective knowledge. *The Origin maintains both themes. That is the difficulty we are striving to clarify here.*" The main text formulates this difficulty as the constitutive ambiguity of writing: writing "accentuates and completes the ambiguity of all language." It is the movement of *essential and constituting incorporability* — and it is *also* the place of *factual and contingent incorporation* for every absolutely ideal object, that is, for truth. Truth, inversely, has its origin in *a pure right to speech and writing*; but once constituted, it conditions expression in its turn *as an empirical fact*. It *depends* on the pure possibility of saying and writing, but is *independent* of what is said and written *insofar as these are in the world*. Whence the section's guiding formula: if it is in its language and through its language that truth suffers a certain *lability*, "its fall (*déchéance*) will be less a fall *toward* language than a degradation *within* language." Crisis is not sense's descent into signs; it is a mutation in the way signs are inhabited.

The long paragraph that follows names, for the first time, the abyssal possibility the Introduction has been circling. As soon as sense is gathered into a sign — and this gathering "is prescribed to it" — the sign becomes "the worldly and *exposed* residence of an *unthought* truth." (The footnote attached to "sign" is, for readers of Derrida's subsequent work, perhaps the most prophetic in the book: he takes the word in the broad sense of signifying sign or "*expressive* sign," graphic or vocal, in the acceptation Husserl gives the term *in opposing it to the "indicative" sign* — First Investigation §§1–5; and one could, on the basis of this distinction, "interpret the phenomenon of *crisis* — which always refers, for Husserl, to a *sickness of language* — as a *degradation of the expression-sign into the indication-sign*, of a 'clear' (*klar*) aim into an *empty symbol*." The expression/indication distinction that will organize the whole of *La voix et le phénomène* is here introduced in a footnote, and crisis is already defined as the becoming-indicative of expression.) The main text proceeds by paradox upon paradox. That truth can thus *perdure without being thought* — in act or in fact — is exactly what emancipates it radically from every empirical subjectivity, every factual life, every real world; and "at the same time," the being-in-community of humanity "*crosses a new stage*" (O., 186): it can henceforth *appear to itself as a transcendental community*. "The act of *authentic* writing is a transcendental reduction operated by and toward the *we*." But — the other face — since, *in order to escape worldliness*, sense must *first be able to gather itself in the world*, to deposit itself in sensible spatio-temporality, "it must put in peril its pure intentional ideality, that is, its *sense of truth*." And so there appears, "in a philosophy which, at least in certain of its motifs, is the contrary of an empiricism, a possibility that until now suited only empiricism and non-philosophy: that of a *disappearance of truth*." Derrida is explicit about choosing "the ambiguous word *disappearance*": what disappears is what is *annihilated* — but also what merely *ceases to appear in fact*, intermittently or definitively, "without being affected in its being or its sense of being." To determine the sense of this "disappearance" of truth is "the most difficult of the problems posed by the *Origin* and by the whole Husserlian philosophy of history" — and Derrida confesses that he has not found in Husserl an *unequivocal* answer to a question that "only turns around the question of phenomenology itself: *what is the sense of appearing?*" This equivocation, he announces, will reveal *both* "how foreign the author of the *Krisis* was to history, how incapable of taking it fundamentally seriously," *and* "to what point, in the same moment, he strives to respect the original signification and possibility of historicity and, in truth, penetrates them profoundly." The double verdict — Husserl foreign to history *and* its profoundest interpreter, in one and the same gesture — is the hermeneutic frame for the entire examination of the three hypotheses about to begin.

A one-sentence unit that opens the inquiry: "What, then, is this possibility of disappearance?" Three numbered analyses will answer it — intrapsychic forgetting, destruction of the sign, and the burning of the archive as transcendental question.

First: "let us set aside the hypothesis of a *death of sense in general within individual consciousness*." Husserl is explicit, in the *Origin* and elsewhere (the note: *Ideen* I, *Erfahrung und Urteil*, but above all the *Logic*, Appendix II — "in terms literally taken up in the *Origin*"), that once sense has *appeared once* in egological consciousness, "its total annihilation becomes impossible." The vanishing of retentions of retentions does not return to nothingness a sense that is conserved as *sedimentary habitus*, whose "sleeping potentiality" can in right be reanimated. "What is called the unconscious" and the "universal substrate" (*tréfonds*) into which sense deposits itself are "*nothing less than a phenomenological nothingness, but... a limit mode of consciousness*." (A note refers to Fink's appendix to the *Krisis* on the problem of the "unconscious" — on the naïveté of the classical problematics of the Unconscious and on whether intentional analysis can open a methodical access to it: Husserlian depth-psychology as the rigorous alternative to both.) But Derrida's point is the *restriction* of this consolation: in this type of analysis — itself weighed down with redoubtable difficulties — Husserl attends only to the permanence and *virtual presence of sense within the monadic subject*, and not to the *absolutely ideal objectivity* of sense as it has been *conquered*, by speech and writing, *over* that subjectivity. Now it is precisely this objectivity that is menaced *as truth in the world*. Hence the displacement that gives the paragraph its closing aphorisms: "The depth of forgetting thus extends into the *spaces of intersubjectivity* and the *distance between communities*. *Forgetting is a historical category.*" (A final note records that Husserl rarely uses the word "forgetting" in the *Krisis* and never in the original text of the *Origin* — perhaps because habit refers it too easily to individual consciousness or its psychological sense, perhaps because it might suggest an annihilation of sense.) The monadological indestructibility of sense is thus real but beside the point: the *Origin*'s forgetting is not a psychic event; it happens *between* subjects, in the communal exteriority that writing opened — and only a historical phenomenology, not a psychology of memory, can address it.

Second: "the graphic sign, *guarantor* (*caution*) of objectivity, can also be *destroyed in fact*." This peril is inherent in the factual worldliness of inscription itself, "and nothing can definitively preserve it from this." A first reading might then conclude: since for Husserl sense is neither an in-itself nor a pure spiritual interiority but through and through "*object*," the forgetting that would follow the destruction of the sign-guardian of objectivity would *not* pass harmlessly, "as in a 'Platonism' or a 'Bergsonism,'" over the surface of a sense it could not breach: "it would not only dissimulate it; it would *annihilate* it in the specific *being-in-the-world* to which its objectivity had been entrusted." For Husserl says it clearly: it is insofar as signs can be *immediately perceptible by everyone in their corporeality*, it is insofar as bodies and corporeal forms are *always already within a horizon of intersubjectivity*, that sense can be *consigned* in them and put in community. Corporeal exteriority doubtless does not *constitute* the sign as such — "but, in a sense that must be clarified, it is *indispensable* to it." The paragraph thus sharpens the dilemma to its finest edge: Husserl's anti-Platonism (sense is object, not separate idea) seems to deliver truth over to the fate of its bodies; the destructibility of parchment seems to become the destructibility of geometry.

And yet — "the hypothesis of such a factual destruction does not interest Husserl *in the least*." While recognizing the "terrifying reality" of the risk incurred, he would deny it "any thinkable — that is, philosophical — signification." Derrida unfolds the Husserlian response in full. Husserl would grant that "a universal conflagration, a burning of the world library, a catastrophe of the monument or of the 'document' in general" would *intrinsically* ravage the *bound* cultural idealities: adhering by their very sense to some facticity, they are vulnerable to the worldly accident, and "*for them alone death is possible*" — death having the transcendental signification accorded to it in ¶141 only insofar as the bound ideality is animated by a transcendental intention guided by the *telos* of an absolute liberation not fully attained. But since what orients Husserl's reflection is precisely *fully liberated* ideality, the absolute objectivity of sense — everything of which mathematics is the model — the menace of an *intrinsic* destruction by the body of the sign can be set aside concerning it. All the factual writings in which truth has been able to sediment itself will never be, in themselves, anything but sensible "*exemplars*," individual events in space and time (which is true only to a certain degree of the bound idealities). Truth depending essentially on none of them, "*they could all be destroyed* without absolute ideality being touched *in its very sense*." It would no doubt be modified, mutilated, overturned *in fact*; "perhaps it would disappear *in fact* from the surface of the world" — but its sense-of-being as truth, "*which is not in the world — neither in this world nor in another*," would remain in itself intact, keeping its own *intrinsic* historicity, its own enchainments, the catastrophe of mundane history remaining *exterior* to it. This is what Husserl means by opposing *internal or intrinsic historicity* (*innere*) to *external history* (*äussere*) — a distinction "which has only a phenomenological sense" and "is decisive." (The footnote traces it to *Ideen* I §25 — precisely apropos the history of geometry, where empiricism is said to remain "outside geometrical thought and intuition" into which one must "enter vitally" to determine its "immanent sense" — then to the *Krisis* §§7 and 15, to "Philosophy as Mankind's Self-Reflection," and "above all" to the *Origin*.) And Derrida anticipates the obvious objection in order to disqualify it on Husserl's behalf: it would be *vain* to retort that historicity, being-in-history, is *precisely the possibility of being intrinsically exposed to the extrinsic* — for one would then miss the absolutely proper historicity of the sense of truth and plunge one's discourse "into the confusion of significations and regions," admitting that a pure ideality can be modified by a *real cause*, "which is to lose sense" itself. If geometry is true, its internal history "must save itself integrally from every sensible aggression." Bound neither to this moment, nor to this territory, nor to this world, but to *the whole-of-the-world* (*Weltall*), nothing will ever prevent the mundane experiences that incarnated it from being *recommenced elsewhere* — rediscovering anew, "*without traces*, in another real history, after the entombment of this world, the paths of an inhumed adventure." Before the *veritas aeterna* — whose *proper historicity* Husserl wants to seize, and of which he speaks more and more often "as his thought lets itself be won over by history" — no real becoming has any interest other than that of the *variable example*; the hypothesis of world catastrophe "could even serve, in this sense, as a *revelatory fiction*." The paragraph is Husserl's case stated at full strength — and the objection Derrida lodged and withdrew ("intrinsically exposed to the extrinsic") remains in the text like a splinter: it is, almost word for word, the thesis his own later philosophy of the trace will maintain against this page.

To test the Husserlian response, Derrida proposes a thought experiment of exact pedigree: one should be able to *repeat analogically* the famous analysis of *Ideen* I §49, which concluded to the *intangibility of pure consciousness* — after a certain eidetic-transcendental reduction — in the case of an *annihilation of the existing world*, or of the dissipation of factual experience "into simulacra by force of internal conflicts." Husserl did not contest that, in such a case, every consciousness would *in fact* be destroyed, engulfed with the world in its mundane existence; indeed that concession is the clearest intention of the fiction, which serves to explicate a reduction revealing the *essential relativity of the world's sense* — as totality of regions — to the *Ur-Region* of transcendental consciousness. Since the latter can always, in full freedom, modify or suspend the thesis of *each*, hence of *every*, contingent existence and transcendence, it is *in right, in its sense*, absolutely independent of the totality of the world. "The situation of truth, of geometrical truth in particular, is analogous. It therefore raises the same questions." The analogy is rigorous: the burning of the world library stands to geometrical sense as world-annihilation stands to pure consciousness — a revelatory fiction proving juridical independence, not a prediction. But by the same token, every difficulty attaching to §49 transfers to the archive.

And Derrida raises exactly those difficulties. One may ask whether this *eidetic* independence, brought to light by a fiction within a "methodological idealism," holds *beyond the moment of Ideen I* — that is, beyond the stage at which the eidetic-transcendental reduction has not yet attained its final radicality and provisionally *immobilizes itself in a region*. For the region of pure consciousness is the "residue" of a "putting out of circuit" that remains *more eidetic than transcendental* — only the deepest of the *eidetic* reductions, uncovering the essential structures of the proto-region: constituting with regard to the world, certainly, but *constituted in itself*, and of which Husserl will say that it is not the "*last word*" of the transcendental regression (*Ideen* I §81; these first reductions lead only to the "threshold of phenomenology," §88). Would Husserl have judged the fiction valid when, in the *Cartesian Meditations*, he was studying the *genetic constitution of the ego* in the "unity" of its "history"? In a certain sense, yes: under the solipsist hypothesis in which the *Meditations* first install themselves, pure consciousness is still considered as that into which no worldly facticity can penetrate *as such* — "a system of being closed upon itself" (*Ideen* I §49). No doubt the intra-egological sedimentation, the potential evidences, the "residues" and "referrals" that this "history" makes necessary are only a *weave of sense*. "But are they not *also*, by the irreplaceability, the irreversibility, the invariability of their enchainments, '*facts*' — factual structures with regard to which pure consciousness would no longer be free?" Could these sedimentary structures *survive in right* the annihilation — the total "variation" — of facticity? Would they not be marked, *as sense*, by a certain order of the factual world to which past consciousness — structurally implied in every present consciousness — bound itself in its own enchainments? Husserl, Derrida concedes, would probably reply that one is then considering *factual* structures of the ego's life, "bound" to some reducible contingency, and not essential structures reduced to their pure ideality: the "unity of the history" of the ego is that of the *eidos* "ego"; what the Husserlian description aims at, what is independent, is "the *essential form* of every enchainment, of every sedimentation, hence of every history, *for every ego*" — and within this form of historicity, reached as an invariant, all facto-historical enchainments are "variable at will." The paragraph stages, with complete fairness, the deepest exchange of the section: Derrida's question (does not the very *pastness* of the ego's past, the irreversibility of its sedimented order, introduce fact into the heart of the transcendental?) against Husserl's invariant *form* of genesis. The question is not adjudicated here; it is the same question as that of ¶112's "last facticity," and Derrida will leave it open as the place where phenomenology and its other communicate.

The Husserlian answer is then applied to geometry, and the second hypothesis is closed. The enchainments and sedimentations of geometrical truth being *freed from all facticity*, "no worldly catastrophe can endanger *truth itself*. Every factual danger stops at the threshold of its internal historicity." Even if all the geometrical "documents" — *and likewise all the real geometers* — were to founder one day, to speak of this as an event "*of*" geometry would be "to commit the gravest of confusions of sense and to abdicate the responsibility of all rigorous discourse." One cannot go back on these evidences without making *the sensible* the foundation of geometrical truth — hence without putting in question the sense of geometry as constituted eidetic science; and that sense was fixed "in all security" in the *static* analyses which, as was seen above (¶¶64, 71), are the indispensable guard-rail (*garde-fou*) of every genetic or historical phenomenology. The argument is formally complete — and Derrida has stated it so well precisely in order to expose, in the next paragraph, the one premise it cannot secure.

Third hypothesis — and the dialectical center of section VII. "We would be *fully convinced* if writing were here considered by Husserl — as in the static analyses — *as a sensible phenomenon*." But have we not just learned (¶¶142–145) that writing, insofar as it *founded* or helped found the absolute objectivity of truth, was *not* simply a constituted sensible body (*Körper*) but also a *constituting* proper body (*Leib*) — "the intentional originarity of a *Here-and-Now* of truth"? If writing is at once *factual event* and *upsurge of sense*, at once *Körper* and *Leib*, "how would it save its *Leiblichkeit* from a corporeal disaster?" The question is the section's crux: the burning library destroys only *Körper*, says Husserl — but the *Origin* itself has made the graphic body more than *Körper*. Husserl, Derrida continues, will not immobilize his analysis in this ambiguity, which in his eyes is only a provisional and factual *confusion of regions*: the phenomenologist must dissipate it, on pain of resigning himself to equivocation, choosing silence, or "precipitating phenomenology into *Philosophy*" (capital P: speculative metaphysics). So Husserl "maintains the dissociative analysis and *disarticulates* the ambiguity." To grasp the danger that menaces *truth itself* in its constituting speech or writing — without leaving "*internal*" historicity — he will track the *intention* of writing (or of reading) in itself and in its purity, isolating in a *new reduction* the intentional act that constitutes the *Körper* as *Leib* and maintains it in its *Leiblichkeit*, in its living sense of truth. Such an analysis "no longer needs the *Körper* as such": it is in the intentional dimension of the animated body — in the *geistige Leiblichkeit*, more precisely in the *Geistigkeit* of the *Leib*, *to the exclusion of all factual corporeality* — that sense is intrinsically menaced. Although in the *word* — the concrete spoken or written word — *Körper* and *Leib*, body and flesh, are *numerically, in fact, one and the same being*, "their senses are definitively heterogeneous, and nothing can come to the one from the other." Hence the conclusion that closes Husserl's escape route while exhibiting its cost: the forgetting of truth itself will never be anything but "*the failure of an act and the abdication of a responsibility*" — a *défaillance* (a lapse, a fainting) "more than a defeat" — and it can be made to appear in person only within an *intentional history*. Everything here is suspended on one extraordinary admission: body and flesh of the sign are *in fact the same being* yet *in sense absolutely heterogeneous*. The unity of the word is split by a juridical frontier that no fact can cross — and Derrida's whole later thought of writing will consist in asking whether such a frontier can hold within *one* being, whether the *Leib* of language can really be insulated from the destructibility of its *Körper* when they are "numerically the same."

From this disarticulation, the status of forgetting is settled within Husserl's frame. If forgetting remains, as disappearance of *intersubjective* truth, a *historical* category (¶152), it can nevertheless be described as "a phenomenon of the *ego*, one of its intentional '*modifications*'": as intentional sense, everything can and must be described as a modification of the pure ego — provided the sense of each modification is respected with prudence, as Husserl attempts, for example, in the difficult constitution of the *alter ego*. And one sees too that, for the same reason, "forgetting will never be *radical*, however deep it may be, and sense can always — in principle and in right — be *reactivated*." The two sentences perform the recuperation announced in ¶150's "double verdict": the crisis is real, but it is an *intentional* event, a sleep of sense rather than its death — and a sleep, in right, always reversible.

Where does the danger concretely lodge? Derrida recalls that in the *Logic* and then in the *Krisis*, linguistic objectivation and *mathematical symbolization* were presented as the occasion of the "technicist and objectivist alienation," degrading science into "an art or a game" (the note gathers *Krisis* §9f, the *Investigations* on "signs deprived of signification" and "game-signification," and *Erfahrung und Urteil* §65 on real signs as "bearers" of idealities). In the *Origin*, the accusation is directed more specially against *the methodological and operative teaching of mathematics*: one learns to *use* signs whose *originary* sense — which is not the logical sense, always sedimented and accessible to an *elucidation* (¶71's *Verdeutlichung*) — dissimulates itself, *potentializes* itself beneath sedimentations. And these sedimentations — which are nothing but *intentions and intentional senses put to sleep* — do not merely *superpose* themselves in the internal becoming of sense: they *implicate themselves in their totality*, more or less virtually, at each stage or each story (the notion of *Stufe* having in the *Origin* "a sense at once structural and genetic," translatable equally by *étage*, story, or *étape*, stage). There follows one of the Introduction's bravura passages, on the *geological image* of "sedimentation," which "remarkably translates the style of this implication." The image gathers — "virtually, it too" — several strata of sense: the image of the *level*, the *stratum* deposited by an invasion or a progression *after* the radical novelty of an irruption or an upsurge — and here Derrida releases the famous wordplay: every advance, every pro-position (*Satz*) of a new sense is *at the same time* a *leap* (*Satz*) and a sedimentary *fall-back* (*satzartig*) of sense (German *Satz*: sentence/proposition, leap, and — in *Bodensatz* — sediment, dregs); the image of the *substantial permanence* of what is afterward *sup-posed*, of what *sits beneath* the bark of actual evidence; the image of the *dissimulated presence* that an activity of *excavation* can always re-produce to the light of day, as the foundation — itself founded — of higher stratifications; all of it within the *structural and internal unity of one system*, a "region" in which all the deposits, solidary but distinct, are originally commanded by an *archi-tectonic*. The passage does for "sedimentation" what ¶57 did for "ether": it shows that Husserl's governing metaphor is not an ornament but a precise structural notation — proposition as leap-and-fall, foundation as buried but excavatable, the whole governed by an architectonics — and that the *Origin*'s method (excavation, reactivation) is written into the metaphor itself.

"Before sedimented sense, the danger is first of all *passivity*." In the *Origin*, Husserl insists more on the *receptive welcome* of signs — "in the first place in *reading*" — than on the second-degree technical or logical activity, which not only does not contradict this first passivity but presupposes it. In its first moment, the synthesis that awakens the sign to signification is *in fact necessarily passive and associative*. (The footnote situates the doctrine of passive synthesis in *Erfahrung und Urteil* and the *Meditations*, but notes that it is once again the *Logic* — Appendix II — that orients it, as in the *Origin*, by the problem of the *sign* and the sedimentation of ideal objectities; it points to *Logic* §16 for the elements of a *phenomenology of reading*; and it ends with a remark of great consequence: the themes of passivity and sedimentation — of the *potentiality* of sense — "derive all their gravity from having imposed themselves on a philosophy of *actual* evidence whose 'principle of principles' is the immediate and in-act presence of sense itself. If reactivation is precious and urgent, it is because it can lead back to actual and active evidence a sense thus recovered from historical virtuality. *If, in appearance, phenomenology let itself be summoned outside itself by history, it thus found in reactivation the medium of its fidelity*." Phenomenology's exodus into history is a loop home.) The possibility of *abandoning oneself* to this first awaiting of sense is "a permanent danger" — but "only a *freedom* can let itself be thus menaced": we are always free to reawaken the passively received sense, to reanimate all its virtualities and "convert them back" into "the corresponding activity" (O., 186). This freedom is "*the faculty of reactivation, originarily proper to every human being as a speaking being*" (O., 186). Through this reactivation — which, Husserl specifies, is *not* "in fact" the "norm," a certain comprehension being always possible without it — "I actively re-produce the originary evidence; I make myself fully responsible for, and conscious of, the sense I take in charge." *Reaktivierung* is, in the domain of ideal objectities, "the very act of all *Verantwortung* and all *Besinnung*" in the senses defined earlier: it permits laying bare, beneath the sedimentary barks of linguistic and cultural acquisitions, "the naked sense of the founding evidence." And this sense is reanimated *in that* I restore it to its dependence on *my* act and reproduce it in myself *such as it was produced the first time by another*. Of course, the *activity* of reactivation is *second*: what it gives back to me is the originary donating intuition — that of the geometrical formation, for example — "which is at once an activity and a passivity"; if activity is above all illuminated here, it is doubtless because the evidence considered is that of *created and founded* ideal formations. (A closing footnote widens the aperture vertiginously: to clarify this point one would have to address directly, for itself, "the difficult and decisive problem of activity and passivity in general" in phenomenology; and such a study "should perhaps conclude that phenomenology has only ever debated exigently the sense of this couple of concepts — or struggled indefinitely *with* it, that is, with the most 'irreducible,' and thereby perhaps the most obscuring, heritage of Western philosophy." Husserl himself, in one of his finest analyses, notes that the distinction cannot be "rigid" and that its sense must each time be "recreated" according to "the concrete situation of the analysis" — *Erfahrung und Urteil* §23. The activity/passivity pair as the West's most irreducible and most obscuring legacy: a sentence that quietly files phenomenology itself within the metaphysics whose closure Derrida will later announce.)

Reactivation's responsibility, finally, is a *co-responsibility*: it engages the one who *receives*, "but also and first the one who *creates* and then *expresses* the sense." For the sedimentations obliterate sense only in the measure that *surfaces have been abandoned to them*: "*the equivocity of expression is the terrain of election of the sedimentary deposits*." That is why the proto-founding geometer, and those who succeed him, must be "concerned for the *univocity* of linguistic expression, and to secure products expressible in a univocal way, thanks to a very attentive *coining* (*frappe*) of words, propositions and enchainments of propositions" (O., 188). The economy of responsibility is thus distributed across the whole communication chain: the reader must reactivate, but the writer must *mint* — the monetary figure of the *frappe*, the striking of coin, rejoining the treasure and capital of ¶¶74–79 — expressions that offer no fold, no recess, in which dormant intentions could silt up. With this, the Introduction passes to its great meditation on univocity and equivocity.

Husserl, Derrida observes, "never ceased to appeal to the imperative of univocity": equivocity is "the path of all philosophical aberration." But — the difficulty doubles on itself — it is all the harder not to rush down that path in that "the sense of equivocity in general is itself equivocal." There is a *contingent* plurivocity and an *essential* plurivocity, already distinguished in the *Investigations* (First Investigation §26). The first depends on an *objective convention*: thus the word "*chien*" — Husserl's example is the German *Hund* — signifies at once "a species of animal" and "a kind of cart in use in the mines." This plurivocity deceives no one, "and we are always free to reduce it." The second is of *subjective* origin: it depends on the original intentions, the always new experiences, that animate the identity of the objective sense and make it enter "unforeseeable configurations" — a plurivocity that is not "contingent" but "*inevitable*," which "no artificial device and no convention could eliminate from languages." The footnote quotes the *Investigations*' reassurance about the first kind (the ideal unity of each signification is untouched by the contingent fact of sharing a designation: "it is up to us to limit such an expression to a single signification") and then opens a window onto the history of philosophy: the design of univocity supposes "a decisive rupture with spontaneous language, with the '*civil language*' of which Leibniz spoke," after which the "philosophical" or "learned" language can freely give itself its own conventions — and does not Husserl's sentence sound like "the faithful echo" of Théophile in the *Nouveaux Essais*: "it depends on us to fix significations, at least in some learned language, and to agree on them *in order to destroy this tower of Babel*"? This optimism, Derrida adds, is only one of the affinities between the Leibnizian and Husserlian philosophies of language; Husserl very early felt himself heir to a Leibnizian conception of logic in general (*Prolegomena* §60). Babel, then, is the name of the situation; conventional Babel can be legislated away — but the second, *subjective* Babel, born of the inexhaustibility of intentional life, is the real adversary.

It is nevertheless precisely this *essential* equivocity that science and philosophy "must surmount": it is "inevitable" *only in natural language* — in the facto-cultural phenomenon preceding the reduction. And Derrida pauses on the stakes, reactivating the double verdict of ¶150: that Husserl is so attached to reducing, within sense, the equivocation of cultural naïveté "reveals a concern that one may once again interpret *just as well* as a refusal of history *as* a profound fidelity to the pure sense of historicity." *On the one hand*, univocity "subtracts truth from history": the univocal expression "comes wholly to the surface" (*fait totalement surface* — the surfacing of ¶130 now as ideal of language), offering "no fold" to the more or less virtual significations that intentions might deposit in it along the pathways of a language and a culture. Whence — a remark of great reach — Husserl's case against equivocity is "always associated with a *critique of depth*." (The footnote documents this astonishing theme: *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* — "Depth is a symptom of the chaos that true science must order into a cosmos... True science, so far as its real doctrine extends, *knows no depth*"; Husserl proposing to *re-mint* (*umprägen*), "as in a revaluation of coinage, the conjectures of depth into univocal rational forms"; and even the marginalia to *Sein und Zeit*, which attribute to a *Tiefsinnigkeit* — a profundity-mongering — the Heideggerian "displacement" toward what Husserl took to be a facto-anthropological plane. To the value of *depth* Husserl prefers that of *interiority*: penetration of the *inner*, intrinsic — *wesentlich*, essential — sense. The minting figure of ¶162 returns: clarity is a currency reform.) Because univocal language gives everything to be seen in an actual evidence, because nothing hides or announces itself in "the penumbra of potential intentions," because it has "mastered every dynamic of sense," it *remains the same* — preserving its ideal identity through all the becoming of culture. Univocity is thus the condition of communication between generations of researchers at any distance; it assures the *exactitude* of translation and the *purity* of tradition.
