(A note adds that exactitude and univocity are overlapping notions for Husserl, the exactitude of expression having for its condition the exactitude of *sense* — whence geometry, model of the sciences with exact objects, attains univocity more easily than other sciences, *phenomenology in particular*; the point will return.) And then the other hand: "at the very moment it subtracts sense from historical modification, univocity *alone* makes possible a *pure* history as transmission and gathering of sense. It is only the index of the limpidity of the historical ether." Once more (¶57), the requirement formulated *before* the practice of the reductions turns out to *be* a reduction: "the reduction of empirical history toward a pure history." And the paragraph closes on the destabilizing coda: this reduction "must be indefinitely recommenced, *for language neither can nor should be maintained under the protection of univocity*." The double "ni ne peut ni ne doit" — can not *and should not* — quietly announces that equivocity is not only a fate but, in some measure yet to be determined, a function.

Why language *should* not be purely univocal is now shown by a magnificent symmetry — the page that made this Introduction famous. If a *radical equivocity* forbids history by plunging it into "the nocturnal and poorly transmissible riches of 'bound' ideality," an *absolute univocity* "would itself have no other consequence than to *sterilize or paralyze history in the indigence of an indefinite iteration*." A history of pure transparencies would have nothing to transmit but the same; tradition would be a stutter. Since equivocity always testifies to "a certain depth of becoming and dissimulation of a past," whoever wants to assume and *interiorize* the memory of a culture — "in a sort of *Erinnerung* in the Hegelian sense" — faces a choice between *two endeavors*. One "would resemble that of *James Joyce*": to *repeat and take charge of the totality of equivocation itself*, in a language that brings to the surface, in the greatest possible *synchrony*, the greatest potency of the intentions buried, accumulated, and interwoven "in the soul of each linguistic atom, of each vocable, of each word, of each simple proposition, by the totality of mundane cultures, in the greatest genius of their forms (mythology, religion, sciences, arts, literature, politics, philosophy, etc.)"; to make appear "the structural unity of total empirical culture in the *generalized equivocation* of a writing that no longer *translates* one language into another on the basis of common kernels of sense, but *circulates through all languages at once*, accumulates their energies, actualizes their most secret consonances, discloses their most distant common horizons, *cultivates the associative syntheses instead of fleeing them*, and rediscovers the poetic value of *passivity*"; in short, a writing that, instead of putting equivocation out of play with quotation marks, instead of "reducing" it, "installs itself resolutely in the *labyrinthine field* of culture 'bound' by its equivocations, in order to travel through and *reconnoiter*, in the most *actual* possible way, the most profound possible *historical* distance." The other pole "is Husserl's": to *reduce or impoverish* empirical language methodically "to the actual transparency of its univocal and translatable elements," in order to grasp at its pure source "a historicity or a traditionality that *no factual historical totality will deliver of itself*" — and which, Derrida adds with quiet decisiveness, "is *always already presupposed* by every Odyssean repetition of the Joycean type, as by every philosophy of history (in the current sense) and by every phenomenology of spirit." The essences of *finite* totalities, the typology of the *figures of spirit*, will always be idealities *bound* to empirical history; to dwell in them, to confuse them with the movement of truth, is to lapse into historicism. (The footnote completes the thrust against Hegel: Husserl always associated "Hegelianism" with "romanticism" and the "historicism" it produced once "faith" in its "metaphysics of history" was lost — and was not the very expression *Weltanschauung* first Hegelian? citing Hyppolite's *Genèse et structure*.) Every term of the Joyce description — writing that does not translate but circulates, the cultivation of associative synthesis, the rehabilitation of passivity, installation within the labyrinth — is built from Husserl's own categories with their values inverted: Joyce is the *Origin*'s photographic negative, equivocity assumed as the medium of total memory. And the verdict at this stage is Husserlian: even *Finnegans Wake* presupposes, in order to be read at all, the translatable kernel it floods.

But the verdict is immediately rebalanced — "the Husserlian project, transcendental '*parallel*' of the Joycean project, knows *the same relativity*." Joyce's enterprise — which also proceeded, Derrida notes, "from a certain anti-historicism and a will to 'awake from the *nightmare* of history'" (*Ulysses*), to master history in "a total and actual resumption" — could succeed only "by making its allowance to *univocity*": drawing on given univocities or laboring to produce others, *without which the very text of its repetition would have been unintelligible* — or would at least have remained so forever and for everyone. Symmetrically, "Husserl must admit into *pure historicity* an *irreducible* equivocity — *enriching and always renascent*." For absolute univocity is imaginable only in *two limit-cases*, both of which collapse. First: if the designated thing were not only an absolutely *one*, immutable, natural object, but a being whose unity, identity, and objectivity were *in themselves anterior to all culture*. Yet even supposing such a thing and such a perception existed, "the intervention of linguistic ideality, the project of univocity — that is, the *act of language itself* — would install it from the outset *in a culture*, in a network of linguistic relations and oppositions, which would charge the word with *lateral and virtual* intentions or reminiscences." Hence the lapidary law: "*Equivocity is the congenital mark of every culture.*" The first hypothesis — a univocal *and* natural language — is "absurd and contradictory": the very act that would name the pre-cultural thing *is* culture, and brings the network with it. The argument deserves emphasis, for it is structural and not empirical: it is not that pure designation happens to fail, but that language *as system of differences* (the "network of relations and oppositions" — Saussure's lesson absorbed into transcendental analysis) makes lateral resonance constitutive of every word. The pre-cultural arbiter of ¶128 is unreachable not only because buried (¶129) but because the gesture of appeal to it speaks.

Second limit-case: what if, "at the other pole of language," the object to be designated is *absolutely ideal* — the geometrical object, for example? This time the chance of univocity would be offered not by a *pre*\-cultural but by a *trans*\-cultural object; and it would answer, in any case, to the very *vocation of science*. The paragraph completes itself in the *Origin*'s words (O., 188–9): conformably to the essence of science, it belongs to the role of its *functionaries* to demand in permanence, or to have the personal certainty, that everything they bring to scientific *statement* be said "*once for all*" — that it be "established," indefinitely reproducible in its identity, usable in evidence for later theoretical and practical ends, "*as indubitably reactivable in the identity of its authentic sense*." The Herbartian "once for all" of ¶114 returns as a *deontology*: the scientist as functionary of humanity (the *Krisis*' word) is the officer of univocity, charged with minting (¶162) statements whose reactivability is guaranteed in advance.

But even this second hope must be relativized — and the analysis here reaches one of its summits. This identity of sense, foundation of univocity and condition of reactivation, "is *always relative*, because it always inscribes itself *within a mobile system of relations* and takes its source in *a project of acquisition open to infinity*." Even if these relations are, within a science, relations of pure idealities and "truths," they nonetheless give rise to "singular placings-in-perspective, multiple enchainments of sense, hence mediate and potential aims." If equivocity is *in fact* always irreducible, it is because "*words and language in general are not and can never be absolute objects*": they have no resistant and permanent identity absolutely proper to them; "they hold their being-of-language from an *intention that traverses them as mediations*"; the "*same*" word is always "*other*" according to the always different intentional acts that make of it a signifying word. There is here "a sort of *pure equivocity*, which grows *in the very rhythm of science*" — the more a science enriches its system, the more perspectives traverse each of its expressions. (The footnote draws the consequence backward: this is why — as noted at ¶116 — Husserl could not pose the question of absolute ideal objectivity *about language itself*, whose ideality is always that of a "*thematic index*" and never of a theme; "this irreducible mediateness would thus render illusory *any salvation promised by speech or writing themselves*." Writing saves only as conduit, never as substance.) Husserl himself concedes the point in a note of the *Origin*: scientific statement, without being put in question in its truth, remains always *provisional*, and "*objective, absolutely established knowledge of the truth is an infinite idea*" (O., 189). Absolute univocity is therefore inaccessible — "but as an *Idea in the Kantian sense* can be." And from this Derrida draws the dissymmetry that adjudicates between the two poles: if Husserl's sought univocity and Joyce's generalized equivocation are both *in fact* relative, "they are not relative *symmetrically*. For their *common telos*, the positive value of univocity, reveals itself *immediately* only in the relativity defined by Husserl. *Univocity is also the absolute horizon of equivocity.*" In conferring on univocity the sense of an *infinite task*, Husserl does not make of it — "as we might have feared" — the value of a language subtracted from history by impoverishment, "but the condition, *at once a priori and teleological, of all historicity*: that without which the very equivocations of empirical culture and history would not be possible." The resolution is exact and characteristically double-edged: Joyce wins the fact (equivocity is irreducible, even in geometry), Husserl wins the structure (equivocation itself is legible only against the horizon of the univocal — the *malentendu* logic of ¶129 and the Euryclean logic of ¶115 generalized to language as such). Univocity is saved, but only as telos; and a telos, as the next paragraphs will show, presupposes precisely the finitude it infinitely defers.

The problematic of univocity "immediately reverberates on that of reactivation": their schema is the same, since without a *minimal transparency* of language no reactivation would be imaginable. But if univocity is in fact always relative, and if it alone permits the reduction of empirical culture and sedimentation, "is one not entitled to *doubt the possibility of a pure history of sense*?" All the more so, Derrida continues, in that Husserl himself, after presenting the faculty of reactivation, does not fail to pose "the grave question of its *finitude*." In a science whose enrichment is as extraordinary as geometry's, it is *impossible* that each geometer — at each instant, and each time he takes up his task again "after necessary interruptions" — should operate a *total and immediate* reactivation of "*the prodigious chain of foundations back to the archi-premises*" (O., 189). The necessity of these interruptions is a *factual* necessity — "sleep, professional pauses, etc." — which has *no sense* with regard to geometrical truth; "but it is no less *irreducible*." The comedy of the example (the geometer must sleep) is the seriousness of the argument: a pure nonsense, an utterly extrinsic fact, turns out to condition *in fact* the life of the purest sense — the same scandal of the "last facticity" (¶112) now lodged in the very organ of tradition.

And the finitude proves to be double — extrinsic *and* essential. A *total* reactivation, "even if it were possible, would paralyze the internal history of geometry as surely as would the radical impossibility of all reactivation": the geometer who at every step re-lived the whole chain from the archi-premises would never advance — the univocity-paralysis of ¶165 in temporal form. Husserl, Derrida notes, is not alarmed: total recuperation of origins remains here, once again, only a *teleological horizon*. But beneath the extrinsic necessity of interruptions hides "an *essential and internal* necessity": since *no piece of the geometrical edifice is independent*, "no *immediate* reactivation is possible, at any level whatsoever" — every theorem implicates the system. That is why, Husserl remarks, the "power, *individual as much as communal*," of reactivation is of "*an evident finitude*" (O., 193). "Immediate totality will always be refused to it." The admission is enormous: the very faculty on which the salvation of tradition was staked (¶161) is structurally — not accidentally — finite; mediateness is internal to the edifice of the most exact of sciences.

Derrida now drives the admission to its limit, in the paragraph that comes closest to stating his own position against Husserl's. "The evidence of this finitude and this necessary mediateness could strike Husserl's whole design with non-sense." Since it is in fact irreducible, "should not this finitude furnish *the true point of departure* of a reflection on history?" Without this *essential dissimulation of origins* — under the hypothesis of an all-powerful reactivation — "what would the *consciousness of historicity* be?" (Nothing: a humanity in full actual possession of its archi-premises would have no past, only an eternal present of evidence.) Granted, that consciousness would also be nothing "if access to the origins were *radically forbidden* to it." But must one not then say — "for history to have its proper density" — that the *night* in which the archi-premises are engulfed, while letting itself be *penetrated* but never *dissipated*, conceals "not only the *fact* but the *founding sense*"? and that the "critical" forgetting of origins is "not the accidental going-astray, but *the shadow faithful to the movement of truth*"? In that case, "the distinction between fact and sense (or right) *would efface itself* in the becoming-conscious of an *originary finitude*." Every term has been prepared and every term is now turned: forgetting, which for Husserl was failure and abdication (¶158), becomes truth's faithful shadow; dissimulation, which was crisis, becomes the very density of history; and the fact/sense distinction — the master-distinction on which the entire Husserlian edifice rests (¶¶47–51, 154, 158\) — is shown to *presuppose* a finitude it cannot itself think. The Heideggerian resonance of "originary finitude" is unmistakable and surely deliberate; but the argument is generated immanently, from Husserl's own admission of the essential finitude of reactivation. It is the Introduction's most radical moment — offered, characteristically, in the interrogative mode.

And the Husserlian rejoinder is given its full, strange force. For Husserl, "as one knows, this finitude can appear *in its originarity* only on the basis of the *Idea of an infinite history*": finitude is legible as finitude only against the infinite — the structure of ¶129 yet again, raised now to the ultimate stakes. So, before the finitude of reactivation, Husserl does not renounce the first orientation of his research. He defers the problem and invites us, "with a somewhat enigmatic brevity," to "remark" that there exists "an *idealization*: namely, the liberation from its limits and, in a certain way, the *infinitization of our power*" (O., 193). A secondary idealizing operation thus comes to *relever* — to relieve, lift up, take over (the verb with which Derrida will later translate Hegel's *aufheben*) — the reactivating power from its finitude, permitting it to *surpass itself*: by a movement analogous to the constitution of the unity of the world's infinite horizon; analogous to the evidence, beyond the finite enchainment of retentions and protentions, of a *total unity of the immanent flux* as Idea in the Kantian sense (*Ideen* I §83); analogous "above all" to the movement that gives birth to the *ideal exactitude of geometry* "by passage to the infinite limit of a finite and qualitative sensible intuition." Indeed, Derrida adds, "here, precisely, it is the *geometrical* idealization that permits the infinitization of the reactivating power": operating "in the diaphaneity of pure ideality," that power easily transgresses *in right* limits which are then no more than the "insignificant" limits of pure facticity. But the paragraph's last movement opens the wound it had seemed to close. This idealization, whose correlate is an *infinite Idea*, "always intervenes *decisively* at the *difficult moments* of the Husserlian description" — and "the phenomenological status of its evidence remains rather *mysterious*." The impossibility of adequately determining the *content* of this Idea does not impair, says Husserl in *Ideen* I, the rational transparency of its evidence (*Einsichtigkeit*; a note records S. Bachelard's justification for rendering *Einsicht* as "rational evidence"). And yet: "the certainty of *what can never present itself immediately and as such to an intuition* should pose grave problems for a phenomenology" — problems similar, for example, to those of the constitution of the *alter ego* by an irreducibly *mediate* intentionality. Derrida promises to return to this directly when the production of geometrical exactitude by idealization is at issue; for the moment Husserl himself sets the difficulty aside: "*The original evidence of such an idealization will occupy us further*" (O., 193). The diagnosis lodged here — that the Idea in the Kantian sense, evidence of what never appears, is at once phenomenology's indispensable resource and its internal scandal, the non-phenomenal condition of the principle of principles — is the thesis on which the Introduction's final sections will stake everything.

Section VII closes by descending from structure to situation. The power of reactivation "must therefore be *transmitted*," lest science decay into "a *tradition become empty of sense*." But as science distances itself from its beginnings and its logical superstructures accumulate, the chances of such transmission *decrease* — "until the day the power comes to fail. '*That, alas, is our situation, and that of all modern times*'" (O., 195). The progress of science can then continue *while the sense of origin has been lost*: the very logicality of scientific gestures, "imprisoned in mediateness," founders in "a sort of *oneiric and inhuman absurdity*" — science as sleepwalking. And Derrida seals the diagnosis with a remarkable retrieval: had not *Plato* described this situation — Plato, "for whom the eternity of the essences was perhaps only *the other name of a non-empirical historicity*"? Geometry and its kindred sciences, exiled far from their principial intuitions, "*incapable of seeing* (ἰδεῖν)," riveted to hypotheses held for principles, confusing the symbol with the truth, "*seem to us to dream*" (*Republic* VII, 533c). The audacity is double: the *Krisis*' diagnosis of modernity — technization, the emptying of sense, science as game — is discovered, word for word, in *Republic* VII; and Platonism itself, the Introduction's constant foil (¶¶56, 81, 153), is reinterpreted in one subordinate clause as a *philosophy of intrinsic historicity* avant la lettre, the "eternity" of the *eidē* renamed omnitemporal traditionality. The dreaming sciences must be wakened: whence the closing sentence — the *question en retour* is *urgent*, the question "that will awaken science, *for us and through us*, to its originary sense — that is, as we now know, to its *final* sense." The arc of section VII ends by reciting its deepest lesson (¶93): origin and telos are the same awakening.

## **VIII**

Section VIII opens at the moment when the *Origin* itself pauses to take stock. The method and sense of the question of origin now stand illuminated, together with the conditions of a traditionality of science *in general*; and at the moment of closing these preliminary considerations, Husserl recalls their *exemplary* and fully "*historical*" character — historical "in the sense of *Historie*," Derrida specifies: of historical inquiry, historiography, and not merely of *Geschichte*, happening. The quotation (O., 199): in every case, the problems, the clarifying researches, the principial rational evidences are *historical* (*historisch*); we stand within the historical horizon in which — "however few determinate things we know — *everything is historical*. But this horizon has its *essential structure*, which must be unveiled by a methodical interrogation." The two sentences hold the *Origin*'s whole wager: universal historicity ("everything is historical" — the principial evidences included) *and* an invariant structure of that historicity, accessible to method. Everything is historical; therefore — not nevertheless — an eidetics of history is required.

With regard to the other sciences, as to the world of *prescientific* culture, other returns to origin are thus *prescribed*: always possible, though they remain as yet in "*non-problematicity*" — prescribed but not yet activated as problems. And this field of interrogation "has no limits, since historicity embraces the infinite totality of being and sense." The *Origin* says so itself (O., 199–200): with the particular style given them here, these problems immediately awaken "the *total* problem of the *universal historicity* of the correlative mode of being of humanity and the cultural world, and the *a priori structure* of this historicity." The exemplarity announced in ¶¶3 and 79 is thus formally cashed out: geometry was the guiding example; the true theme is the a priori of universal historicity — the correlation humanity/cultural-world in its total historical mode of being.

A short paragraph of stage-setting: having opened the widest horizon of his question about geometry, and before returning to the *determinate* origin of this science, Husserl responds — "in a sort of complementary elucidation" — to *two methodological objections, diametrically opposed to one another*. The architecture of section VIII is thereby given: the epistemologist's objection (the return to origins is not history) and the historicist-ethnologist's objection (there is no universal a priori of history), the *Origin* defending its third way against both — exactly the double front of ¶¶64, 81, 88\.

The first objection "would proceed from a classical *epistemologism*": the return to originary evidence and founding concepts is no doubt an indispensable task, *but it is in no way historical*; it can give itself the illusion of history only by verbal and symbolic allusions to some "unfindable," barely mythical *Thales*. Derrida notes the irony with documentary precision: this is the very objection Husserl had *wielded on his own behalf* when, concerning the origin of the sciences and of geometry in particular, he attacked empiricism and external history — and a footnote sends the reader to *Ideen* I §25, where Husserl develops, "in strangely similar terms, the objection he here feigns to address to himself," adding that the confrontation of the two texts "can be remarkably illuminating as to the sense and the fidelity of the Husserlian itinerary." Husserl now rejects the objection "because it takes his own style of historical research in a *contre-sens*" — a style "as *internal* and as little empiricist as possible." Need one recall that it was never a question of returning to Thales and the factual beginnings of geometry? But "to renounce factual history is in no way to cut oneself off from history in general: it is, on the contrary, *to open oneself to the sense of historicity*." And then, in a sentence "whose accent, at least, breaks with that of the first phenomenology, but which only confirms and deepens, with an admirable fidelity, the initial mistrust of classical history," Husserl writes (O., 201): "The all-powerful *dogma* of the *principial break* between epistemological elucidation and historical explicitation — as well as psychological explicitation in the order of the sciences of spirit — between *epistemological* origin and *genetic* origin: this dogma, insofar as one does not inadmissibly limit, as is the custom, the concepts of 'history,' 'historical explicitation' and 'genesis,' *is overturned from top to bottom*. Or rather, what is thus overturned is the *closure* (*clôture*) on account of which precisely the most original and most profound problems of history remain dissimulated." The quotation is among the most important in the *Origin* for understanding Derrida's life-long relation to Husserl: the founder of the anti-psychologistic, anti-geneticist "principial break" of 1900 here declares that very dogma overturned — *quaestio juris* and *quaestio facti*, validity and genesis, no longer separable once "history" and "genesis" are understood in their full (intentional, transcendental) concepts. Derrida underlines the fidelity within the reversal: the mistrust of *classical* history is maintained; what falls is the closure that protected epistemology *from* history — and the word *clôture*, here Husserl's, will have its own destiny in Derrida's vocabulary.

What replaces the dogma is then formulated. To become conscious of a science *as tradition and form of culture* is to become conscious of its *integral historicity*: from this fact, every *intra-scientific* elucidation — every return to first axioms, originary evidences and founding concepts — is *at the same time a "historical unveiling."* Whatever our ignorance about real history, "we know *a priori* that every cultural present — hence every scientific present — implicates *in its totality the totality of the past*." And the unity of this incessant totalization, which always operates "in the form of the *historical Present* (the 'Primordial in itself')," leads, if correctly interrogated, "to the *universal Apriori of history*." Like the in-itself-unmodifiable Absolute of the *Living Present* in which it is founded (¶78), the historical Present is at first "only the irreducible and pure *place* and *movement*" of this totalization and traditionalization: "*it is the Absolute of history*," as — the *Origin*'s words — "*living movement of the solidarity and mutual implication* (*des Miteinander und Ineinander*) *of originary sense-formation* (*Sinnbildung*) *and sense-sedimentation*" (O., 203). (The footnote specifies, with Husserl, that what is meant is the historical Present *in general*, ultimate universal form of every possible historical experience, itself founded in the Living Present of egological consciousness; and that, as Husserl notes, total intrinsic history "passes through the intrinsic history of the totality of individual persons" — the communal absolute never bypasses the monads, per ¶¶85–87.) The paragraph completes the edifice begun in ¶78: Living Present → historical Present → universal Apriori of history, each level the form of totalization of the one below; and the Absolute of history is defined not as a content but as the *with-one-another and in-one-another* of formation and sedimentation — production and deposit, leap and fall (¶160), as one living movement.

The methodological corollary: every *particular* historical investigation must mark, in right, its more or less immediate dependence on this absolutely principial evidence; every habitual fact-history "*remains in non-intelligibility*" so long as these a prioris have not been explicated and its method has not been adapted to the notion of *intrinsic history* — "intentional history of sense." The claim is not imperialist but juridical (per ¶99): fact-history may proceed, but its own intelligibility is on loan from the apriori it ignores.

"This leads us to the second riposte, directed this time against *historicism* rather than against empirical history." The schema of the critique is analogous to that of *Philosophy as Rigorous Science* — but the historicism Husserl now attacks, despite its affinities with the Diltheyan theory of *Weltanschauung*, "seems to have a more *ethno-sociological*, more modern style." And the stakes have shifted in a way Derrida flags as decisive: what Husserl wants to wrest from historical relativism is "less the truth or the ideal norms of science and philosophy than *the a prioris of historical science itself*." Not: geometry is safe from the historians; but: history *as a science* is impossible on historicist premises — the relativist saws off the branch on which the ethnological fact sits.

The objection in its modern form: "*ethnologism*" opposes to the universal apriori — to the unconditioned and apodictic structures, the unitary ground of history that Husserl means to describe — "the teeming multiplicity of testimonies attesting that each people, each tribe, each human group has *its* world, *its* apriori, *its* order, *its* logic, *its* history." The plural of the aprioris against the singular of the Apriori: the form of the objection could not be more contemporary, and Derrida lets its full weight be felt.

Husserl's answer unfolds in two movements. *On the one hand*, these irrecusable testimonies "do not belie but on the contrary *presuppose* the structure of universal horizon and the a prioris of history that Husserl designates": they only *articulate* upon them their singular and determinate a prioris. It suffices, then, to respect these articulations and "the complicated hierarchy" that subordinates more or less determinate *material* a prioris to the *a priori form of universal historicity*. (The plural of cultural worlds is real; it is ordered, not dissolved.) *On the other hand*, the "facts" invoked in support of this relativism "can be determined as *certain historical facts* only if something like *a historical truth* is determinable in general" (a note refers the same argument to the Vienna Lecture). Ranke's "*wie es eigentlich gewesen*" — the "as-it-actually-was" that is the ultimate reference of all fact-history — "presupposes as its horizon a *historical determinability* that no empirical science, by itself and as such, is capable of founding." The transcendental tu quoque is complete: the ethnologist's facts are *historical* facts — datable, attributable, attestable — only within the very apriori of historicity they are marshalled to refute.

A standalone quotation from the *Origin* (O., 208\) seals the argument in Husserl's own voice: the facts that historicism makes valid need not first be submitted to some critical examination — "it suffices that *the assertion of their facticity*, if this assertion is to have a sense, *already presupposes the historical apriori*." The economy of the sentence is the whole refutation: not the facts but the *assertability* of the facts is the lever; relativism is defeated not by contesting its evidence but by reading the conditions of that evidence's sense.

Derrida unfolds the presupposition in a paragraph of remarkable density, whose vocabulary deserves attention. To be able to "*establish*" facts *as facts of history*, "we must *always already* know what history is, and under what — *concrete* — conditions it is possible. One must *already be engaged in a pre-comprehension of historicity*": of the invariants of history that are, for example, *language, tradition, community*. (The Heideggerian terms — *toujours déjà*, *pré-compréhension* — are deliberately imported into the exposition of Husserl, quietly suggesting that the hermeneutic circle and the historical apriori are answers to one question.) For the *ethnological fact* to appear, ethnological *communication* must already be opened "in the horizon of universal humanity": two men or two groups must have been able to understand one another from the possibilities, however poor, of a universal language (¶128's argument now run in the transcendental register); the ethnologist must be sure, "with an *apodictic* certainty," that the other men also live, necessarily, in a community of language and tradition, in the horizon of a history — and sure, too, "of what that means in general." In the last recourse, one must know that "the *historical Present in general*, irreducible form of every historical experience, is the foundation of all historicity," and that within it I shall always be able to come to an agreement with "the most distant, the most different '*astre*'" — the other as remote star, the word borrowed from the Earth-fragment's planetary scenarios (¶132). However foreign two men may be to one another, they will always understand each other — "*at the limit*" — "in the community of their *Living Present*, in which the historical Present is rooted." That each of their fundamental Presents is *also* materially determined by its insertion in the factual content of a tradition, a social structure, a language; that they do not have the same *content* of sense — "this in no way affects the community of their *form*." And this universal form, "which is the most originary *and the most concrete* lived experience," is presupposed by every being-together; "it seems to be also *the last retrenchment* (*le dernier retranchement*), hence *the most responsible security, of every phenomenological reduction*. It is in this *instance* that the most radical *unity of the world* announces itself." The paragraph is the keystone of Husserl's universalism as Derrida reconstructs it: beneath every cultural difference, the form of the Living Present as the absolutely common — at once the most concrete (each one's own life) and the most universal (the same form for all); final answer to ¶¶129–130's doubts about translatability, and final guarantee of the reduction itself. But the military metaphor — the *last retrenchment*, the final defensive line — says with perfect candor that this is where phenomenology would make its last stand; and Derrida's later work will besiege exactly this position, asking whether the Living Present can be a *form* common and self-identical if temporalization is, as ¶134 conceded, dialectical alteration.

The apriori thus established, Derrida turns to its delicate adjudication with relativism — and with Merleau-Ponty. Every problematic of historical facts *implicates* these historical invariants "at the very moment it authorizes a certain relativism"; and relativism "keeps all its value provided its level of materiality and its a priori conditions are suitably determined." (A footnote forestalls a misreading: there is "in no way" a critique here of historical or socio-ethnological science *as such* — Husserl only recalls it to its presuppositions, which phenomenology alone can bring to light as such, and which scientists themselves have sometimes taken in charge; the same courtesy toward history as *Geisteswissenschaft* was already paid in *Philosophy as Rigorous Science*.) It is in this frame, Derrida proposes, that one can interpret "the part given to relativism" in the famous *Letter to Lévy-Bruhl* of March 11, 1935 — one year before the *Origin* — in which some believed, on the contrary, that Husserl *renounced* the historical a prioris discovered by imaginative variation, conceding that the pure phenomenology of history had to expect from the empirical sciences, ethnology in particular, "something other than examples." (The note quotes the letter's key concessions: the "indubitable legitimacy" of "historical relativism *as anthropological fact*" — Derrida's italics doing the interpretive work in advance — and the possible and necessary task of a comprehensive *Einfühlung*, an empathetic penetration, toward "primitive" societies that are "without history" (*geschichtlos*); while insisting "vigorously" that the rights of relativism so understood are preserved and "conserved" by the *intentional analysis* of transcendental phenomenology.) This was notably Merleau-Ponty's reading, and Derrida quotes it twice at length: Husserl "seems to admit that it is not useless that the facts *shake the imagination*," as if imagination left to itself could not represent the possibilities of existence realized in different cultures — as if it were "perhaps not possible for us, who live within certain historical traditions, to think, by the sole effort of imaginative variation, the historical possible of the primitives of whom Lévy-Bruhl speaks"; and again: historical relativism is "no longer dominated at a stroke" by a thought that "would hold the keys of history" and could "draw up the table of all historical possibles *before* any experimental inquiry"; the thinker must "go to the school of the facts"; "the eidetics of history no longer dispenses us from historical investigation"; philosophy keeps its function of last instance, "*but it must begin by understanding all the experiences*" (Derrida's emphasis). The stakes could not be higher: on Merleau-Ponty's reading, the late Husserl had tacitly abandoned the primacy of essence over fact — phenomenology converted to the empirical sciences of man.

A single-sentence unit, the hinge of the whole discussion: "*Is such an interpretation justified?*"

Derrida's answer begins by fixing what Husserl actually conceded. The relativism to which Husserl grants a right is the one attaching to historico-anthropological "*facts*" *as such and in their facticity* — a right Husserl *never* contested, "even in *Philosophy as Rigorous Science*." And the historical a prioris to which he always appealed — "and more and more, precisely" — were never presented, it seems, as the "*keys of history*" or as a "*table of all historical possibles before any experimental inquiry*." Since the "history" and "historical possibles" of which Merleau-Ponty speaks designate the *material and determinate content* of historical modifications — the *factual* possible realized in this or that society, culture, epoch — to read Husserl so would be "to lend him the pretension of *deducing facticity itself a priori*": a hypothesis that "contradicts the very premises of phenomenology" and cannot be entertained. No doubt Husserl thought that all the determinate possibles of history must *conform* to the a priori essences of historicity concerning every possible culture, language, tradition; "but never did he dream of *foreseeing*, by some eidetic deduction, all the facts, all the particular possibles" that must conform to these a prioris. The first correction is thus a matter of logical type: the apriori of historicity constrains the *form* of any possible history; it predicts nothing, and was never meant to.

"But is *not deducing facticity a priori* the same as 'going to the school of the facts'? No more so" — if that means abandoning eidetic intuition, even provisionally, and using facts "otherwise than as *examples* in an imaginative variation." The technique of variation in eidetic reading never aimed to *exhaust* the multiplicity of factual possibilities: on the contrary, its privilege is precisely "to be able to operate on a *single* one of these possibles, in a consciousness of example" (one case, freely varied, suffices for the essence — the whole point of the method). Nor was it ever its mission to "dispense us from historical investigation" — or if it does so, it is not by claiming to substitute "the solitary reflection of the historian" for inquiry going out to meet the facts: "simply, it *precedes in right* every material historical investigation, and has no need of the facts *as such* to reveal to the historian the a priori sense of his activity and his objects." And so far is Husserl from "beginning by understanding all the experiences," from abandoning or limiting imaginative variation, that variation "is explicitly and frequently *prescribed* in the *Origin* — which one may consider one of Husserl's last writings." It remains in his eyes "*the* method" by which we obtain "an apriori of the historical world that is universal and, in that, fixed and forever authentically originary"; and further on (O., 209), in the passage Derrida quotes at length: we are, and know ourselves, able "to vary in complete freedom, in thought, in imagination, our human historical existence"; and "precisely in the free act of this variation and this traversal of the imaginaries of the life-world there appears, in the relief of an *apodictic evidence*, a component of essential universality that effectively *persists through all the variants*... We have then untied ourselves from every attachment to the historical world in its sense of facticity — that world being itself considered as *one of the possibilities of thought*." The dossier is decisive against Merleau-Ponty's reading on the textual plane: a year *after* the letter, the *Origin* re-prescribes variation as *the* method and reduces the factual historical world to one thinkable possibility among others.

A brief paragraph adds the standing concession that disarms the objection without yielding to it: no doubt imaginative variation and the reduction "*here again take their point of departure, in fact, in facticity*. But here again they retain of the fact only its *exemplarity* and its essential structure — its '*possibility*,' not its facticity." The constant Husserlian figure (¶¶54, 64, 90): beginning *from* the constituted fact, retaining only its *sense* — the school of facts attended only long enough to learn what facts exemplify.

Then the generous half of the adjudication. If the discovery of the a priori structures and invariants of universal historicity is methodologically and juridically *first*, "it teaches us nothing — that is evident, and first of all in Husserl's eyes — about the *specificity* of each real society or historical moment" offered to the activity of the sociologist or historian. So it was never a question of that — nor of "constructing, by a simply imaginative variation of one's own experiences, what makes the sense of *other* experiences and *other* civilizations" (Merleau-Ponty's phrase, quoted directly; the footnote gives its context, and recalls that already in the *Phénoménologie de la perception* Merleau-Ponty had read Husserl's entire last period as a "tacit rupture with the philosophy of essences" — a rupture in which Husserl "was only explicating and thematizing procedures of analysis he had himself long applied"). Derrida grants the negative: eidetics is not a machine for generating the content of the other.

But the grant is immediately given its transcendental frame. If I nevertheless did "construct" thus "the sense of other experiences and other civilizations," it would be "in order to discover that in which they are *also* experiences and civilizations — *not that in which they are other*." And to attain this sense of *every* civilization or experience, "I shall first have had to *reduce what is mine*" (in the factual sense) in the experience and civilization from which I in fact depart. Only once the sense of experience or civilization *in general* has been clarified can I legitimately attempt to determine the *difference* between determinate facts of civilization and experience — which does not mean abandoning, from that point on, every eidetic attitude: *within* greater factual determination, "other reductions are still possible and necessary," to be articulated prudently according to their degree of generality and dependence, always respecting — as Husserl specifies in the *Origin* — the rule of strict "*subsumption*" of the singular under the universal. As material determination increases, "relativism" extends its rights; "but as it is dependent in the highest degree, it will never be" — Husserl in the same letter — "*the last word of scientific knowledge*." The architecture is now complete: a *hierarchy of regional eidetics and reductions* descending into material specificity, in which the relativist's whole domain is housed — legitimate, fertile, and at every level founded.

And the empirical sciences themselves receive a transcendental dignity. The work of the historian, the sociologist, the ethnologist constitutes, "in the encounter of factual difference, a sort of *realized imaginative variation*": one can use it directly to accede to the concrete and universal components of sociality or historicity — the field-worker as the eidetician's involuntary collaborator, facticity itself running the variations. Since the invariants will teach me nothing of the specificity of *this* society or *this* epoch, it is *there* in particular that I must "*einfühlen*," as Husserl said to Lévy-Bruhl. But this *Einfühlung*, as factual determination of difference, "precisely cannot institute the science *in right*": it is itself possible only *within and in virtue of* the universal a priori structures of sociality and historicity — it presupposes "an immediate *transcendental community* of the totality of historical humanities, and the possibility of an *Einfühlung in general*." (The very act of empathizing with the radically other presupposes the universal we of ¶184 — the argument of ¶¶128 and 184 turned now upon the ethnographer's own method.) And in the material determination of historicities, *Einfühlung* is moreover "strictly conformed to the method of every historical phenomenology, since it penetrates historical significations *from within* and makes the *external* inquiry depend on *internal* intuition" — the inner/outer rule of ¶154 governing even fieldwork. The Letter to Lévy-Bruhl is thus fully reabsorbed: what Merleau-Ponty read as conversion to the facts, Derrida reads as the assignment of the facts their transcendentally founded — and bounded — place.

There remains the letter's most troubling phrase, and Derrida faces it directly: how reconcile the affirmation that historicity is an essential horizon-structure of *every* humanity with the allusion to the "*non-historicity*" (*Geschichtlosigkeit*) of certain archaic societies? His answer reconstructs Husserl's implicit doctrine. This non-historicity has, it seems, "no pure and absolute signification" for Husserl: it would only *empirically, materially modify* the a priori structure of universal historicity; it would be "the form of historicity proper to *finite* societies, closed in their '*locked horizons*,' still inaccessible to the irruption of the '*European*' Idea of the infinite task and tradition." Their "*stagnation*" would be "not the simple vacancy of historicity, but *finitude in the project and the gathering of sense*." It is therefore "only *in comparison with* the infinite and pure historicity of the European eidos that archaic societies appear 'without history'"; in the *Krisis*, Husserl indeed grants only an *empirical type* to all societies that do not participate in the European Idea — non-historicity being then "the inferior limit-mode of empirical historicity." And here Derrida names the structure that has been gathering since ¶79: "one finds here again the ambiguity of an *example* that is at once an *indifferent sample* and a *teleological model*." In a first sense, every community is in history — historicity is the essential horizon of humanity, since there is no humanity without sociality and culture — and *any* society whatever, European, archaic, or other, can serve as example in an eidetic recognition. "But on the other hand, *Europe has the privilege of being the good example*, for it incarnates in its purity the *Telos* of all historicity — universality, omnitemporality, infinite traditionality; in becoming conscious of the pure and infinite possibility of historicity, it has *awakened history to its own end*." In this second sense, *pure* historicity is "reserved to the European eidos"; the empirical types of non-European societies are then "only more or less historical, tending, at the inferior limit, toward non-historicity." The paragraph is the Introduction's most unflinching exposition of Husserl's Eurocentrism — neither apology nor denunciation, but anatomy: the whole doctrine rests on the slippage, identified at ¶79 and ¶127 (the *pré-ingérence* of telos in eidos), by which the eidetic example becomes teleological norm. Derrida exposes the hinge and leaves it exposed; the question of whether *any* philosophy of exemplary historicity can avoid this slippage will pursue him for decades.

The doctrine is completed by Husserl's own stratification. Husserl is thus led to distinguish the originality of several *levels* within the most universal eidos of historicity: in a very brief fragment "whose inspiration is very close to that of the *Origin*" (*Stufen der Geschichtlichkeit. Erste Geschichtlichkeit*, 1934 — *Krisis* Beilage XXVI), he determines *three stages or stories* (*étapes ou étages* — the *Stufe* ambiguity of ¶160 again) of historicity. As one rises in this hierarchy, or progresses in this becoming, "historicity takes greater possession of its essence." First, historicity *in the most general sense*: essence of all human existence insofar as it moves necessarily in the spiritual space of a culture and a tradition. The immediately superior level: that of *European culture, the theoretical project, and philosophy*. The third, finally, marked by "the *conversion of philosophy into phenomenology*." (The footnote adds the parallel texts: "scientific life... signifies a historicity *of a new kind*" — Beilage XXVII; and the Vienna Lecture's "revolution at the heart of historicity.") Thus at each stage "the *revolution* that overturns the antecedent project by an *infinitization* is only the *becoming-conscious of a hidden intention*" — and Derrida generalizes, in a parenthesis that condenses the whole Husserlian teleology: "one may indeed posit as a phenomenological rule *the equivalence of every becoming-conscious with an infinitization*." (Prise de conscience \= infinitisation: the rule unites ¶32's infinitization of Greek geometry, ¶80's irruption of the infinite, and the *Origin* itself as reactivation.) Moreover, since these three moments are "stratifying structures of different heights, they do not exclude one another in fact": not only do they coexist in the world, but "*one and the same society can make them cohabit within itself*, in the differentiated unity of an organic simultaneity" — historicity's stages are not a chronology but an architecture, and modern Europe contains its own archaic strata.

Section VIII closes its adjudication by weighing the opposite temptation. It is therefore indeed "toward the *absolute eidetic and teleological invariants* of historicity that Husserlian reflection is resolutely oriented" — the *internal and dynamic differentiation* of these invariants (the three *Stufen*) must not make one lose sight of this; it is precisely the sign that what is at issue are "the invariants *of historicity*, the essences *of becoming*." One might then be tempted by an interpretation "diametrically opposed to Merleau-Ponty's": that Husserl, far from opening the phenomenological parentheses to historical facticity in all its forms, "leaves history *outside* more than ever." One could always say that, by definition and like all conditions of possibility, the invariants of history thus tracked by Husserl "are not *historical* in themselves" — and conclude, as Walter Biemel does, that "*Husserl's attempts to grasp historicity thematically can be considered failures*." (The footnote pays Biemel's lecture a double tribute: he rightly sees in the *Krisis* a work of old age too easily read as a *turning*, despite the deep continuity binding it to the earlier researches; and at the end, while underlining this fidelity, he recalls Husserl's *malaise* — in a great quantity of manuscripts of the K III group, Husserl "asks himself whether philosophy really needs history"; the *Krisis*' Beilagen XXV and XXVIII ask "Why does the philosopher need the history of philosophy?" and "How does one need history?". The founder's own late insomnia over the necessity of his historical turn — the question of the whole Introduction, posed by Husserl to himself.) The dialectical situation is now perfectly symmetrical: Merleau-Ponty reads the late Husserl as surrendering essence to fact; Biemel reads him as never having touched history at all. Derrida has set the two verdicts of ¶150 — foreign to history / its profoundest interpreter — into the mouths of two authoritative readers, and must now adjudicate.

The reply to Biemel has the same transcendental form as the reply to the ethnologist (¶182): "But what would historicity *be* — and the discourse *on* history — if none of these invariances were possible?" To speak of *failure* in the thematization of historicity, "must one not already have access to an invariant and more or less thematic sense of historicity? and is this sense not precisely the one that *announces itself* in Husserl's last meditations, however unfinished they are?" The objection consumes itself: Biemel's verdict of failure measures Husserl against a sense of historicity which is available at all only through the kind of invariant Husserl sought — the *Rückfrage* validated once more by the impossibility of coherently denying its terrain.

And the closing paragraph of section VIII turns the apparent weakness into the precise location of Husserl's originality. If the thematization of the apodictic invariants were "in default," it would be so "with regard to *history* rather than to *historicity*" — and the defeat would be flagrant only if Husserl had, at any moment, been interested in something like *history*: "It does not seem he ever was." His original merit would then be to have described, "in a properly *transcendental* procedure (in a sense of this word that Kantianism cannot exhaust)," conditions of possibility of history "that were *at the same time concrete*" — concrete "because *lived in the form of the horizon*." The notion of *horizon* is here decisive: "*horizon-consciousness*," "*horizon-certainty*," "*horizon-knowledge*" — "such are the key concepts of the *Origin*." The horizon is given to a *lived* evidence, to a *concrete* knowledge that, Husserl says, is never "*learned*," that no empirical moment can deliver, "since it always presupposes it": an *originary* knowledge bearing on "the totality of possible historical experiences." Then the definition, one of the Introduction's most lapidary and most quoted: "*The horizon is the always-already-there of a future that keeps intact — even as it has announced itself to consciousness — the indetermination of its infinite opening.*" Structural determination of every material indetermination, the horizon is virtually present to every experience, "of which it is at once the *unity* and the *incompletion* — the unity *anticipated* in every incompletion." And the conceptual yield is stated in the final sentence: the notion of horizon "converts the *abstract* condition of possibility of criticism into the *concrete infinite potentiality* secretly presupposed in it; *it thereby makes the a priori and the teleological coincide*." Section VIII thus ends by naming the operator that resolves — or holds together — every tension it has traversed: the horizon is how an apriori can be lived rather than deduced, concrete rather than formal, and how the condition of possibility (a priori, before) and the infinite task (telos, ahead) can be one structure. The Kantian transcendental is "exhausted" because its conditions were abstract and its Ideas regulative only; Husserl's horizon is a *lived* apriori whose very mode of givenness is anticipation. That the *toujours-déjà* of the horizon and the *jamais appris* of its knowledge again speak in Heideggerian cadences — being-toward the open, thrownness into the already-there — is no accident of translation: Derrida is quietly exhibiting the *Origin* as the place where Husserl's transcendentalism and the existential analytic communicate. The question he leaves in reserve is the one ¶172 flagged: whether an "evidence" whose content can never be given — the horizon-knowledge of an infinite opening — does not strain the principle of principles it is supposed to fulfill.

## **IX**

After this enlargement of the reflection to the problems of universal historicity, Husserl *narrows the field* of his analysis and returns to the origin of geometry: he proposes, in a few pages, "the most concrete descriptions of this text" — those most often retained by the commentators because, as Husserl himself underlines, they at last transgress the "*formal generalities*" and touch on the *constitution of the geometrical proto-idealities*, on the basis of *human praxis*, in the *pre-scientific sphere of the cultural world*. The sentence quietly registers a reception-history: what most readers took for the *Origin*'s substance — the famous pages on measurement, polishing, surveying — Derrida has deferred for two-thirds of his Introduction, having argued throughout that the "formal generalities" (traditionality, language, writing, historicity) are the text's true depth.

The placement of this analysis — whose content, Derrida warns, "is less new in Husserl's œuvre than it may have seemed" — appears "rigorously commanded by the procedure of the meditation, despite all the freedom of its gait." Two orderings converge. First: having determined the conditions of *traditionality in general*, one has the right to return to *one* of these traditions — which, a moment ago the *exemplary guide*, is now studied *in itself*; having fixed the sense and method of *every* question of origin, one poses *a singular* question of origin. Second: geometry was recognized as a traditional system of *ideal objectities* — and in an ideal objectity, one must account for *both the objectivity and the ideality*. These can be treated *separately*, despite their deep solidarity and reciprocal conditioning. For it is by its *objectivation* that ideality — analyzed up to now *in general* and not *as geometrical* — enters into tradition and can thus be "delivered, then handed over" (*délivrée, puis livrée* — freed into circulation). It was therefore necessary to begin, as Husserl did, by accounting for *objectivity* — that is, for the *historicity* of ideal objectity in general: the recourse to language, to writing, to the power of reactivation, and finally to the *method* by which the a priori structures of historicity could be interrogated. Thanks to this method, which alone permits circumscribing with apodictic certainty the invariants of historicity in general, "we can now go back, *on this side of science*, to the invariants of the *pre-scientific world* from which the geometrical proto-idealities were produced and founded." Thus, having defined the conditions of the *objectivity* of ideal objects, one can attempt — "by a *new reduction* of constituted scientific objectivity and of all its proper historicity" — to describe the conditions of *geometrical ideality itself*. Husserl asked earlier: how could ideal sense, *already constituted* in subjective immanence, be objectivated and engaged in history and the movement of intersubjectivity? He now asks: how, in an "*anterior*" moment, could *ideality itself* be constituted? The architecture of the whole *Origin* is here laid bare in two questions, and the apparent disorder of Husserl's meditation revealed as a strict regressive order: objectivity first (sections V–VIII of the Introduction), then ideality (sections IX–X) — each step a further reduction.

It is the necessity of such a *recursion* "through a series of '*zig-zags*'" — Husserl's own word for his method, the back-and-forth between constituted sense and constituting origin — that seems to inspire him when he writes (O., 209): following this method, we can also, surpassing the formal generalities evoked above, "take for theme *the determinate apodictic* of which the proto-founder of geometry could dispose *on the basis of the pre-scientific world*, and which had to serve him as *material for the idealizations*." Two terms organize everything to come: the *determinate apodictic* — invariant, but contentful — of the pre-scientific world; and its role as *material* (*Material*) for idealization, the act that remains to be described.

First stage: to delimit, among the a priori structures of the pre-scientific world, *those from which a geometry could be instituted*. And this description is "always possible, since the stratum of the pre-scientific world is *never destroyed nor definitively dissimulated*": it remains *intact* beneath the universe determined by the ideal exactitude of science — which is, according to an image Husserl uses at least twice, "nothing other than *a garment of ideas* (*vêtement d'idées*) thrown over the world of immediate experience and intuition, over the life-world, such that each product of science has its sense-foundation in this immediate experience and world of experience, and refers back to them. *This garment of ideas makes us take for true being what is a method*." (The footnote locates the image in *Erfahrung und Urteil* — in a paragraph precisely concerned with geometry's ideal exactitude — and in *Krisis* §9h, "The life-world as forgotten sense-foundation of natural science"; and it adds a gloss that condenses the Introduction's whole double verdict: the ambiguity of Husserl's attitude toward science — "valorized in the highest degree as project and devalorized in its superstructural precarity and its dissimulating power" — refers to "the very movement of the 'historical' constitution of sense: *the creation that unveils and the sedimentation that covers over imply one another*." Sinnbildung and Sedimentierung, ¶178's Miteinander und Ineinander, as the single root of science's grandeur and its crisis.) The *Ideenkleid* passage — the most famous sentence of the *Krisis* — is thus put in its working position: the life-world is not lost, only dressed; and the dress is mistaken for the body.

It is therefore fitting to *reduce the ideal sedimentations of science* in order to discover "the nakedness of the pre-geometrical world." This new "*epoché* of the objective sciences," whose problematic is developed in the *Krisis* (§§33–39), "is difficult on several counts" — and three numbered difficulties follow.

First, it has the difficulty of *every* reduction: it "must keep itself from being a forgetting and a negation, a subtraction or a devaluation of what it de-sediments or methodically neutralizes." To bracket science is not to despise or annul it — the constant Husserlian caution (¶¶64, 121), here applied to the hardest case, since the temptation to read the *Krisis* as anti-science is permanent.

Second: being a reduction of *objective-exact* science, it "must not make us renounce *all scientificity*." The thematization of the *Lebenswelt* must itself be "scientific" and attain a prioris "which are no longer the habitual a prioris of logic and objective science." (A note adds the lexical key: in the *Origin*, "the 'logical' always has the sense of the '*sedimented*.'") Husserl often presents this as a "*paradox*": the *Lebenswelt*, pre-objective sphere of "subjective-relative" significations, *has an unconditioned universal structure* — one "prescribed to its very relativity." And it is in the a prioris of the *Lebenswelt* that the a prioris of logic and objective science are "*rooted*" and "*founded*"; it is by naïveté that one confines oneself to the latter and ignores their "*sense-relation*" (*Sinnbeziehung*) to the life-world — without this founding relation they are "*in the air*" (*en l'air*). The paradox is the *Krisis*' deepest methodological discovery: relativity itself has invariant structure; the science of the pre-scientific is possible — indeed it alone gives the sciences their ground.

Third — and the most pointed, for it carries a barely veiled polemic. It does not suffice to *dissolve* the truths of science (Bolzano's "*truths in themselves*," as Husserl calls them): one must *ceaselessly problematize the relation* between the subjective-relative truths of the *Lebenswelt* and the objective-exact truths of science. The paradox of their mutual relation makes *both* truths "enigmatic" at once; and it is "in the insecurity of this enigma, in the instability of the *in-between*, that the epoché must hold itself in tension, *between the ἀρχή and the τέλος of a passage*." Two truths — that of *doxa* and that of *epistēmē* — whose senses and a prioris are in themselves heterogeneous, remain in a situation of "inter-relativity" (*Aufeinanderbezogenheit*); the "in itself" truth of science is nonetheless *truth-of* the subjective-relative world in which it has its foundations. There is, no doubt, a "naïve *lightness* of *Bodenlosigkeit*" — groundlessness: that of the rationalists and traditional scientists who move without discomfort in the atmosphere of the logical and objective a prioris, never relating them to their historical soil in the life-world, never asking "*what am I doing?*" and "*where does this come from?*" — the crisis-naïveté diagnosed since "But there is a naïveté just as grave, of a more *modern* style: a naïveté of *depth* and not of lightness" — it consists in "descending again toward pre-scientific *perception* *without problematizing the 'transgression'* (*Überschreitung*) of the truths of the life-world toward the world of truths 'in themselves.'" The return to the structures of pre-scientific experience "must never cease to maintain within itself the question: *How can the a prioris of scientific objectivity be constituted from the a prioris of the life-world?*" Without this question, that return — "however penetrating it may be" — risks "abdicating all scientificity in general and all philosophical dignity, for having wished to precipitate a legitimate reaction against what Husserl calls 'intellectualist *hypertrophy*.'" And if one considers that this question is "*at once historical and transcendental*, one sees to what *irresponsible empiricism* all the '*phenomenologies*' of pre-scientific perception would condemn themselves that did not let themselves be *besieged* by it." The plural in quotation marks names its target without naming it: the *Phénoménologie de la perception* and its descendance. The symmetry is exact and devastating — the objectivist forgets the ground; the phenomenologist of perception, descending to the ground, forgets the *passage*, the upward transgression by which exact truth arises from the inexact; and only the question of that passage (the *Origin*'s question) keeps the return to the life-world philosophical. Section IX thus completes, on methodological terrain, the dispute with Merleau-Ponty begun in section VIII.

A further calibration: one must also "keep from forgetting that the pre-scientific world available to the proto-geometer, and which we thus rediscover, *does not have the radicality of the ante-predicative world*" to which Husserl tries to return above all in *Erfahrung und Urteil*. (And even that ante-predicative world, the footnote notes, is not attained there in its first radicality, supposing — like *Ideen* I — "an already constituted temporality": the regression has further to go than any of Husserl's books went.) The proto-geometer's world is "a world of *culture*, already in-formed by predication, values, empirical techniques, the practice of a *measurement* and an *inductivity* that have, themselves, their style of certainty." The point matters enormously for what follows: geometry's cradle is not raw perception but an already technical, already measuring culture — idealization will be the transformation of a *praxis*, not the sublimation of pure sensibility.

This permits a final specification of "the dependence and a certain *secondarity* of our text" — those of *every* point of departure and guiding thread for a reflection on universal historicity. Certainly, the pre-scientific world is uncovered in its essential structures by a *double reduction*: of every determinate factual culture, and of the scientific superstructures that extend beyond particular cultural areas so as to free themselves from them (the two reductions of ¶64, now in descending order). But this must not make us forget that this pre-scientific *cultural* world "can in its turn be reduced in a *radical* 'epoché,'" when one wants to make one's way toward what it *always already supposes*: "the transcendental constitution of the object *in general* (before the ideal object which nevertheless serves as example-model for objectivity), the *ante-predicative* stratum of experience, the static and genetic constitution of the ego and the alter ego, *primordial temporality*, etc." These reductions, moreover, were operated in texts *prior* to the *Krisis*: already in *Ideen* I, the enlargement of the transcendental reduction extends "*by anticipation*" to the *eidetics of history*, which Husserl thought still remained to be made. Having justified the putting-out-of-play of "all eidetic domains of transcendent order," of "real physical nature," of the empirical or eidetic sciences of nature (geometry, kinematics, pure physics), Husserl wrote (§60, Derrida's emphasis): likewise, since we have excluded all the experimental sciences of animate nature and all the empirical sciences of spirit bearing on personal beings, "on men *as subjects of history* and vehicles of civilization," *we also put out of circuit the eidetic sciences corresponding to these objects* — and we do so "*in advance and in idea*; for up to now, as everyone knows, no one has succeeded in giving these eidetic sciences (for example traditional psychology, sociology) a foundation — at least a pure foundation, sheltered from all objections."

From this anticipation, the closing paragraph of section IX draws the deepest consequence about the *Origin*'s own status. One could say that Husserl "submitted *in advance* to the authentic transcendental reduction the eidetics of history that he will attempt to constitute from the *Krisis* on" — the eidetics of history is itself *bracketed* by the deeper reduction of *Ideen* I §60. "It is doubtless for this reason that the word '*transcendental*,' which Husserl almost always reserves for the pure constituting activity of the ego, *is never used in the Origin*." If Derrida has himself spoken of *transcendental historicity*, "it was to distinguish it at once from empirical history *and* from a simple eidetics of history, parallel to the other eidetics of nature and spirit." For the eidos of historicity, as explicated since the *Krisis*, "seems to *overstep* the limits that *Ideen* I had assigned to it by anticipation: its science is no longer simply one science of spirit among others. It is the science of *an activity constituting the whole sphere of absolute ideal objectivity and all the eidetic sciences*." History is not a region; it is the constituting movement of every ideal region — which is why no regional eidetic can contain it. "That this *constituting* history is itself *more profoundly constituted* — such is, beyond doubt, one of the most permanent motifs of Husserlian thought; one of the most difficult also, for it accords ill with that of a historicity which, as Husserl said more and more often, *traverses everything through and through — and first of all the ego itself*." The dilemma on which the section closes is the Introduction's structural dilemma stated in its purest form: either history is constituted (by the a-historical Living Present — but then historicity does not traverse everything), or it traverses everything including the ego (but then the constituting source is itself historical, and the regression has no resting place). The remarkable footnote adds the philological seal: the expression "*transcendental history*" appears, to Derrida's knowledge, *only once* in Husserl — in an unpublished C-manuscript (C 8 II, October 1929\) — where it names the *inter-monadic relation* (itself considered, of course, as an intentional modification of the monad in general in its primordial temporality) "thanks to which the constitution of a *common world* becomes possible"; a relation that "structurally implies the horizon of the history of spirit, past and to come," a history that "discloses to us *what perception cannot give us*." A single occurrence, in the archive's night, of the concept the whole Introduction has been constructing: history as the transcendental itself — and as what exceeds the gift of perception, hence the principle of principles.

## **X**

Section X opens on the inventory itself. "What, then, are the essential and general components of the pre-scientific cultural world? — or rather, what are, *within it*, the invariant structures that conditioned the advent of geometry?" However deep our ignorance of the historical *facts*, "we know, with an immediate and apodictic knowledge of which we can always become conscious," the following — and the first four invariants follow in a tight enumeration. (1) That this pre-geometrical world was "a world of *things* disposed according to an *anexact* space and time" — *anexact*: the positive term, soon to be elaborated, for an inexactitude that is not a defect (a note traces the idea from *Krisis* §9a to its direct inscription, in §36, "in a form identical to the one it takes in the *Origin*"). (2) That these things had to have "*corporeality*" (*corporéité*) — corporeality being a particular determination of *thingliness* (*Dinglichkeit*) in general; but since culture must already have furrowed the world — "since language and intersubjectivity must have *preceded* geometry" (a footnote observes that this justifies, "on a precise point," the *anteriority* of the *Origin*'s analyses of language and being-in-community: the order of Husserl's meditation, so often found wandering, is again vindicated) — corporeality does not exhaustively cover thingliness: "For men, existing necessarily *in community*, are not thinkable as mere bodies, and whatever the cultural objects that correspond to them structurally, these are in any case not exhausted in their corporeal being" (O., 210). (3) That these pure bodies had to have *spatial shapes*, shapes of movement, and "processes of *deformation*." (4) That to these pre-geometrical spatio-temporal shapes there must necessarily be "related," by a supplementary eidetic determination, *material qualities* (color, weight, hardness, etc.). The apodictic inventory thus runs: things, in anexact space-time, as bodies (but not only bodies), with shapes and alterations, clothed in qualities — the *Lebenswelt*'s skeleton, known a priori without a single document.

Derrida at once gives the inventory its systematic pedigree: in *Ideen* I, explaining the principles of the articulation and internal structure of the *regions*, Husserl treated these eidetic characters "as an *index*, whereas they are a *direct theme* in the *Origin*." The quotation (§72, Derrida's emphasis): the structure of the supreme concrete genus (the region) — issuing partly from disjoint supreme genera, partly from supreme genera *founded* in one another and thus mutually inclusive — corresponds to the structure of the concrete objects depending on it, formed by ultimate differences partly disjoint, partly mutually founded: "for example, in the case of the *thing*, the *temporal, spatial and material* determination." What the *Origin* excavates as the proto-geometer's world is thus exactly the regional ontology of the thing from 1913 — temporality, spatiality, materiality as the founded strata of *Dinglichkeit* — now read genetically: the *Origin*'s novelty is of theme, not of doctrine (per ¶199's warning).

From the regional placement follows the classification of geometry itself. Pure geometry and pure kinematics (and all the associated sciences of which they are here the example) "will therefore be *material* eidetics, since they have for object the thingly, then corporeal, determination of the object in general. But they are *abstract* material sciences": they treat only *certain* eidetic components of the corporeal thing in general, "abstraction made of its independent and concrete totality" — which comprises also the sensible "material" (*stofflich*) qualities and the totality of its predicates. "Spatial shapes, temporal shapes, shapes of movement are always *levied* (*prélevées*) on the totality of the perceived body." The taxonomy matters doubly: against formalism, geometry is *material* (it has a content, spatiality, and is no game of signs — ¶73's "sciences dites déductives" again); against concretism, it is *abstract* (its object is a stratum lifted from the full thing, never the thing). The word *prélevées* — levied, sampled, deducted — quietly carries the economic register: geometry lives on a withdrawal from the perceptual estate.

And a static analysis alone could therefore remind us, a priori and in all rigor, that the proto-geometer "always already disposed of *anexact* spatio-temporal shapes, of *morphological types* with '*vague*' essences, which could always give rise to a pre-geometrical *descriptive* science." One could call it "*geography*" — earth-writing, the inexact science of the *Bodenkörper* of ¶132, named in deliberate contrast to the impossible "geo-logy" of that page. On this subject, Derrida insists, "the rigor of the eidetic assertion, like that of the determination of the *vague* essences, is in no way impaired by the necessary anexactitude of the perceived object": one must guard against "the scientific naïveté that makes one consider this anexactitude of the object or concept as a '*defect*' (*tare*), as an *in*exactitude." Husserl again, in *Ideen* I (§74 — a paragraph on "descriptive and exact sciences" that a footnote calls "very important for the intelligence of the *Origin*"): the most perfect geometry and its most perfect practical mastery "can in no way help the scientist who wants to *describe* nature to express, in concepts of exact geometry, the very thing he expresses so simply, so comprehensibly, so fully appropriately, with words like *notched*, *indented*, *lens-shaped*, *umbelliform*, etc.; these simple concepts are *inexact by essence and not by chance*; for this reason also they are *non-mathematical*." Essential inexactitude: the vague morphological type (the "round," the "notched") is not a failed circle but a different *kind* of essence, with its own rigor, its own descriptive science, its own univocity. The doctrine, promised at ¶144 (the ideality of letters and phonemes) and at ¶172, is now formally in place — and with it the whole question of section X: how does the *exact* arise from the essentially *anexact*?

The fifth invariant — given its own full paragraph — names the bridge: *praxis*.
