By a necessity "which is that of praxis in daily life," certain shapes and certain processes of transformation could be "perceived, restored and *progressively perfected*: for example *rigid lines*, *smooth surfaces*." All morphological — pre-geometrical — determination operates "according to the *qualitative graduality* of sensible intuition": surfaces *more or less* polished, sides, lines or angles *more or less* coarse. Yet this does not forbid "a rigorous and univocal eidetic fixation of the vague morphological types": in the *Origin*, Husserl writes — "in a somewhat enigmatic parenthesis" — that before the appearance of exactitude, "*from facticity, an essential form lets itself be recognized by the method of variation*" (O., 211). The sense of the remark, Derrida shows, clarifies itself from *Ideen* I and the *Krisis*: by imaginative variation one can obtain *inexact but pure* morphological types — "the '*round*,' for example, *beneath* which the geometrical ideality of the '*circle*' is constructed" (the operation of "*substruction*," a notion also taken up in the *Krisis*). The "round" is already endowed with a certain ideality: it does not merge with the multiplicity of natural shapes that more or less correspond to it in perception; "only an *imaginative* aim can attain it in its pre-geometrical purity." But — the decisive boundary — "this pure ideality is of the *sensible* order," and must be carefully distinguished from pure *geometrical* ideality, "which is, in itself, unbound from all sensible *or imaginative* intuitivity." For it is imagination that delivers the pure morphological type, and "*imagination can only transform sensible forms into sensible forms again*" (*Krisis* §9a). Pure sensible ideality is therefore *pre-mathematical*; once constituted, the pure mathematical would be accessible only to an "*understanding*" (*entendement*) — a notion, Derrida notes, with no precise technical sense in Husserl, but conceivable "in the sense of *Cartesian intellectualism*, since it frees itself *at once*, as from two homogeneous faculties, from imagination *and* from sensibility." The *Krisis* says it in lines "whose precise content does not seem to be found in other Husserlian texts": in the intuitively given surrounding world, by an abstract direction of the regard fixed on spatio-temporal shapes alone, we experience "*bodies*" — not geometrically-ideal bodies but the very bodies we effectively experience; we can transform them at will in imagination, but the free and in a certain sense "ideal" possibilities thus obtained "are anything but geometrically-ideal possibilities": they are not the geometrically "*pure*" shapes that can be inscribed in *ideal space* — pure bodies, pure straight lines, pure planes, pure figures, and the movements and deformations that hold in pure figures. "*Geometrical space therefore does not signify some imaginary space.*" The architecture of idealities is now complete in three stories: empirical shape (the more-or-less round stone), pure morphological type (the "round," reached by imaginative variation, sensible still), geometrical ideality (the circle, unbound from sensibility *and* imagination alike) — and between the second and third stands a passage that no imagination can make, since imagination only trades sensible for sensible. The gap thus opened is exactly where idealization, the *passage à la limite*, must be lodged; section X's remaining task is to describe the leap.

First, however, the regression must be given its stopping rule. Although geometrical ideality is *produced from* sensible morphological ideality, "this facto-historical point of departure is *suppressed as foundation* within constituted geometry" — the ladder is internal to the genesis, not to the validity. The imaginative-sensible idealization without which geometry could not have been born "no doubt poses in its turn delicate problems of origin, of which Husserl is very conscious." (The footnote here is one of the Introduction's most remarkable asides — on *imagination* itself. Access to the origin of sensible ideality, product of the imagination, would require "a direct thematization of imagination as such"; yet imagination, "whose *operative* role is nevertheless so decisive" — Fink's category from ¶109 — "seems never to have been sufficiently interrogated by Husserl." It keeps "an ambiguous status": on one side a *derived and founded reproductive power*, on the other "the manifestation of a *radical theoretical freedom*" — it is imagination that "makes the exemplarity of the fact arise and delivers the sense of the fact out of the facticity of the fact" (the engine of all eidetics, per ¶¶188–189); presented in the *Krisis* as a faculty homogeneous with sensibility, it is "simultaneously what tears morphological ideality from pure sensible reality." And then the historical coda: it is "by beginning to thematize imagination directly, as an original lived experience, *with the help of* imagination as the operative instrument of all eidetics — by freely describing the phenomenological conditions of *fiction*, hence of the phenomenological method itself — that the *Sartrean breakthrough* (*la trouée sartrienne*) so profoundly unbalanced, then overturned, the landscape of Husserlian phenomenology *and abandoned its horizon*." Sartre's *L'imaginaire* as the moment French phenomenology turned Husserl's instrument on Husserl's blind spot — a sentence that is also a discreet self-portrait of Derrida's own procedure with writing.) Nevertheless: this origin, "though it is the origin of what precedes and conditions geometry, *does not merge with the origin of geometry itself*"; it authorizes only what was just called "geography." In every phenomenological regression toward the beginnings, "the notion of *internal* or intrinsic history and sense permits drawing *stopping-notches* (*crans d'arrêt*)" — ratchet-stops — "and articulating, if not avoiding, every *regressus ad infinitum*." The internal sense of geometry, delivered by a static analysis, "commands that the question of geometry's origin *stop at the constituted sense of what immediately conditioned geometry*": the provenance of the *pre*\-geometrical idealities can provisionally be left in shadow. Husserl says so himself (O., 200): questions like that of the clarification of geometry's origin "have their *closure* (*clôture*), which prescribes that the interrogation not transgress these pre-scientific materials." The word *clôture* returns (¶177), now in its methodological function: each question of origin is bounded by the internal sense of its constituted object; the regress is articulated into relays, not abolished — and what lies below the closure is referred to another, deeper inquiry, named in the next paragraph.

A short paragraph names the deeper inquiry to which the sub-geometrical questions are remanded: the problems of origin posed *outside* this closure — concerning the sense of *pre-exact or pre-objective spatio-temporality* — "would take their place within that new *transcendental aesthetic* that Husserl projects in particular in the Conclusion of *Formal and Transcendental Logic*" (the footnote calls those few pages "very important... for determining the *architectonic situation* of the *Origin*," adding *Meditations* §61). The reference completes the map drawn at ¶134: beneath the *Origin*'s closure lies the science of primordial temporality and pre-exact spatiality — the transcendental aesthetic in the renewed, non-Kantian sense — which is also the root of intersubjectivity; the *Origin* is architectonically *suspended* over it.

A paradox then secures the boundary between imaginative and geometrical ideality from the side of *application*. It is precisely *because* the ideality of geometrical space is *not* imaginary — hence not sensible — "that it can relate to the *total unity* of the sensible world, and that an *applied* geometry remains possible — going so far as to merge, in our eyes, with the '*true nature*' that it thereby dissimulates *at the same stroke*." (The footnote cites *Krisis* §9a: the reciprocity of passage between aprioric theory and empirie enjoys "so quotidian a confidence" that we habitually no longer distinguish the space and spatial shapes of which geometry speaks from those of empirical effectivity, "as if they were one and the same thing" — the *Ideenkleid* of ¶201 operating as perceptual habit.) For a *sensible* ideality, always born of imagination, could give rise only to "a *fantastic* space and science of space, an unforeseeable and inorganic *proliferation* of morphological types": in that case it would be impossible to affirm, as we legitimately and in all security do, that "*we have one and not a double universal form of the world, one and not a double geometry*" (§9c). The argument is elegant: imagination, being free, multiplies; only an ideality unbound from imagination can be *one*, and only what is one can clothe the one world. The unity of geometry and the unity of the world answer to each other — and the very success of application (the garment fitting so well it disappears) is what breeds the crisis-confusion of method with being.

The sensible anticipation holds not only for *shapes* but for *geometrical measurement*. Measure "takes shape by and in *praxis*" — for example from "the *intention of equity* (*équité*) *in distribution*" (O., 211): justice in the sharing of land and goods as the practical cradle of the metric. An empirical technique of measurement — in *surveying* (*arpentage*), architecture, etc. — "must necessarily belong to every pre-scientific culture." Husserl does not dwell on it in the *Origin*; but in the *Krisis* he seems to consider empirical metrics "a *stage superior* to that of sensible morphology on the path toward pure geometrical ideality," since it advances in the direction of the *univocal, intersubjective — hence ideal-objective — determination* of the geometrical thing (the notes gather *Krisis* §9a on surveying and §9h on surveying as "pre-geometrical production" that is also "*sense-pedestal* (*socle de sens*) for geometry"). And at a much later level, the *arithmetization* of geometry will be evoked as "a new revolution within geometry" — whereby "the origin of this science will only be the more deeply buried, and its sense '*evacuated*'" (*Krisis* §9f: an arithmetization that "leads, as if of itself, in a certain way, to the evacuation of its sense"; with the remarkable corroboration from *Ideen* I §70, where the "*pure*" geometer is defined as the one "*who renounces the algebraic method*"). The ladder of section X thus acquires its full set of rungs — morphology, metric praxis, idealization, then arithmetization and formalization — and the law of ¶160 holds at every rung: each advance is a leap *and* a burial, each gain in operativity a loss in reactivable sense.

A summary paragraph closes the inventory: we therefore know *a priori* that "the thing, the body, pre-exact spatio-temporality, the vague morphological and *phoronomic* types" (shapes of motion), "the metric art, and the possibility of imaginative variation *had to be already there*, in the cultural field offered to the '*philosopher who did not yet know geometry but must be imaginable as its inventor*'" (O., 211). Husserl's own formula deserves the emphasis Derrida gives it: the proto-geometer is no historical individual but an *imaginable* function — the necessary form of an inventor, deduced from the constituted sense of the invention; Erstmaligkeit (¶52) as eidetic role.

"For the instauration of geometry could only be a *philosophical* act." Husserl, who often speaks of "platonizing geometry," always assigned to this instauration "a *contemporaneity of sense* with 'the Platonic school,' 'Platonism,' the Greeks 'guided by the Platonic doctrine of Ideas,' 'Platonic idealism'" (the references run from the *Logic* through *Ideen* I to the *Krisis*; one footnote contains a caution of the greatest importance: as Husserl often remarks, the allusion to Greece, to the *Greek* origin of philosophy and mathematics, "has no external historico-empirical sense — it is the *evential index of an internal sense of origin*"; to which Derrida appends the barbed observation that "it is, of course, the whole problem of a phenomenology of history that the '*indicative*' character of such language *supposes resolved*" — "Greece" functioning as indication, the degraded sign-type of ¶150, at the very heart of the doctrine of origin; another note signals, among the most precise sketches of the relation between Plato's philosophy and the advent of pure mathematics by idealization, the text published by Boehm as Beilage VII of *Erste Philosophie* I). The philosopher is "the man who inaugurates the *theoretical attitude*" — which is nothing but "the *radical freedom* of spirit": it authorizes the surpassing of the finite and opens the horizon of knowledge as that of a *pre-having* (*pré-avoir*), a project or task (*Vorhaben*) that is *infinite*. Thereby it makes possible the decisive "*passage to the limit*" of idealization and the constitution of the mathematical field in general. And the final distinction guards the concept: this passage to the limit is "the surpassing of every sensible and factual limit — *the ideal limit of an infinite transgression*, not the factual limit of the transgressed finitude." The limit toward which idealization passes is not a far-off endpoint of the sensible series (the millionth polishing) but of another order altogether: the pole of an infinite *transgression* — which is why no imagination, confined to transforming sensible into sensible (¶213), can reach it, and why only the theoretical attitude's infinite *Vorhaben* could open it.

From the inaugural infinitization, mathematics "will know *new* infinitizations, which will be so many *internal revolutions*." For if the originary infinitization opens to the Greeks the mathematical field of infinite fecundities, it nonetheless first *limits* the a priori *system* of that productivity: the very infinity of the content of production "will be confined within an a priori system which, for the Greeks, will always be *closed*" — the guide here being Euclidean geometry, or rather the "*ideal Euclid*," Husserl's expression, which attaches to the sense and not the historical fact. Later, at the dawn of modern times, "it is the a priori system *itself* that will be overturned by a new infinitization" — but this one will take place only *within* infinity as the possibility of a mathematical apriori in general. Whence the distinction Derrida proposes: between, on the one hand, *infinitization as the founding act of mathematics* — the unveiling of mathematical *apriority itself*, the possibility of mathematization in general — and, on the other, *infinitizations as enlargements of the a priori systems*, which "would only add dimensions of infinity to the a prioris but would not concern apriority itself." In the *Origin*, it is the infinitization in the *first* sense that interests Husserl: that is why he reduces "all the a priori systems of past or present geometry, in order to regrasp at its source the origin of apriority itself — the *instituting* infinitization." The distinction — apriority versus a priori systems, the opening of the field versus the successive architectures built in it — gives ¶32's analysis its final precision, and equips Derrida for the philological problem that follows.

For there is an apparent contradiction in the corpus, "signaled by P. Ricœur," between the Vienna Lecture and the *Krisis* itself, which — Ricœur notes — "withdraws from Greek thought, and singularly from Euclidean geometry, *the glory of having conceived an infinite task of knowledge*." Did the Greeks discover the infinite task (Vienna) or only finite tasks within a closed apriori (*Krisis* §8)? The distinction of ¶220 has been forged precisely to dissolve this antinomy, and the next paragraphs will lay the two texts side by side.

Derrida concedes that his proposed difference between the two infinities "could not totally efface an opposition which, in the *literality* of the texts, remains flagrant" — and places the two most apparently irreconcilable passages side by side, quoted at length. *Text A* (Vienna Lecture): "Only Greek philosophy leads, by a development proper to it, to a science *in the form of infinite theory*, of which Greek geometry furnished, for millennia, the example and the sovereign model. Mathematics — the idea of the infinite, of infinite tasks — is like a Babylonian tower: though unfinished, it remains a task full of sense, *open onto the infinite*; this infinity has for correlate *the new man with infinite goals*"; and further: infinity is discovered "first in the form of the *idealization* of magnitudes, measures, numbers, figures, straight lines, poles, surfaces"; thenceforth nature and the intuitive world "transmute into a mathematical world"; Antiquity gave the example: with mathematics it "*for the first time discovered infinite tasks* — the star that will guide the march of the sciences for all later epochs." *Text B* (*Krisis* §8, Husserl's emphasis): the Ancients, guided by the Platonic doctrine of Ideas, had certainly *already idealized* the empirical numbers, magnitudes, figures, points, lines, surfaces, bodies, transforming geometry's propositions into ideal-geometrical ones; "more still," with Euclidean geometry was born "the extremely impressive idea of a systematically unitary deductive theory," directed toward an ideal, distant, elevated goal, resting on "axiomatic" founding concepts and propositions, progressing by apodictic consequences — "a whole issued from pure rationality." "*But* Euclidean geometry, and ancient mathematics in general, knows only *finite* tasks, *an apriori closed in its finitude*" — likewise the apriori of Aristotelian syllogistic, as the apriori to which every other is subordinated; that far Antiquity reached, but "never to the point of grasping the possibility of the *infinite task*" that for us is bound, as if self-evidently, with the concept of geometrical space and of geometry as its corresponding science. The dossier is stated at full strength: same author, same years, the Greeks credited with the discovery of infinity and denied it.

The resolution applies the distinction. The Vienna text, Derrida notes, attributes to Greek philosophy and geometry "only the infinitization *in the first sense*": the creative idealization of mathematics in general — "which will not be refused them in the *Krisis*" (Text B grants the idealizing transformation in its first sentences). There is "an infinity that surfaces with the discovery of the *apriority* of mathematics in general and the transgression of the sensible finitudes, *even if the first a priori system is in itself closed*," as the second passage says. From a finite a priori system, *within it*, "an infinity of mathematical operations and transformations are already possible, even if they are not infinitely creative." Above all: despite the closure of the system, "*we are in mathematical infinity* for having definitively idealized and surpassed the sensible and factual finitudes. The infinite infinity of the modern revolution can therefore *announce itself* in the finite infinity of the ancient creation." In becoming conscious of what they were creating — mathematical apriority — the Greeks "simply would not have become conscious of *all the powers of infinity enveloped in it*," nor, of course, of the pure and infinite historicity of mathematics; that would happen only progressively and later, "by a chain of revolutionary developments, conformably to the deep historicity of mathematics and to *a creativity that always proceeds by unveiling*." (A footnote adds the alignment: by their intention, the Vienna Lecture and the *Origin* "are closer to each other than either is to the *Krisis*" — both seek a *proto*\-origin anterior to the "Galilean" origin of modernity.) Creation as unveiling: the formula resolves the antinomy by the now-familiar rule of ¶194 — every prise de conscience is an infinitization, and each infinitization reveals retrospectively what the prior closure both opened and hid.

"If it were so, the contrast would be less abrupt between two texts of which one thematizes mathematical *apriority* and the other the a priori *system or systems* — or rather, mathematical *systematicity*." Within the infinity opened by the Greeks "a new infinitization is produced, which will make the earlier closure appear *not* as a closure paralyzing the Greeks *on the threshold* of mathematical infinity itself, but as a closure limiting them *secondarily*, within the mathematical field in general." Even in the spirit of the *Krisis*, the modern infinitization "will mark less an authentic upsurge than a sort of *resurrection* of geometry" — and, in the same movement, the law of ¶¶160 and 217 strikes again: "this renaissance to itself will at the same time be only the *new obliteration of the first birth-certificate*." The process of intra-mathematical infinitization "can moreover generalize itself thereafter to infinity, and at an accelerated rhythm." (The footnote is a small treatise on *clôture*: the *Krisis* extract poses the difficult intra-mathematical problem of closure, "a notion that can have multiple senses according to the contexts where it is employed" — S. Bachelard's chapter is cited; and there is also "a closure of the mathematical domain *in general*, in its ideal unity of mathematical sense, *within which every infinitization will have to maintain itself*, simply because it still concerns ideal-mathematical objectities": Husserl speaks of "*an infinite and yet self-enclosed world* of ideal objectities as field of work" — *Krisis* §9a. Infinite yet closed: the structure of ¶¶69–70's mathematical horizon, generalized — and a meditation on "closure" that Derrida's later "closure of metaphysics" will inherit, with the same logic of an infinity confined by the unity of a sense.)

But the resolution breeds a vertigo, which Derrida formulates in the interrogative mode of If *each* infinitization is "a new birth of geometry to its authentic originary intention — of which one then perceives that it had remained, to a certain extent, hidden by the closure of the prior system — one may ask whether it is still legitimate to speak of *one* origin of geometry. Does geometry not have *an infinity of birth-certificates*, in each of which another birth would announce itself while still dissimulating itself? *Must one not say that geometry is en route toward its origin, instead of proceeding from it?*" The question turns the *Origin*'s title against itself: if origin reveals itself only teleologically, then the origin lies ahead, not behind — the beginning is the last thing to arrive.

And Husserl, Derrida answers, "would doubtless grant it": the teleological sense and the sense of origin "have always implied one another in his eyes"; announcing themselves in one another, "they will fully reveal themselves only through one another, *at the infinite pole of history*" (the identity of archē and telos established at ¶¶93 and 173). But then the boundary questions multiply: why make geometry *begin* at pure idealization and exactitude? why not at imaginative-sensible idealization and morphological typology, "since exactitude is already anticipated there"? or, inversely, why still call "geometrical" the systems that have totally freed themselves from concrete geometry? This type of question, Derrida rules, "no doubt *relativizes* the specificity of geometrical sense as such, but does not put it in question *in itself*." The geometrical telos "is doubtless only the fragment or particular segment of a *universal* Telos that traverses, precedes and exceeds it; but the adventure of geometry articulates itself rigorously *within* it: it had not begun *as such* before the apparition of absolutely pure and non-sensible ideality; it remains the adventure *of geometry* so long as pure ideal objectities hold themselves within the field of apriority opened by the Greeks." (The footnote adds the calibration: this is true insofar as these objectities relate to spatiality in general — geometry in the strict sense — or movement in general — kinematics; though Husserl often says "geometry" is an "*abbreviation*" for all the objective and exact sciences of pure spatio-temporality; and it is true *in general* for every absolutely pure and "free" ideal objectity, if one considers geometry in its exemplarity.) Husserl can therefore *at once* speak of a pure sense and internal historicity of geometry *and* say, as he often does, that "a universal teleology of Reason was working human history *before* the Greco-European prise de conscience," that pure ideality announces itself in bound ideality, etc. "He thereby saves at one stroke the *absolute* originality of the sense or internal historicity of each traditional line *and* its 'relativity' within universal historicity. He is thus assured of penetrating universal historicity *only from within* — above all if he orients his regard, by privilege, on a tradition as 'exemplary' as that of mathematics." The segment-structure answers the vertigo of ¶225: origins are relative cuts in a universal teleology, but each cut is absolute *for its line*, defined by the upsurge of a new sense (pure ideality, for geometry); and the method of internal reading (¶154) is saved, since every line can be entered from inside. What remains standing, unreduced, is the *exemplarity* on which the whole operation leans — the privilege of the mathematical thread within the universal Telos, flagged once more in the final clause with quotation marks that have been accumulating doubt since A paragraph of capital importance fixes the ontological status of the infinite's irruption. The opening of the infinite, "far from being the access to some possible, anhistorical in itself, discovered within a history that would thereby be transfigured, is on the contrary *nothing but the opening of history itself*, in the greatest depth and greatest purity of its essence." The Greeks did not break out of history into a timeless realm; they broke history *open* — into its own essence. "Without this *tearing of the finite* (*déchirure du fini*), historical humanity — or rather the historical humanities — would have had only the socio-anthropological unity of an *empirical type*. Now, we have clearly seen it: *an empirical history does not distinguish itself essentially from a non-history*." The last sentence gathers ¶¶81–82 and ¶193 into a single law: empiricity, the finite repetition of a type, is *Geschichtlosigkeit*; history in the pregnant sense begins only with the infinite task. Infinitization is therefore not an event *in* history but the event *of* history — the same identity of truth's irruption and history's opening that ¶82 called truth "the deepest history."

Whence the methodological consequence, stated with complete equanimity: no more in the Vienna Lecture or the *Krisis* than here does Husserl "judge it necessary to account *historically* for the birth of philosophy" that conditioned geometry's birth. "This birth was that of *pure history* itself. The origin of historicity (*Geschichtlichkeit*) will never be the province of a history (*Historie*)." Although in the order of facticity the theoretical attitude is "second and intermittent" (*Erfahrung und Urteil* §14), "it would be vain to describe the phenomenological and intrinsic *genesis* of what, precisely, *founds the possibility of such a description*": the regress halts, of necessity, at the act that opens the very dimension of regressing. This does not mean, Derrida adds with deliberate generosity, that an *extrinsic*, "parallel" historical approach is impossible or useless — one mobilizing all available factual data (geographic, economic, cultural, sociological, psychological) "with the finest competence and the greatest methodological security, without yielding to causalism, atomism, etc."; one can likewise attempt "a facto-genetic description of *the most ambitious transcendental reduction itself*, with the help of all available empirical instruments." But such attempts "would have their full value only insofar as they were conducted with the certainty that one is then speaking of *everything except* the reduction *itself*, the origin of philosophy and of history *themselves and as such*. In the best of cases, one speaks of what is strictly '*parallel*' to them." The word "parallel" carries its full Husserlian charge: as the psychic is the worldly parallel of the transcendental (the doubling of ¶¶44, 64), so the sociology of the Greek miracle is the worldly parallel of the origin — exhaustively informative, and never touching the thing itself.

From the sufficiency of the conditions, the brevity of the act. Once Husserl gives himself *both* the pre-scientific cultural world *and* the philosopher as conditions of geometry's origin, "the absence of any concrete description of the *instituting acts* must not surprise. Nor disappoint. These conditions were indispensable, *but also sufficient*." And so, "in seven very allusive lines that add nothing to the prior static descriptions, the sense of the inaugural operation is exhausted." The finitudes available to the philosopher proto-geometer — among the highest of which are the "bound" idealities — and which he perceives *on a horizon of the infinite*, "are evidently, as formations born of praxis and conceived with a view to perfectioning, only the *supports* of a *praxis of a new kind*, from which formations *of similar name but of a new kind* are born. It is evident in advance that this new kind will be a product born of *a spiritual act of idealization*, of a '*pure*' *thinking* that has its *material* in the universal pre-givens, already described, of this factual humanity and human surrounding world, and *creates from them 'ideal objectities'*" (O., 212). Everything the section has assembled — the apodictic inventory (¶209), praxis and perfectioning (¶213), the theoretical attitude (¶219) — converges in Husserl's seven lines; what they do *not* contain is any description of the act itself, and the next paragraphs ask why.

"Here we are then, in the last recourse, before an *idealizing operation* whose activity is never studied for itself — *and whose conditions do not have to be* — since it is a *radically instaurative* operation." This idealization is the one that brings forth, from a sensible ideality — the morphological type "round," for example — "a superior ideality, absolutely objective, exact and non-sensible: the '*circle*,' a formation '*of similar name but of a new kind*.'" (To regrasp the original aspect of the sensible morphological ideality, one must "constantly wean oneself from the geometrical habit that tends to obscure it" — and Derrida calls Gaston Bachelard to witness, apropos his chapter "The phenomenology of roundness": "The difficulty we had to vanquish in writing this chapter was to *keep away from all geometrical evidence*" — *La poétique de l'espace*. The poet's roundness, the round of nests and of repose, must be defended against the circle.) The enormous footnote then documents that "the same principle and the same notion of *substructive idealization*" run, "without substantial further explicitation, from one end of Husserl's work to the other" — and the inventory is itself an argument: (a) the *Investigations* (II.1, §18), whose lines on idealization "the *Origin* will add nothing to": the image "serves only as *support* for the *intellectio*"; it offers no true example of the figure aimed at, only an example of the sensible category that forms the "natural points of departure" of geometrical "idealizations"; it is in these *intellective* processes that the idea of the geometrical figure is constituted — their actual effectuation being the precondition of the *first formation* of primitive geometrical expressions, "*but not* of the comprehension that reanimates them, nor of their permanent employment"; (b) *Ideen* I §74; (c) the treatise *Realitätswissenschaft und Idealisierung*; (d) *Erfahrung und Urteil* §10; (e) the *Logic* §96c and conclusion; (f) the *Krisis* §9a and §36 — where it is said in summary that "the Categorial of the life-world *has the same names* but does not aim, so to speak, at the theoretical idealizations and *hypothetical substructions* of the geometer and physicist" (Derrida's emphasis: the "similar name" formula of the *Origin*, found again); (g) Beilage III. From 1901 to 1936, the *same* doctrine, never deepened: the constancy is the symptom — idealization is phenomenology's permanent recourse and permanent unexamined remainder.

The contrast is then drawn as sharply as possible. Unlike morphological ideality, exact ideality "was produced *without the essential help of sensibility and imagination*: it detached itself, *by a leap* (*par un saut*), from every descriptive *mooring* (*amarre*)" — the mooring-lines of ¶92, now cut by the inaugural act itself. No doubt this leap "took its footing or its impetus (*son appui ou son appel*)" on sensible ideality: Husserl always speaks of the sensible "support," "substrate," "substructures" (*soubassements*) of geometry (a note adds *Ideen* I §70: the sensible type serves as *foundation* for geometry *in the course of constituting itself*; afterward, it serves only as illustrative "auxiliary" or "adjuvant" to a geometrical activity that traverses it toward pure ideality). "But the foundations (*fondations*) are not here *grounds* (*fondements*)" — the launching-pad is not the basis of validity — "although the latter must not make one forget the former." It is always a "*pure thinking*" that is responsible for the idealizing take-off and for geometrical truth as such. And then the sentence that gives the whole problem its profile: "the *inaugural* character of the idealizing act, the radical and *irruptive freedom* it manifests, the *decisory discontinuity* that tears it from its past conditions — all this *withholds it from any genealogical description*." (The footnote seconds it with the mathematician-philosopher Gonseth: "The passage from the intuitive notion — the line of sight — to the ideal notion — the straight line — is something *entirely indescribable*.") The origin of geometry, pursued through one hundred and forty pages, is reached and found to be *structurally indescribable*: not contingently undocumented (¶52) but essentially refractory to genealogy, because a creative leap has no sufficient conditions — if it did, it would not be inaugural. The aporia is not a failure of the *Origin* but its deepest finding.

"If they teach us nothing more about the *process* of idealization, are the earlier texts more precise about the origin of the idealizing *power*? It does not seem so." In its most concrete determinations, the operation is always presented as a "*passage to the limit*": from an *anticipatory structure of intentionality*, morphological ideality is surpassed "toward the ideal and invariant *pole* of an *infinite approximation*." The footnote assembles the two most precise passages. (A) *Ideen* I §74 (Husserl's emphasis): geometrical concepts are "*ideal*" concepts, expressing "something one cannot '*see*'"; their origin, and hence content, differ essentially from those of *descriptive* concepts, which express essences issuing "without intermediary from simple intuition." Exact concepts have for correlates essences with the character "*of Ideas in the Kantian sense*"; opposed to them stand the *morphological* essences, correlates of descriptive concepts. This *Ideation* erects the ideal essences as ideal "*limits*" that "*can in principle be discovered in no sensible intuition*," and which the morphological essences "approach" more or less *without ever attaining*. (B) *Krisis* §9a — "more genetic in gait," with Husserl "more sensitive to the difficulty of a description which, he thinks, *remains still to be done*": without engaging more deeply in the essential enchainments ("*which has never been done systematically and is by no means easy*"), we already understand that from the *praxis of perfectioning*, "in freely penetrating the horizon of a *conceivable* (*erdenklicher*) perfectioning," according to the "*always-again*" (*Immer-wieder*), "*limit-shapes* (*formes-limites*) are everywhere sketched, toward which, as toward *an invariant and never accessible pole*, each series of perfectioning is each time projected. In the interest we take in these ideal shapes, and in the consequent activity we devote to them — to determine them and to construct new ones from those already determined — *we are 'Geometers.'*" And likewise, for the larger sphere embracing the dimension of time, "we are mathematicians of the '*pure*' shapes (*Gestalten*), whose universal form (*Form*) is *the co-idealized form of space-time itself*. In place of *real* praxis... we now have an *ideal praxis*, that of a 'pure thinking' that holds itself exclusively in the domain of pure limit-shapes." The promised analysis (¶172) is now delivered in Husserl's own best words — and its center is exposed: the pole is *invariant and never accessible*; geometry is born when interest transfers from the accessible relative perfections to the inaccessible absolute pole. Everything therefore hangs on how an inaccessible pole can be *given* — the question of the next paragraph.

"But for the intentional anticipation itself to *leap to the infinite*, it must already be *ideal*. What at once authorizes and commands this idealization of anticipation is *the presence to consciousness of an Idea in the Kantian sense*." The Idea is the object of an *ideation* — the name Husserl often gives to idealization, and which must be distinguished from ideation as *essence-intuition* (*Wesensschau*). The difference between these two ideations: "one can *constitute* an object in a *creation*, the other *determine* it in an *intuition*. The originary geometrical ideation, for example, *makes an essence arise that did not exist before it*. It is therefore *more historical*." But once the ideal object is constituted, within ready-made geometry, the *Wesensschau* "resumes its rights." And it is no accident that the same word names two different operations: in both cases the object is an *irreal* essence, though in no way fantastic; in constituted geometry, the *Wesensschau* "only *repeats* the productive idealization." Then the circle is closed in both directions: if the geometrical *Wesensschau* is possible only because the idealizing ideation has already produced the geometrical object, "inversely, the originary passage-to-the-limit is possible only if it is *guided by an essence* that one can always anticipate and then '*recognize*,' because it is a question of *a truth of pure space*. That is why the passages to the limit are not made arbitrarily and in just any direction. That is why geometry is this extraordinary operation: *the creation of an eidetic*." Creation guided by what it creates; invention that is recognition: the paradox is total and totally Husserlian — and Derrida draws its structural consequence for history. The infinite history of geometry "will always see its unity commanded by the eidetic structure of a region — more precisely, by the unity of an abstract '*moment*' (spatiality) of a region. This unity is certainly not historical; it is empirically unmodifiable. *But it is nothing but the unity of the infinite historical development of the eidetic called geometry. It is nothing outside the history of geometry itself.*" The two final sentences hold the Introduction's whole resolution of essence and history in suspension: the eidos commands history and *is nothing* apart from it — neither Platonism (essence outside becoming) nor historicism (becoming without essence), but their strict mutual envelopment, the *Miteinander und Ineinander* of ¶178 raised to the relation of essence and time.

The limit-essences thus presuppose "the opening of the horizon and the *breakthrough* (*trouée*) toward the infinite of an '*immer wieder*' or an '*und so weiter*'" — an always-again, an and-so-forth — "which is the very movement of mathematical idealization in general." (The footnote: on the "always again" and "and so forth" as fundamental forms of idealization — "*for de facto no one can always again*" (*Logic*): the idealization consists precisely in passing from the factual impossibility of indefinite iteration to its ideal possibility; with S. Bachelard's commentary, and *Ideen* I §149, where the intuition of the "and so forth" is compared to Kantian pure intuition, "of which ideation would be only the phenomenological clarification.") If the structure of the "always once more" is fundamental here, then "a privilege must be recognized in the *protentional* dimension of intentionality, and in that of the *future*, in the constitution of space in general" — geometry is born of the future, not the present, of intentional life. But a contrast then opens between the two spaces. In *lived* space, the indefiniteness of the adumbrations (*esquisses*) is "a transcendence that one can *by essence* never master" — the thing always has further sides, and perception is structurally in arrears. The *idealized* space of mathematics, by contrast, "permits us to *betake ourselves immediately to the infinite limit* of a movement unachieved in fact": one can thus "appropriate and absolutely reduce the transcendence of every lived future *in the very gesture by which one liberates it for an infinite development*." Mathematical space "no longer knows what Sartre calls '*transphenomenality*'" — its developments "will never escape us *in right*." "That is why it can appear *more reassuring*, more ours. *But is this not also because it has become more foreign to us?*" The closing question is the *Krisis* in one line: the perfect domestication of space — no hidden sides, no surprises, total juridical possession — is identically its perfect alienation from the lived; the most "ours" is the most estranged, and the security of exactitude is the very form of the loss of sense.

The regression then runs to its deepest level. On condition of respecting and traversing in return numerous mediations, "we would thus be led back once more toward *primordial temporality*." The "always once more" that delivers exactitude "inscribes the advent of the mathematical in the *ethico-teleological prescription of the infinite task*" — and the infinite task founds itself, in turn, "in the movement of originary phenomenological *temporalization*, where the Living Present of consciousness retains itself as primordial Absolute only in *an indefinite protention*, animated and unified by *the Idea (in the Kantian sense) of the total flux of lived experience*." (The footnote attached here is among the Introduction's richest: it cites *Ideen* I §83 — "the grasp of the unitary flux of lived experience conceived as an '*Idea*'" — and declares this Idea "*the common root of the theoretical and the ethical*." Finite objective ethical values are no doubt constituted, in Husserl, by a theoretical subject (Levinas and Berger are credited with establishing the point); "but at a deeper level, theoretical consciousness is nothing other, in itself and well understood, than a *practical* consciousness: consciousness of infinite task and position of absolute value, for oneself and for humanity, as rational subjectivity." Husserl himself: "*To be reasonable is to will to be reasonable*... Reason does not suffer being distinguished into 'theoretical,' 'practical' or aesthetic... *To be human is to be in a teleological sense — it is to have-to-be* (*devoir être*)." The unity of Reason in all its uses, "instead of manifesting itself fully in the practical function, as would be the case in Kant, would do so, according to Husserl, in the *theoretical* project" — whence the call for a systematic confrontation of Husserl with Kant on one side, *Fichte* on the other.) Then, the footnote absorbed, the main text performs the Introduction's final analysis of the Living Present — its most concentrated page on time. "We have seen it: the Living Present is the phenomenological absolute *out of which I can never go*, because it is that *in* which, *toward* which, *from* which every going-out is effected." Now the Living Present has the irreducible originality of a Now, foundation of a Here, "only if it *retains* — in order to distinguish itself from it — the past Now *as such*": as the *past present of an absolute origin*, instead of merely succeeding it in an objective time. But this retention "would not be possible without a *protention which is its very form*": first, because what it retains is a Now that was itself an original project, itself retaining another project, and so on; second, because retention is always the essential modification of a Now "always in suspense (*en haleine*), always tense toward a next Now." The Absolute of the Living Present is therefore "only the indefinite *Maintenance*" — maintaining, and *main-tenance*, the now-holding (*maintenant*) — "of this double envelopment." But — the decisive step — this Maintenance appears *as such*, is the Living Present, has the phenomenological sense of a consciousness, "only if the unity of this movement gives itself as *indefinite*, and if its sense of indefiniteness *announces itself in the Present*" — only if the opening of the *infinite future* is, as such, "a possibility *lived* as sense and as right." Otherwise — the extraordinary aside — "*death* would be understood not as *sense* but as extrinsic *fact* of temporalization": for finite consciousness to live its own time as sense, the indefiniteness of the flux must be given, and death thereby interiorized as meaning rather than suffered as interruption. "The unity of infinity, condition of this temporalization, *must therefore be thought*, since it announces itself *without appearing and without being contained in a Present*. This thought unity, which makes possible the phenomenalization of time as such, is therefore once again *the Idea in the Kantian sense, which never phenomenalizes itself*." The result is vertiginous and exactly stated: at the source of all appearing — the temporalization of the Living Present — operates a unity that *never appears*; phenomenality itself is made possible by a non-phenomenon. The Idea is no longer one recourse among others at "difficult moments" (¶172): it is the condition of the Urquelle itself, lodged in the heart of the principle of principles.

Hence the famous evocation of the night manuscripts. The unfinishedness of Husserl's reflections on primordial temporality has long been underlined — "their richness, but also the dissatisfaction in which, it is said, they left their author." If the manuscripts of *Group C* "so rightly fascinate Husserl's commentators, is it not because they touch the deepest region of phenomenological reflection — where *the shadow risks being no longer the provision of appearing*, the field offered to phenomenal light, *but the forever nocturnal source of light itself*?" And: "is it not in this *essential shadow* that the Idea, and the idealizing power that occupies us here exemplarily as origin of the mathematical, hold themselves in reserve?" The chiaroscuro of ¶¶129, 164, 171 culminates: in the doctrine of horizons, shadow was always penultimate — the not-yet-seen, light's reserve; at the level of the C-manuscripts, the shadow threatens to become *originary*, the source of light rather than its margin. Husserl's critique of "depth" (¶164) meets here its internal limit: the deepest region of his own analysis is structurally nocturnal.

The diagnosis is then formalized. The Idea in the Kantian sense, "regulative pole of every infinite task, assumes diverse but always analogous and decisive functions at several points of the Husserlian itinerary" — Ricœur rightly grants it "*a mediating role between consciousness and history*." And yet, "while marking it with the highest and most constant teleological dignity, while according a growing attention to what it conditions, Husserl *never made the Idea itself the theme of a phenomenological description*. He never directly defined its *type of evidence*" — within a phenomenology whose "principle of principles" and archetypal form of evidence are "the immediate presence of the thing itself '*in person*'": that is, implicitly, of the thing *defined or definable* in its phenomenon — "*hence of the finite thing*." Whence the parenthesis that splits phenomenology down the middle: the motif of *finitude* has "perhaps more affinity than first appears with the principle of a phenomenology that would thus be *cloven* (*fendue*) between the *finitist* consciousness of its principle and the *infinitist* consciousness of its final foundation — an '*Endstiftung*' indefinitely deferred in its content but always evident in its regulative value." The attached footnote is a complete essay on the Husserl–Heidegger dialogue and must be given at length. An essential finitude can come to light in phenomenology *in another sense*: to recognize the necessity for the transcendental reduction to remain *eidetic* (to avoid empirical idealism) — the necessity of making the absolute foundation of the sense of being appear *in a region*, the region "consciousness," unified by an ego and an Idea, a region which, though *Ur-Region*, "is nonetheless a domain of determinate beings" — is to recognize that transcendental idealism, "according to the Kantian tradition itself, does not go without the affirmation of a *radical finitude of the philosopher*. The unitary foundation of all the regions can appear only *in* a region; it can therefore only *dissimulate itself* under a determinate type of beingness *at the very moment it appears to itself as foundation*. Without this *occultation*, philosophical discourse would renounce all eidetic rigor — that is, all sense." Eidetic limitation is thus indispensable, and the reduction receives its true sense, "which, contrary to appearances, is that of *prudence and critical humility*." Without this *disappearing of the foundation, necessary to appearing itself* — without this limitation within a certain regionality, without this reduction "that Heidegger implicitly reproaches it for" — Husserl thinks philosophy falls back even more surely into regionality: indeed into *empirical* regionality, "for example in the form of anthropological facticity — here, Husserl thinks, that of *Dasein*." At this point, Derrida writes, "the dialogue between Husserl and Heidegger could be indefinite" — unless one considers that the reduction is *always already supposed as an essential possibility of Dasein*, and that, inversely, consciousness, as transcendental source, "is *not* a 'region' in the strict sense, even if the necessity of an eidetic language must make it be considered as such." And the last word, on which the two adversaries are made to agree: "For Husserl and for Heidegger, *the complicity of appearing and dissimulation* appears in any case *originary, essential and definitive*." The footnote quietly accomplishes what the entire French reception had attempted: a non-polemical formula for the Husserl–Heidegger relation, in which Husserl's "naïve" regionalism is revealed as critical humility, Heidegger's reproach is answered and reversed (Dasein as facticity, from Husserl's side), each position is shown translatable into the other (reduction as possibility of Dasein; consciousness as non-region) — and both are gathered under one law, *Verbergung* within *Unverborgenheit*, the self-concealing of the ground in its very appearing, which is as much the late Heidegger's lethe as it is Husserl's occulted *Ur-Region*.

"It is no accident that there is no phenomenology of the Idea." The Idea cannot give itself *in person*; it cannot be determined in an evidence — "for it is only *the possibility of evidence* and *the opening of 'seeing' itself*: it is only *determinability* as the horizon of every intuition in general, *the invisible milieu of seeing*," analogous to "the diaphaneity of the Aristotelian *Diaphane* — elemental third, yet single provenance, of sight and the visible." Derrida quotes the *De Anima* (418b): "though visible, \[the diaphanous\] is not visible by itself but by the aid of a foreign color"; it is thanks to it alone "that the color of every object is perceived." The transparent medium that makes color visible while never being seen as such: the most exact figure imaginable for the Idea — and a vertiginous one, since it lodges at the heart of phenomenology, the philosophy of light and seeing, an Aristotelian element older than the distinction of subject and object. "If there is *nothing to say* of the Idea itself, it is because it is *that from which something in general can be said*. Its original presence therefore cannot belong to the type of phenomenological evidence." Despite the multiplicity of references to the Idea in the late writings, the most precise text on its type of evidence is found — symptomatically — far back, in *Ideen* I's chapter on the phenomenology of Reason (§143), apropos the adequate givenness of the *transcendent thing*: a problem analogous to that of the total unity of the immanent flux. Though the transcendent natural thing cannot be given "with integral determination and equally integral intuitivity, *in any closed, finite consciousness*," nevertheless "*the perfect givenness of the thing is prescribed as an 'Idea' (in the Kantian sense)*." The thing itself, the X of perception, already had the structure now found at history's pole: complete givenness as prescribed, never possessed.

This Idea of the *infinite determinability of the same X* — like that of the world in general — "*prescribes by virtue of its essence a proper type of evidence*" (§143). But this evidence of the Idea as regulative possibility "is absolutely *exceptional* in phenomenology: it has no proper content — or rather, it is *not the evidence of a content of the Idea*. It is evidence only insofar as it is *finite* — that is, here, *formal* — since the content of the infinite Idea absents itself and refuses itself to every intuition." Husserl's own sentence is quoted as the resolution in paradox: "*The idea of an infinity motivated by essence is not itself an infinity; the evidence that this infinity can by principle not be given does not exclude, but rather demands, that the Idea of this infinity be given with evidence.*" (A note adds the *Logic* §16c's passing promise of "this infinite anticipation that must be elucidated phenomenologically (an anticipation which, as such, has *an evidence of its own*)" — a promise, Derrida observes, never gone beyond, the note itself referring back to *Ideen* I.) The structure is finally exact: of the infinite, the *Idea* is finitely evident; the evidence of non-givability and the givenness of the Idea require each other.

A short paragraph distills it: in the Idea of infinity, "there is determined evidence only *of the Idea*, but not of *that of which it is the Idea*. The Idea is the pole of *a pure intention, empty of every determined object*." And therefore: "*it alone reveals the being of the intention: intentionality itself*." Where every fulfilled act shows an object, only the structurally unfulfillable intention shows *intending as such* — the Idea is phenomenology's mirror, the one place where its fundamental concept appears in person precisely because nothing else does.

"Thus, for once, in a specific evidence, *nothing appears*. What appears is only the *regulative possibility of appearing*," and the *finite certainty* of infinite phenomenological determinability — "that is, *a certainty without corresponding evidence*. By definition, nothing can be added to this formal determination of the Idea": as infinite determinability of the X, the Idea "is only *the relation to the object*. It is, in the broadest sense, *objectivity itself*." The principle of principles is thus formally breached at one point — a certainty *without* evidence — and that point turns out to be nothing marginal: it is objectivity as such, the theme of the entire *Origin*.

The confrontation with Kant can now be settled. In his article *Kant et Husserl*, Ricœur writes that "the distinction, fundamental in Kant, between *intention* and *intuition*" is "*totally unknown in Husserl*" (the footnote quotes the article's key passage — Kant radically dissociates the *relation to* something and the *vision of* something; "the *Etwas* \= X is an intention without intuition," the distinction underlying that of thinking and knowing — and summarizes its strong thesis: Kantianism "limits and founds" phenomenology while Husserl "does" it, down to the Kantian re-reading of the Fifth Meditation, where the practical determination of the person by respect must precede the theoretical constitution of the alter ego. A final caveat sets aside the familiar Husserlian *empty* intentions — symbolic, disappointed, unfulfilled aims — whose "vacancy is circumscribed," always referring to a determinate intuition *in its absence*: they are not what is at issue.) Derrida's adjudication is finely balanced. "In fact, such a distinction is never *thematic* in Husserl" — granted. An intention in which nothing gives itself cannot, as such, have a phenomenological character, and Husserl cannot describe it concretely — "*at least in its content*; for its *form* is a concrete and lived evidence, *which is not the case in Kant*." (In this sense, a phenomenology "cannot, as such, found itself in itself, nor itself indicate its own limits.") But then the question is turned back on Ricœur: is not the certainty — without determinate material evidence — of the infinite determinability of X "*an intention without intuition*, an empty intention that *founds*, and distinguishes itself from, every determinate phenomenological intuition? Is it not the same for the consciousness of infinite task and for teleological certainty in all its forms?" This pure sense of intention, this *intentionality*, is assuredly "the last thing a phenomenology can describe directly" — otherwise than in its finite acts, intuitions, results, objects; "but *without wishing or being able to describe it*, Husserl nevertheless *recognizes* it, *distinguishes* it, and *posits it as the highest source of value*." He situates "the space in which consciousness *signifies to itself* the prescription of the Idea and thus recognizes itself as transcendental consciousness *through the sign of the infinite*: the *interval* between the Idea of infinity, in its formal-finite but concrete evidence, and the infinity itself of which one has the Idea. It is from this *horizon-certainty* that the historicity of sense and the development of Reason are set free." Against Ricœur: the Kantian distinction is *operative* in Husserl, at the precise point of the Idea. Beyond Ricœur: in Husserl, unlike Kant, even the empty intention has a *lived, concrete* evidence of its form — phenomenology absorbs its outside as a mode of experience. And the vocabulary of the resolution — consciousness *signifying to itself* through the *sign* of the infinite, across an *interval* — says discreetly what *La voix et le phénomène* will say aloud: at its origin and at its pole, presence is constituted through the sign, through the relation to what is not present; the philosophy of intuition rests on an irreducible moment of *signitive* relation.

Section X closes by drawing the architectonic moral. This perhaps helps us understand why the Idea in the Kantian sense — and here, the mathematical idealization that presupposes it — "could only be *operative* and not thematic concepts" (the footnote referring once more to Fink's "so illuminating" distinction). This phenomenological non-thematization "obeys a profound and irreducible necessity. The Idea is *that from which a phenomenology institutes itself* in order to accomplish the final intention of philosophy. That a phenomenological determination of the Idea itself is, from then on, *radically impossible* — this signifies perhaps that *phenomenology cannot reflect itself in a phenomenology of phenomenology*, and that its *Logos* can never appear *as such*, can never give itself to a philosophy of *seeing*, but only — *like all Speech* (*comme toute Parole*) — *be heard through the visible*." The *Endstiftung* of phenomenology, "its ultimate critical jurisdiction, what tells it its sense, its value and its right, is therefore never directly commensurable with a phenomenology." At least — the recuperative movement, made one last time — phenomenology can give access to itself *in a philosophy*, insofar as the Endstiftung "*announces itself* in a concrete phenomenological evidence, in a concrete consciousness that *makes itself responsible* for it despite its finitude, and insofar as it founds a transcendental historicity and intersubjectivity. It is from this *anticipation lived as a radical responsibility* that Husserlian phenomenology sets out. This does not seem to be the case for the Kantian critique considered in its letter." The chapter's findings are thus sealed in three theses of immense consequence: phenomenology rests on what it cannot thematize (Fink's operativity raised to a law of essence); it cannot close on itself in a phenomenology of phenomenology — the reduction of the reduction of ¶109 receiving here its reason; and its Logos is not *seen* but *heard* — the metaphorics of light and intuition yielding, at the foundation, to those of speech and audition. Phenomenology survives the discovery, but as a philosophy *of responsibility before what never appears* — anticipation lived as radical responsibility — rather than as a pure philosophy of vision: which is to say that its superiority over Kant is purchased by admitting, at its ground, precisely what the principle of principles excluded.

## **XI**

The final section opens by confronting head-on the suspicion that section X has bred. That *the presence of the Idea alone authorizes the leap to the pure ideality of the limit-shape* and the advent of the mathematical "could give rise to doubts about the *specific historicity* of this origin." Do we not stand "before *an anhistorical Idea* on the one hand, and its *insertion* into the event and the historical fact on the other?" One would then collide "with the very reefs Husserl precisely wants to avoid, and one would *miss phenomenological history*." The danger is the relapse into Platonism diagnosed throughout: an eternal Idea descending into time, idealization as the contingent meeting of a timeless essence with a datable event. And Derrida states the corrective thesis that will govern the whole section: "In truth, it is of the *profound historicity of the Idea* that one must try to become conscious." Not: how does the eternal Idea enter history? — but: how is the Idea *itself* historical through and through?

The thesis is developed by a controlled redefinition of eternity. No doubt the Idea, and the Reason "hidden in history and in man as *animal rationale*," are *eternal* — "Husserl says so often. But this eternity *is nothing but a historicity*. It is the possibility of history itself. Its *supra-temporality* — with regard to empirical temporality — *is nothing but an omnitemporality*." (The omnitemporality of ¶¶51, 112: not above time but coextensive with all time, the mode of being of the ideal object now extended to the Idea and to Reason.) "The Idea, like Reason, *is nothing outside the history where it exposes itself* — that is, where, in one and the same movement, it *unveils itself and lets itself be threatened*." The sentence rejoins ¶233's "nothing outside the history of geometry itself" and gives it its sharpest edge: to *expose* (s'exposer) is at once to manifest and to imperil — the Idea's appearing and its vulnerability (the crisis, the forgetting, the disappearance of ¶150) are one act. There is no safe eternal residence from which the Idea would descend unscathed; its only being is its exposure.

From this, the necessity of a *responsible subjectivity*. Since the Idea "is nothing outside history *but the sense of all history*, only a *historico-transcendental subjectivity* can make itself responsible for it." Thus Husserl, in the *Cartesian Meditations* (I, §4), speaks of a *final sense* (*Zwecksinn*) of science to be unveiled as a "*noematic phenomenon*." In the revelation of the Idea by the acts of a transcendental subjectivity, "*progressivity* is not an extrinsic contingency affecting the Idea, but the imperative *prescription of its essence*." (The footnote: that the Idea is not immediately graspable in its evidence "is just as well the sign of its profound historicity" — and the developed title of "Philosophy as Mankind's Self-Reflection" is quoted to confirm that the movement of Reason to realize itself "through degrees of development" *requires as its own function* that this becoming-conscious itself develop by degrees.) The Idea "is *not* an Absolute existing *first* in the plenitude of its essence and *descending* into a history, or revealing itself to a subjectivity whose acts would not be *intrinsically indispensable* to it." (A second footnote of capital importance: Husserl rigorously distinguishes the *Idea* from the *eidos* — the Idea is therefore *not the essence*; whence the difficulty, already signaled, of an *intuitive grasp* and an *evidence* of what is neither a being nor an essence. But one must also say of the Idea that "*it has no essence*, for it is only the *opening of the horizon* for the apparition and determination of every essence. Invisible condition of evidence, it *loses, in saving sight, the reference to seeing indicated in the eidos*" — the very word *eidos* deriving from *idein*, to see — "a notion from which it nonetheless issues, in its mysterious Platonic hearth. *The Idea can only be heard* (*s'entendre*)." The audition/vision reversal of ¶243 is here grounded etymologically and ontologically: the eidos is seen, the Idea only heard, because the Idea is the invisible medium of all seeing.) If the Idea descended ready-made, one could say of all transcendental historicity that it is only an "*empirical history*... used as a *developer* (*révélateur*, in the photographic sense) of essential enchainments" (Cavaillès). The polemic with Cavaillès is now joined: against the descent-model, the acts of subjectivity are *constitutive* of the Idea's very being, not the occasion of its appearing.

The grammatical meditation that follows is one of the Introduction's subtlest, and it carries the whole reconciliation of the Absolute with intentional historicity. These essential enchainments "would be impossible, would be *nothing*, without a transcendental subjectivity and its transcendental historicity." The Absolute of the Idea, "as *Telos* of an infinite determinability, *is the Absolute of intentional historicity*"; and "the *of* (*de*) designates *neither a simply objective genitive nor a simply subjective genitive*." It is neither an objective Absolute, independent, revealing itself to an intention relative to it, awaiting and conforming to it; nor a subjective Absolute, creating sense and assimilating it in its interiority. It is "the *intentional Absolute of the objectivized*, of the *pure relation to the object* — a relation in which subject and object *engender and govern one another reciprocally*." If the *of* announces neither objective nor subjective genitive, "it is because it is a question of the Absolute of *genitivity itself*, as the pure possibility of a genetic relation" — a relation that can mark the genealogical *secondarity and dependence of the subject* as well as those of the object, "marking thus, by the very opening of its indetermination, *their originary interdependence*." The indeterminacy of the genitive is not a vagueness but the precise name for a relation prior to the terms it relates: neither idealism nor realism, but the constituting correlation as such — the "intentional" of intentionality raised to an absolute, in which neither pole precedes the other. From this Derrida draws a polemical consequence against Cavaillès: why must one *choose*, as Cavaillès thought, between an "*absolute logic*" and a "*transcendental logic*," or between "*a consciousness of progress*" and "*a progress of consciousness*"? — "all the more so since the *dialecticity of genesis* that Cavaillès opposes to the 'activity' of Husserlian consciousness is precisely and abundantly described by Husserl, at diverse levels and although the word is never pronounced." The most celebrated objection of post-war French epistemology — that phenomenology, anchoring objectivity in a *creative subjectivity*, cannot think the autonomous dialectic of conceptual becoming — is met by the claim that Husserl's own analyses of temporalization *are* that dialectic, unnamed.

The claim is then made good. "We have seen how much this 'activity' of consciousness was at once *anterior and posterior* to a passivity" (¶161); how "the movement of primordial temporalization, ultimate foundation of all constitution, was *dialectical through and through*"; and how, "as all authentic dialecticity requires, it was only the *dialectic of the dialectic* — the indefinite, mutual and irreducible implication of protentions and retentions — *with the non-dialectic*: the absolute and concrete identity of the Living Present, universal form of all consciousness." The formula "dialectic of the dialectic with the non-dialectic" is Derrida's precise statement of the structure he reads in the Living Present (¶235): temporal alteration (retention/protention) enveloped within a self-identity that is not itself dialectical — neither pure flux nor pure self-presence, but their co-implication. If the Absolute of transcendental history is indeed, as the *Origin* says, the "*living movement of the solidarity and mutual implication* (*Miteinander und Ineinander*) *of originary sense-formation and sense-sedimentation*" (¶178), "then the *creative activity* of sense implies *in itself* a *passivity* with regard to the constituted and sedimented sense — which itself appears and acts as such only in the *project of a new creativity*." What Cavaillès judged impossible or "difficult to admit for phenomenology, where precisely the motor of research and the foundation of objectivities are the relation to a *creative subjectivity*," is "precisely what Husserl describes in the *Origin* and each time the theme of *sedimentation* is at the focus of his reflection." In Cavaillès's own terms, Husserl shows that a subjectivity "*normed*" in its Present by a constituted objective sense — its "absolute logic" — "*attaches* its norms to a *superior subjectivity*," that is, to *itself* in the creative movement by which it surpasses itself, produces a new sense that will also be the moment of a *superior becoming-conscious*, in which the past sense, sedimented and at first retained in a sort of objectivist attitude, will be "*awakened to its dependence* on living subjectivity," and so on. The reading turns Cavaillès's dichotomy into a single spiraling movement: consciousness of progress *and* progress of consciousness are the same sedimentation-and-reactivation, the leap-and-fall of ¶160 now recognized as the dialectic Cavaillès demanded and denied to Husserl.

Derrida presses the rapprochement to its most pointed claim. Husserl "seems never to have thought that it was '*abusing the singularity of the absolute to reserve to it the coincidence between constituting moment and constituted moment*'" (Cavaillès again). "Simply, this *coincidence* is, in his eyes, only the absolute *unity of the movement of sense*" — that is, "the unity of the *non-coincidence* and the *indefinite co-implication* of the constituted moment and the constituting moment, in the absolute identity of a Living Present that *projects and maintains itself dialectically*." The decisive nuance: Husserl does not assert a static *coincidence* of constituting and constituted (which would indeed abuse the absolute), but a *unity-of-non-coincidence* — the two moments never collapse into one, yet are never separable; their gap *is* the movement, and the movement *is* the Living Present. Cavaillès's objection is thus not refuted but absorbed: the dialectic he opposed to Husserl is rebaptized as the inner structure of the Husserlian absolute. (That Derrida performs this absorption by *introducing* the word "dialectic" where Husserl never used it, and by the Tran-Duc-Thao-inflected vocabulary the early sections flagged, marks the limit of the rapprochement: it is Husserl read forward, toward a dialectic of constitution that the letter of the texts withholds.)

A methodological caution, in the form of a numbered return to fundamentals. "Of course, all this remains paradoxical and contradictory so long as one does not cease to consider — implicitly or not — *the Idea as something* (*quelque chose*) and *Reason as a power* (*un pouvoir*)." One must therefore constantly return: *first*, to Husserl's concrete descriptions of "the *non-real* inclusion of the noema in consciousness, the *ideality of noematic sense*" — which is "neither of the subject nor of the object, *and is therefore nothing but the objectivity of the object*, the appearing of its '*as such*' for a consciousness"; and of "the *non-fantastic irreality of the eidos*, which is nothing other than *the sense and the possibility of the factual reality* to which it always relates, immediately or not, as the rigorous prescription of its essential mode of appearing." The warning identifies the source of every Platonist misreading: hypostasis — taking the Idea for a *thing*, Reason for a *faculty* (the "psychological phantom" of ¶252). The noema (¶105) and the eidos are recalled as precisely *not* beings but structures of appearing-for-consciousness, so that the Idea, a fortiori, cannot be a super-being. Then the conditional that names the stakes: "if one admits for a single instant — even as an irreducible *presumption* — that there is *in Husserl* what there perhaps was not even *in Plato*, except in the literality of his myths and his pedagogy: namely a '*Platonism*' of the eidos or of the Idea, then the whole phenomenological enterprise becomes, *especially when it is a question of history, a novel* (*un roman*)." The provocation is double-edged: a Platonist Husserl would be a fabulist — but so, perhaps, would be a Platonist *Plato*, the "Platonism" of separated Forms being itself possibly only the literal residue of myth and pedagogy. "The Idea is still *less a being* than the eidos, if that is possible; for the eidos is an object *determinable and accessible to a finite intuition*. The Idea is not. It is always '*epekeina tēs ousias*'" — beyond beingness, *Republic* 509b. "*Telos* of the infinite determinability of being, it is only the *opening of being to the light of its phenomenality*: light of light, sun of the visible sun, *hidden sun that shows without showing itself*, of which a Plato *muffled beneath Platonism* doubtless spoke to us." The closing image rescues Plato *from* Platonism: the Good beyond being, the sun that makes seeing possible while remaining unseen (¶238's Diaphane, now in its Platonic original), is read not as the arch-Form but as the first name for the Idea-as-horizon, the invisible condition of visibility — Derrida claiming for Husserl's operative Idea a Platonic ancestry older and stranger than the doctrine of two worlds.

The second return — to "the *Husserlian notion of Reason*." The "*hidden Reason*" — even if certain expressions sometimes suggest it — "is not a power dissimulated in the shadow of a historical subjectivity, or in the *back-world* (*arrière-monde*) of a becoming. It is not an eternity *at work in* a history." Why not? "First, because *without Reason there is no history*" — no pure transmission of sense as tradition of truth. "Next, because, *reciprocally, there is no Reason without history*" — without the concrete and founding acts of transcendental subjectivity, its objectivations and sedimentations. The reciprocity forbids both hypostases. For "when one speaks of Reason hidden *in man*, it is difficult to free oneself from the *psychological phantom of faculty and power*; when one speaks of Reason hidden *in history*, one has trouble effacing the *imaginative schema of noumenal substance*." Held to these speculative prejudices, "either history would have only an empirical and extrinsic signification, or Reason would be only a *myth*; one would have, once more, to *choose between Reason and History*." And Derrida recalls the early antidote: "very early, in his critique of psychologism and in the motif of the '*return to the things themselves*' as the advent of '*true positivism*,' Husserl invited us to chase away the spectre of the *faculties of the soul* and all the vestiges of the classical *substantialisms*." (A long footnote extends the point to the transcendental Ego itself: it "has no other content than the empirical I, and has moreover *no proper real content* — though it is *not* the abstract form of a content either," as many false problems suppose; "in its most radical moment, every transcendental reduction gives access to a subjectivity *historical through and through*"; and Husserl is quoted from a 1930 letter: "with the transcendental reduction I attained... *concrete and real* subjectivity in the ultimate sense, in all the plenitude of its being and life... *absolute subjectivity in its historicity*." The transcendental is not the bloodless residue of an abstraction but the concrete historical life itself, regrasped.) The anti-substantialist motif of 1900 thus pre-emptively dismantles the Platonist reading of the late teleology: Reason is no hidden faculty, no world-soul, because Husserl's first gesture was to abolish faculties and souls.

The positive determination follows. "If Reason is only the *essential structure of the transcendental ego and nous*, it is, like them, *historical through and through*" (the footnote: "*Reason is not a faculty having the character of an accidental fact*... it is *a form of universal and essential structure of transcendental subjectivity in general*" — *Cartesian Meditations* §23). "*Inversely, historicity as such is rational through and through.*" The chiasmus completes the reciprocity of ¶251: all Reason is historical, all historicity rational — neither term names a thing, each names the structure of the other. "But the being that *articulates* Reason and History between them is a '*sense*,' a *teleological having-to-be* (*devoir-être*) that constitutes being as a *movement*." It is "in this problematic that the last pages of the *Origin* engage themselves" — and Husserl is quoted (O., 213): "Do we not then stand before the great and profound problematic horizon of *Reason* — that same Reason which functions in *every* man, however primitive he still is, *as animal rationale*?" Every factual type of humanity has this essence of *animal rationale*; each has "*a root in the essential component of the universal human, a root in which announces itself a teleological Reason traversing all historicity through and through*.
