Thus is indicated an original problematic bearing on the *totality* of history and on the *total sense* that, in the last instance, gives it its unity." Reason is not above the primitive but already in him as the root of an infinite task not yet awakened — the doctrine of ¶193's *Stufen* given its ground in a single universal-human *animal rationale*.

"The first philosophical act (like the first geometrical act that presupposes it) is *only the becoming-conscious of this historical rationality* '*in its incessant movement to elucidate itself*.'" Teleological Reason "already inhabited humanity in its empirical types *before* the philosophical becoming-conscious that made it be born to itself, announcing to history *the pure sense of historicity — that is, its very sense*." (The footnote gives the great formula: philosophy is "nothing other, through and through, than *rationalism diversifying itself* according to the planes where intention and accomplishment deploy themselves; it is the *Ratio* in its incessant movement to *elucidate itself* (*Selbsterhellung*), from the first irruption of philosophy into a humanity *whose innate reason had until then remained inaccessible to itself, plunged in confusion and night*.") The becoming-conscious "of what was already there marks a *rupture* and, consequently, a *radical and creative origin*. Every birth-to-itself of a latent intention is a *re-birth*." (Footnote: just as man, "even the Papuan, represents a new stage in animality by opposition to the beast, so philosophical reason represents a new stage in humanity and in its reason" — the Vienna Lecture, whose evolutionary-Eurocentric figure Derrida cites without softening.) Come to itself, philosophical Reason "can then exercise only the '*archontic*' function of *commencement and command* (*commandement*)." The Greek *archē* as both beginning and rule (¶205's *archē*/*telos*): the philosopher "must *command* insofar as he accedes to the *demand of the Logos*; insofar as he *responds* to it and *answers for* it (*y répond et en répond*), taking upon himself the responsibility of a *mandate*. It is in this sense that Husserl defines him as the '*functionary of humanity*.'" The *Funktionär der Menschheit* of ¶167 returns as the figure of responsibility: the philosopher commands only by obeying, his authority identical with his answerability before a Logos that has summoned him.

But who is the "*self*" of this *self*\-elucidation (*Selbsterhellung*)? "Is human transcendental consciousness only the place of *reflexive articulation* — the *mediation* of a Logos repossessing itself *through* it?" Some late manuscripts could suggest this, according to which the "*absolute Logos*" would be "*beyond transcendental subjectivity*" (E III, 4). But this "beyond," Derrida argues, "if it designates only a *teleological* transcendence, can very well *not dispossess* historical transcendental subjectivity of the absolute of the *Self*; for the Logos, always having the *form of the Telos*, its transcendence would not be a *real* transcendence, but the ideal *Pole* for the accomplishment of transcendental subjectivity *itself*." That is what "other passages suggest, whose letter is without doubt more conformable to all Husserl's most permanent intentions" (footnote: in the same fragment the Logos' transcendence is defined as "a *transcendental norm*, infinitely distant Pole, *Idea of an absolutely perfect transcendental omni-community*"). The reading is decisive for the section's theology: the "beyond" of the Logos is not an existent God transcending consciousness from without, but the *telic pole* that consciousness sets infinitely ahead of *itself* — transcendence as the structure of the infinite task, not as a separate substance. A long footnote draws out the historical irony: since the Idea here has a *transcendental* sense and is "beyond" only with regard to the *constituted* moment of transcendental subjectivity, "Husserl recovers in depth the *original scholastic sense* of the transcendental" — *unum, verum, bonum* as the *transcategorial* of Aristotelian logic, the medieval *transcendentia* — "*beyond* the Kantian acceptation, but also as a *development* of the Kantian enterprise." The transcendental thus folds its whole history into one point: scholastic (the transcendentals convertible with being), Kantian (conditions of possibility), Husserlian (the constituting pole) — the *Origin*'s teleological Logos rejoining, by way of Kant, the *bonum* beyond categories.

The fragments that *name God* are "marked by the same apparent ambiguity." God is no longer evoked merely, as in *Ideen* I, "as the exemplary model and *limit* of all consciousness of impossibility in the test of an essential truth" — the truth being first what *God himself could not put in question* (the eidetic necessities binding even an infinite intellect, §§44, 79). Nor is God designated as the *transcendent principle* — hence "also '*reduced*' in *Ideen* I" (§58) — of every factual universal teleology, that of nature or of spirit, that is, of history. The divine consciousness, "revealer of the intangibility of constituted essences," is — as content of a fiction and as the ordering *Telos* of the real universe — "*a facticity*." "The *reduction of God as factual being and consciousness* liberates the signification of a *transcendental divinity*, such as it appears in the last writings." And the ambiguity announced "concerns precisely the *relation of the transcendental Absolute as divinity with the transcendental Absolute as historical subjectivity*." In its transcendental sense, God is "sometimes designated as the one toward whom '*I am en route*' and '*who speaks in us*,' sometimes as what '*is nothing other than the Pole*'" (K III). "Sometimes the Logos *expresses itself* through a transcendental history; sometimes it is only the *absolute polar authenticity* of transcendental historicity *itself*." (The footnote insists, with the manuscript: the Pole as "beyond" is always beyond *for the Self* of transcendental consciousness — "*it is its beyond*"; "the path that sets out from each of the I's is *its* path, but all these paths lead to the same pole, situated beyond the world and beyond man: God.") The theological ambiguity is thus the exact replica, at the highest level, of the genitive ambiguity of ¶247: does the Logos *speak itself through* history (objective genitive — God as source descending into time) or *is it* the pure telic authenticity *of* historicity (subjective genitive — God as the ideal pole of subjectivity's self-surpassing)? The two readings of the divine name repeat the two readings of the Absolute of intentional historicity — and Derrida is about to refuse the choice between them.

Derrida formalizes the alternative as two readings of the whole of phenomenology. "In the *first* case" — the Logos expressing itself through transcendental history — "transcendental phenomenology would be only the most rigorous *language* of a *speculative metaphysics* and an *absolute idealism*": Husserl would be a Hegelian malgré lui, the concepts of God and Logos bearing their full speculative weight. "In the *second* case" — God as polar telos of historicity — "the concepts borrowed from metaphysics would have only a *metaphorical and indicative* sense, which would not essentially affect the original *purity* of phenomenology as *transcendental idealism*": the theological vocabulary would be a manner of speaking, leaving intact the finite, constituting subjectivity. The two readings are then restated in terms of *infinity*: in the first case, "the essential and *actual plenitude* of an infinity would deploy itself only in a historical discursivity where it would *let itself be derived*" (infinity given, history its unfolding — the descent-model of ¶246); in the second, "infinity would be only the *indefinite opening* to truth and phenomenality for a subjectivity *always finite in its factual being*" (infinity as task, never possessed — the finitist principle of ¶237).

And Derrida refuses the dilemma in the name of the phenomenological attitude itself. "One could not be more *unfaithful* to Husserl than by seeing here a *dilemma*. That would be to fail, surely, into a *speculative attitude* in the pejorative sense Husserl always assigned it." The phenomenological attitude "is first a *disponibility of attention for the future of a truth that, always already, announces itself*." Instead of "frantically seeking the *option*," one must "strive toward the *necessarily one root* of every dilemma." The questions are then re-posed not as exclusive alternatives but as two faces of one structure: "Does the sense of transcendental historicity make itself *heard through* it, like the Logos that is *in the beginning*?" — or "Is God, on the contrary, only the *final accomplishment*, situated at infinity, the name of the *horizon of horizons*, the *Entelechy* of transcendental historicity itself?" (footnote: "*God is the Entelechy*," F I, 24). "*Both at once, on the basis of a still more profound unity* — such is perhaps the only possible response to the question of historicity." The reconciliation is then stated with great precision: "It is *through constituted history* that God speaks and passes; it is *with regard to* constituted history and to all the constituted moments of transcendental life that God is *beyond*. But he is only the *Pole for-itself* of *constituting* transcendental historicity and historical subjectivity. The *dia-historicity* or the *meta-historicity* of the divine Logos *traverses and surpasses* only the '*Fact*' as the '*all-made*' (*tout-fait*) of history" — only the sedimented, constituted deposit — "but it is *nothing but the pure movement of its historicity*." God is beyond the *constituted*, immanent to the *constituting*: transcendent to every deposited result, identical with the living movement of depositing. The objective and subjective genitives of ¶247 are thus held together — God both speaks *through* history and *is* its pure telic movement — under the "more profound unity" the phenomenological attitude seeks rather than chooses.

This situation of the Logos, Derrida notes, "is profoundly analogous — and it is no accident — to that of *all ideality*, such as the analysis of *language* permitted us to specify" (sections VI–VII). Ideality "is at once *supra-temporal* and *omni-temporal*," and Husserl qualifies it "sometimes one way, sometimes the other, according as he relates it or not to factual temporality." Whence one can say that pure sense, "the *ideality of ideality*, which is nothing other than *the appearing of being*, is at once *supra-temporal* (*in-temporal*, Husserl also sometimes says) and *omni-temporal*" — or again that "'*supra-temporality means omni-temporality*,' the latter being itself only '*a mode of temporality*'" (*Erfahrung und Urteil* §64c, the text of ¶51). And the rhetorical questions push the analogy to its root: "Are not supra-temporality and omni-temporality *also the characters of Time itself*? — of the *Living Present*, which is the absolute concrete *Form* of phenomenological temporality and the primordial Absolute of all transcendental life?" (footnote: Husserl's own "*die urzeitliche, überzeitliche Zeitlichkeit*" — the primordial, supra-temporal "temporality" — speaking of "*My Living Present*," C-manuscripts, 1932). The hidden temporal unity "of the '*dia-*,' '*supra-*' or '*in-*' temporality on the one hand, and of *omni-temporality* on the other, is *the unitary foundation of all the instances dissociated by the diverse reductions*: facticity and essentiality, worldliness and non-worldliness, reality and ideality, *empirie* and transcendentality." This unity — "as *temporal* unity of temporality, for every *Geschehen*, for all *history* as the *gathering of what comes to pass in general*" — "*is historicity itself*." The convergence is complete: the structure found in the ideal object (supra-/omni-temporal, §64c), in God-as-Logos (dia-/meta-historical yet immanent to the movement), and in the Living Present (primordial yet supra-temporal temporality) is one and the same structure; and that structure, as the temporal unity underlying every dualism the reductions had separated, *is* historicity — the root of all dilemmas (¶257) named at last.

From this the Introduction's culminating thesis is drawn. "*If there is a history*, historicity can be only the *passage of a Speech* (*Parole*), the pure tradition of an originary *Logos* toward a polar *Telos*." But — and here the three "since" clauses tighten into the conclusion — "since there can be *nothing outside* the pure historicity of this passage; since there is *no Being that has a sense outside this historicity* and escapes its infinite horizon; since the Logos and the Telos are *nothing outside the Wechselspiel* (the *interplay*) of their reciprocal inspiration — this signifies that *the Absolute is the Passage* (*le Passage*)." Not a term (Logos at the source, Telos at the end) but the *passage* between them, the traditionalization that "circulates from one to the other, illuminating one by the other, in a movement where consciousness *invents its path* in an *indefinite and always-already-begun reduction*" — a movement "where every adventure is a *conversion*, and every return to the origin an *audacity toward the horizon*." The phrase "the Absolute is the Passage" is the Introduction's speculative summit: the absolute is neither arché nor telos but the *between*, the living traditionality that ¶136 located in writing now raised to the status of the absolute itself; and the identity of "return to the origin" with "audacity toward the horizon" reprises, as the form of the absolute, the identity of origin and telos established since But the Passage, being the only Absolute, "is *also the Absolute of a Danger*." For "if the *light of sense* is only *through the Passage*, it is that it can *also lose itself en route*." As speech, "it can lose itself only in the *inauthenticity* of a language and the *abdication* of a speaking being" — the crisis (¶150), now grounded in the very structure of the absolute-as-passage: what must travel can stray. Hence phenomenology "as *Method* of Discourse is first, in this regard, *Selbstbesinnung* and *Verantwortung*" — self-reflection and responsibility — "free resolution in which one '*takes up one's sense again*' (*reprend son sens*) in order to *render oneself accountable, through speech, for a path in peril*." And the temporality of this responsibility is essentially *responsive*: "this speech is *historical because it is always already a response*. To make oneself responsible is to *take charge of a heard speech* (*une parole entendue*), to take upon oneself the *exchange of sense*, in order to *watch over its passage*." (Footnote: from the *Idea of Phenomenology* on, Husserl's whole itinerary confirms the essence of phenomenology — in its fundamental discovery, the transcendental reduction — as the essence of a *Method*, in the richest and "perhaps most enigmatic" sense of the word; Husserl calls the reduction "*the Archi-Method of all philosophical methods*," C 2.) Therefore "in its most radical implications, *Method* is not the neutral preface or preambular exercise of a thought, *but thought itself in the consciousness of its integral historicity*." Method — the reduction — ceases to be propaedeutic and becomes the thing itself: responsible thought as the guarding of a sense in passage, answerability to a Logos always already heard. The audition-motif of ¶243 (the Logos heard, not seen) is here made ethical: responsibility is response to a *heard* word.

"All this develops rigorously the discovery of *intentionality*." Intentionality "is also only the *Absolute of a living Movement* without which neither its end nor its origin would have a chance to appear." Whence the series of identifications that gather the whole Introduction: "*Intentionality is traditionality*." In its greatest depth — "in the pure movement of phenomenological temporalization, as the *going-out-from-self-into-self* of the Absolute of the Living Present — intentionality is the *root of historicity*." And the consequence for the governing question: "If it is so, one does *not* have to ask *what* is the sense of historicity. In all the acceptations of this word, *historicity is sense*." The thesis of ¶82 ("truth is the deepest history") and of ¶245 ("eternity is nothing but historicity") reaches its final form: historicity is not a region of sense to be interrogated for its meaning; it *is* sense, the very movement of intending by which anything means at all. Intentionality (the constituting relation), traditionality (the transmission of sense), temporalization (the self-othering of the Living Present), and historicity are four names for one absolute movement — the Passage of The closing movement of the section guards this thesis against the misreading it most invites — confusion with speculative metaphysics — and situates phenomenology with respect to "the question of Being or of History." "Provided one respects its *phenomenological value*, such an assertion" — historicity is sense — "does *not transgress sense itself*, that is, the *appearing* and the *possibility of appearing* of history. It therefore does not *mix* transcendental idealism and speculative metaphysics, but it marks the moment where phenomenology can *articulate itself without confusion* with a '*philosophy*' posing the *question of Being or of History*." This "*ontological*" question — "in the non-Husserlian sense of the term, the only one that can be opposed, and that is often opposed today, to Husserl's phenomenological ontology" (Heidegger's *Seinsfrage*) — "cannot belong to a phenomenology as such." But — and Derrida marks his distance from the Heideggerian claim of priority just as firmly — "we do not believe either that it can ever, *in a philosophical discourse*, *simply precede* transcendental phenomenology as its *presupposition* or its *latent foundation*." It would mark, on the contrary, "*within philosophy in general*, the moment — *conceived moreover by Husserl* — where phenomenology would *complete itself* (*s'achèverait*) as *philosophical propaedeutic* to every philosophical decision." And since this propaedeutic "*always announces itself as infinite*, this moment is *not a facticity but an ideal sense, a right that will always remain under phenomenological jurisdiction* — a right that only a phenomenology can exercise, in *explicitly anticipating the end of its itinerary*." The placement of the question of Being is thus exactly calibrated against both Husserl and Heidegger: it cannot be answered *within* phenomenology (against a naïve Husserlianism), yet it cannot *precede* phenomenology as a fundamental ontology underlying it (against Heidegger) — it is reached only *at the infinitely deferred end* of the phenomenological road, as the task to which phenomenology is the inexhaustible propaedeutic. Phenomenology does not rest on fundamental ontology; it opens onto the ontological question as its own unattainable horizon.

The penultimate articulation makes the relation operational, distinguishing two questions whose order is irreversible. "This propaedeutic must be *completed in right*, or its factual term *anticipated*, so that one can pass from the question '*how*' to the question '*why*,' *knowing what one is speaking of*. It is in this that every philosophical discourse must *authorize itself by a phenomenology*." One must "*exhaust in right the question of the sense of historicity and of historicity as sense*" — that is, of the *possibility of historical facticity in its appearing* — "in order to be able to *charge with sense* the question: *Is there, and why is there, historical facticity?*" The two questions "are *irreducibly solidary*." The "*why*" — the question of the *existence* of facticity, of why there is history at all rather than not — "can arise only from the *possibility* (in the *metaphysical or ontological*, *not phenomenological*, sense) of a *non-being of facticity*." The architecture is exact: phenomenology answers the *how* (the sense, the structure, the appearing of historicity); only when that is exhausted does the *why* (the existence, the sheer that-there-is of historical fact) become a *meaningful* question — and that question is metaphysical/ontological, beyond phenomenology, yet reachable only through it. The order is the whole point: one cannot ask why there is history before knowing what history's sense is, on pain of speaking without knowing whereof — and phenomenology alone secures that knowing.

The reciprocity of the two questions is then deepened, and the priority of the *téléologique* established. "And *non-being as non-history* lets its *eventuality* be unveiled only on the basis of a consciousness *of pure sense and pure historicity* — that is, a consciousness of *possibility* in the *phenomenological* sense." This consciousness, "which only a phenomenology can bring to light, can be — we have sufficiently seen it — only a *teleological* consciousness." Why? "It is *because* the sense to which we have access is *not the being of the event* — because it can always *not be incarnated*, *be extinguished*, or *not be born* — that the '*why*' draws its gravity from a *phenomenological certainty*, and through it alone recovers the virulence of an '*in view of what?*' (*en vue de quoi?*)." The "why there is history" becomes serious only against the phenomenologically secured possibility that there might *not* be — and that possibility is given only by the consciousness of sense as something distinct from, and not guaranteed by, the being of the event (the lesson of ¶245's "exposure" and ¶260's "Danger"). Hence: "The *ontological* question seems therefore able to arise only *from a teleological affirmation*. That is, from a *freedom*." And the great formula that names the unity these pages have sought: "*Teleology is the threatened unity of sense and being, of phenomenology and ontology.*" This teleology, "which has never ceased to found and animate Husserlian thought, cannot however be *determined in a philosophical language* without *provisionally breaking* this unity to the profit of a phenomenology." The structure is now complete and self-reflexive: sense and being are united in teleology (the having-to-be of ¶252), but to *speak* this unity philosophically one must, provisionally, privilege the side of *sense* — phenomenology — because only the phenomenological consciousness of pure possibility opens the space in which the ontological "why" can be meaningfully posed. Phenomenology's priority is thus *juridical and provisional*, not absolute: it is the necessary breaking of an original unity, in order to give that unity philosophical voice.

The Introduction's exemplarity comes full circle. "Thus, knowing — *from the evential exemplarity* — what the *sense of the event* is, and — *from exemplarity in general* — what the *sense of sense in general* is, we can then ask, in a question that can no longer *proceed from phenomenology as such*, not '*what is the Fact?*' — a question to which a phenomenological ontology answers in principle — but: '*Why are a factual point of departure in facticity, and a reduction, possible in general?*'; or: '*What is the facticity of the fact that exemplarity of the fact supposes?*'; or again: '*What is the originary unity of sense and fact, of which, by themselves, neither the one nor the other can give an account?*'" In other words: "knowing what *sense as historicity* is, I can ask *in clarity why there would be a history rather than nothing*." (The footnote: such a question can be repeated about every singular facticity and every particular form of infinite historicity as horizon of every phenomenon — every determinate form of the world as horizon of every possible experience, "singularly *this* historical world here.") The whole apparatus of exemplarity — geometry as example of ideal objectity (¶3), Europe as example of historicity (¶193), the fact as example of essence (¶189) — converges on the ultimate exemplary question: the Leibnizian "why is there something rather than nothing" transposed into the register of history (*why a history rather than nothing*), now posable "in clarity" precisely because phenomenology has exhausted the *sense* of historicity and thereby earned the right to the question of its *being*.

And here the most striking reversal of the Introduction: the "return to empiricism" that the whole work has guarded against is *re-described as the completion of philosophy* — under one condition. "*On condition that it succeed the possibility of phenomenology and assume its juridical priority*, this *taking-seriously of pure facticity as such* is no longer a return to empiricism or non-philosophy. It accomplishes, on the contrary, *philosophy*." But for this it must "hold itself in the *precarious opening of a question*: the question of the *origin of Being as History*." Every *answer* to such a question "can only *make surface again* (*faire de nouveau surface*) in a phenomenological process. *Ontology has a right only to the question*." It is "in the *forever-open breach* of this question that *Being itself shows itself silently* under the *phenomenological negativity* of the ἕτερον (*heteron*)" — the *other*, the irreducibly other-than-sense that phenomenology can only register as the limit where eidetic subsumption fails. (The long footnote is decisive: it recalls Husserl's own formula gathering "the whole signification of his enterprise" — that for phenomenology, pure existential facticity, "*wild singularity*, always out of reach for every eidetic subsumption, is '*eternally the ἕτερον*'" (*Philosophy as Rigorous Science*). One passes from phenomenology to ontology — "in the non-Husserlian sense" — "when one *questions in silence toward the upsurge of naked facticity* and when one *ceases to consider the Fact in its phenomenological 'function.'*" Then the Fact "can no longer be *exhausted and reduced to sense* by a phenomenological labor, even pursued to infinity. It is *always more or always less, always other*, in any case, than what Husserl defines it as" when he writes — "in a formula that marks the highest ambition of his design" — that "*the Fact itself, with its irrationality, is a structural concept in the system of the concrete apriori*" (*Cartesian Meditations* §39, Husserl's emphasis). "But only a phenomenology can *denude the pure materiality of the Fact* by going to the term of eidetic determination, by exhausting itself. Only it can avoid confusing pure facticity with this or that of its determinations." And the footnote's closing turn — among the most quietly remarkable sentences in the book — having earned the right to "regret that Husserl did not also pose this ontological question, of which there is nothing to say that concerns it itself," asks: "*But how regret that phenomenology is not an ontology?*") The dialectic is complete: the brute fact, the wild *heteron*, is reached not by abandoning phenomenology for empiricism but by *exhausting* phenomenology to the point where it itself exposes, as its own limit, the facticity it cannot dissolve — and the question of *why that fact is* is handed over, silent and open, to an "ontology" that has a right only to the question, never to an answer that would not at once resurface as phenomenological sense.

The final two paragraphs of the body introduce, for the first time and as the Introduction's last word, the motifs of *delay* (*retard*) and *Difference* — the terms in which the young Derrida's own thought announces itself out of the reading of Husserl. "No doubt Being must *always already have given itself to be thought*, in the *presumption* — which is also a *resumption* — *of Method*; no doubt an *access to Being* and a *coming of Being* must *always already have contracted themselves* when phenomenology begins *as right to speech*." (The reduction does not create the relation to Being; it presupposes that Being has always already offered itself — Heidegger's *es gibt* acknowledged.) "And if Being were *not* History through and through, the *delay* (*retard*) of *Discourse* upon the *monstration of Being* would be only a simple and *faulty poverty* of thought as phenomenology" — a mere defect, a belatedness to be overcome. "*That it cannot be so* — because *historicity is prescribed to Being*; that the *delay* is the *destiny of Thought itself* as Discourse — *only a phenomenology can say* and *make surface in a philosophy*." For "only it can make appear *infinite historicity* — that is, *infinite discourse and dialecticity* — as the *pure possibility and the very essence of Being in manifestation*." Only phenomenology can "open *Being-History* to the *absolute subjectivity of Sense*, by making appear, *at the term of the most radical reduction*, *absolute transcendental subjectivity as pure passive-active temporality*, as the *pure auto-temporalization of the Living Present* — that is, *already*, as we have seen, *as intersubjectivity*." This intersubjectivity is "the *discursive and dialectical intersubjectivity of Time with itself*, in the infinite multiplicity and infinite implication of its *absolute origins*, giving right to every other intersubjectivity in general and making *irreducible* the *polemical unity of appearing and disappearing*." (The whole doctrine of ¶134 — the Living Present as already intersubjective, the circulation of originary absolutes — gathered here as the structure of Being-as-History.) Then the philosophical thesis on *delay*: "*The delay is here the philosophical absolute*, because the beginning of methodical reflection can be only the *consciousness of the implication of another absolute origin, anterior and possible in general*." Reflection always *comes after* an origin it did not produce and can never coincide with; this belatedness is not a failure but *the* absolute, the very condition of there being reflection at all. "This *alterity of the absolute origin* appearing *structurally in my Living Present*, and being able to appear and be recognized *only in the originarity of something like my Living Present* — this signifies *the authenticity of the phenomenological delay and limitation*." The reduction's apparent regionalism (¶237) is now vindicated as *authentic delay*: that the other origin can be reached only through *my* Living Present is not a defect of finitude but the structure of origin itself. "Under the *grey appearance of a technique*, the *Reduction* is nothing but the *pure thought of this delay* — pure thought taking consciousness of itself *as delay* in a philosophy." And the closing chiasmus, in the interrogative: "Could there be an *authentic thought of Being as History*, and an *authentic historicity of thought*, if the consciousness of delay could be *reduced*? But could there be a *philosophy* at all if this consciousness of delay were not *originary and pure*?" The delay can be neither eliminated (or there would be no historicity of thought) nor merely suffered (or there would be no philosophy): it must be *originary* — and therefore, the paragraph's last move, it has the *form of anticipation*: "an *originary consciousness of delay* can have only the *pure form of anticipation*. The pure consciousness of delay can be at the same time only a *pure and legitimate presumption — hence aprioric* — without which, here again, *discourse and history would not be possible*." Delay and anticipation coincide: to be originarily late before the absolute origin is the same as to be turned, in pure aprioric presumption, toward the telos — the *Rückfrage* (questioning-back) is identically a questioning-forward.

The penultimate paragraph names the source of delay: *Difference*. "*The impossibility of reposing in the simple maintenance* (*maintenance*) *of a Living Present* — *one and absolutely absolute origin of the Fact and the Right, of Being and Sense, but always other in its identity-to-itself*; the *impotence to enclose oneself in the innocent indivision of the originary Absolute, because it is Present only in deferring itself without respite* (*en se différant sans relâche*) — *this impotence and this impossibility give themselves in an originary and pure consciousness of Difference*." The Living Present, the bedrock of every analysis since ¶78, is here disclosed as *non-self-identical at its core*: it is "present" only by *differing from itself* — retention and protention (¶235), the going-out-from-self-into-self (¶261), now named as *différance* in all but the later spelling. The absolute origin is never an innocent self-coincidence; it is constituted by an irreducible *Difference* from itself, and *that* is why reflection is structurally delayed (one cannot coincide with what does not coincide with itself) and why the Living Present "is present only in deferring itself." "Such a *consciousness*, with the strange style of its unity, *must be able to be rendered to its light*. *Without it, without its proper dehiscence* (*déhiscence* — the bursting-open of a seed-pod), *nothing would appear*." Difference is not a privation of presence but the opening — the dehiscence — through which presence and phenomenality are first possible: the non-coincidence at the origin is the condition of all appearing, as the night-source of light (¶236) and the invisible Diaphane (¶238) were the conditions of seeing.

The Introduction ends by binding *Difference* to the *transcendental* itself — and the transcendental to the structure of the *Rückfrage*. "*The originary Difference of the absolute Origin*, which can and must indefinitely, with an *aprioric security*, *retain and announce its pure concrete form* — as the *beyond* or the *short-of* (*l'au-delà ou l'en-deçà*) *giving sense to every empirical genius and every factual profusion* — this is perhaps *what has always been said under the concept of 'transcendental,' through the enigmatic history of its displacements*." (The whole history of the word — scholastic, Kantian, Husserlian, traced through ¶¶197, 254 — gathered now under a single sense: the transcendental names the originary Difference that gives sense to all factual plenitude while never appearing among it.) Then the three lapidary identifications, the last sentences of the body, each beginning "*Transcendentale serait*": "*Transcendental would be Difference.* *Transcendental would be the pure and interminable disquiet of thought striving to 'reduce' Difference by exceeding factual infinity toward the infinity of its sense and value — that is, by maintaining Difference.*" (To reduce Difference is to maintain it: the phenomenological labor does not abolish the originary non-coincidence but holds it open, exceeding the factual toward sense without ever closing the gap.) "*Transcendental would be the pure certainty of a Thought which, being able to wait toward the Telos that already announces itself only by advancing upon the Origin that indefinitely reserves itself, has never had to learn that it would always be to come* (*toujours à venir*)." Thought is suspended between a Telos it cannot reach and an Origin it cannot recover — moving toward the end only by going back upon a beginning that perpetually withdraws — and its certainty is precisely this: that it is *always to come*, never present to itself, structurally in the future-anterior. The transcendental is this disquiet, this *à-venir* (a word whose later fortune, as *avenir* and as the "to-come," is here in germ).

**Closing note.** The Introduction's last sentences name its own object one final time: "*This strange procession of a 'Rückfrage' — such is the movement sketched in The Origin of Geometry. It is in this too that this writing holds, as Husserl says, 'an exemplary signification.'* — *July 1961\.*" The ending performs what it describes. The *Rückfrage*, the questioning-back-toward-the-origin, has been shown to be a *procession* — a going-forward — because the origin reveals itself only at the telic pole (¶226), only as *à venir*; and Husserl's text, which seemed to ask only how geometry began, has been read as the exemplary staging of the deepest structure of thought — delay, difference, the transcendental as the to-come. The word *exemplary*, with which Derrida closes, is the word with which he opened (¶3): the *Origin of Geometry* is exemplary not because geometry is a privileged content but because, in asking after one origin, it discloses the form of *all* questioning-back toward *the* origin — and discloses that origin as Difference, as delay, as the always-to-come. The commentary thus ends where Derrida's own philosophy begins: the motifs of *différance*, the trace, the supplement, iterability, and the to-come, which the preceding pages have repeatedly seen germinating in footnotes and turns of phrase (the ideality of the signifier-form at ¶144, the sign at the origin of presence at ¶150 and ¶242, *survie* at ¶145, the *clôture* at ¶177 and ¶224, writing as the highest possibility of constitution at ¶142), are here gathered, named, and set to work — no longer as readings of Husserl but as the opening of a thought that will take the *delay* of discourse upon Being as its permanent point of departure.

---

*Commentary complete: the Introduction has been covered paragraph by paragraph from the opening through the close of section XI (July 1961), comprising the untitled opening (¶¶1–5) and sections I–XI (¶¶6–269). Derrida's substantive footnotes have been incorporated inline at the paragraphs they annotate; his translation decisions (Rückfrage, Stiftung/Endstiftung, Leib/Körper, Geistigkeit, Vergegenwärtigung, Selbstbesinnung, Verantwortung, relever for aufheben, clôture, frappe, Bodenkörper, Wechselspiel, the genitival "de," Difference, retard, à venir) have been tracked throughout; and the systematic anticipations of La voix et le phénomène and of the later thought of writing, the trace, iterability, the pharmakon, the supplement, the closure of metaphysics, and différance have been flagged at their points of emergence.*
